“I believe Tavra can read a bad score faster than most living cutters.”

“That was not my question.”

“I have not seen the stolen order.” Serad rested both hands on the parapet. “The Dominion Measure will end road losses that kill thousands across a generation. It will demand service from us. Your mother sees service as a trap because she once gave it under my direction and paid beyond what either of us understood.”

“You say that as though she left you too.”

“She did.”

Eda had never considered the shape of Tavra’s desertion in Serad’s life. He had mentored her, defended her after Nareth, and raised her daughter through College years. His grief did not erase hers. It made the old fracture wider.

“Will tomorrow’s hold harm Kesta?” she asked.

“The approach road will resist movement inside its salted boundary. Nothing beyond.”

“And survey custody?”

“Civil authority. Not my measure.”

A careful division. Serad did not lie, but he sometimes built truth from narrow rooms.

Eda opened Folio Seven at last. The first page showed the Kestan approach: three outer crossings, one center line, a release turn cut broad enough for local shift. She traced the diagram without touching it. The score looked safe at demonstration scale.

The stitched center pages pulled at the binding.

“Why me?” she asked.

Serad answered without ceremony. “Because you hear more than one anchor without forcing them into one voice. Because you recover quickly. Because your company trusts your count. I would choose you if Tavra Ilen had never entered the College.”

Not destiny. Not leverage. Skill.

“And if I refuse?”

“Another center performs tomorrow. The Dominion assignment remains open until Stillday.”

He gave her the possibility plainly. That made refusal harder, not less real.

Eda looked down at the Still Court. Seven anchor shrines waited in evening shadow. A continent’s roads failed in storms, split under carts, and carried children away from vanished villages. She had trained to do more than survive her mother’s mistakes.

“I accept the Kestan demonstration,” she said. “I keep Folio Seven. My release command outranks the arrest warrant inside the field.”

Serad inclined his head. “Agreed.”

She carried the folio to her room and set it beside her travel soles. Before packing, she opened to the Kestan score and copied every permitted line into memory. She did not cut the stitched cord.

Eda called the Fifth Measure captains to her room one at a time. She told each dancer the field limit, the expected road movement, and the order in which anyone could leave rehearsal without penalty. She did not mention Tavra until Captain Sorn asked whether the warrant changed their count.

“No,” Eda said. “We perform a road hold, not an arrest.”

“If she breaks the salt?”

“Then we release before anyone pursues.”

Sorn studied her face. “She is your mother.”

“She is outside my company.”

The answer held until he left. Eda sat beside the travel soles and admitted the part she had not said: Tavra had remained outside every company Eda built because absence could still occupy a place.

At dawn, Eda would lead the Fifth Measure east. If Tavra stepped into her field, Eda would hear what she had come to say.

Listening did not mean leaving with her.

Chapter 6 — Three Steps for a Hill

Tavra reached the demonstration ridge while six Kestan children argued over whose turn it was to move the hill.

The hill measured perhaps thirty paces from toe to crest, no higher than a two-story house. Overnight it had crossed the path between the western hamlet and the imperial field. The children stood beside its lower edge with baskets of plum grafts, impatient rather than afraid.

A girl in a green coat scraped three short lines in the dirt with her heel.

“You step here, here, and here,” she told a smaller boy. “Then wait.”

“Last time it was two.”

“Last time the hill was sleepy.”

“Hills don’t sleep.”

“Then why did it take all morning to get up?”

Ruhan arrived behind Tavra carrying his route book. “They’ll settle the argument before the College settles the road.”

Tavra examined the children’s feet. Plain wrapped soles, no cut scores. Each line marked a familiar greeting to local ground: weight on the outside heel, flat step across the slope, toes lifted as the foot withdrew. The movement carried no forced direction. It asked whether the surface would accept passage.

The green-coated girl took the three steps.

After the third, she waited.

A seam opened along the hill’s base. Loose stones rolled inward. The path rose gently around the eastern toe, forming a shallow ledge wide enough for one person and a basket.

The children cheered and crossed single file.

“They moved a hill,” Ossa said.

“They found where it had nearly moved itself,” Ruhan replied.

Tavra watched the ledge settle under the final child. No center took counter-motion because no score forced scale or permanence. The greeting changed one local possibility into use.

“Can every child do that?” she asked.

“Every Kestan child learns the three steps for a hill that already knows them,” Ruhan said. “Try them in Meren and you may greet a ditch.”

The distinction mattered. Tavra stored it beside the Nareth fragment in her thoughts: simple movement, local knowledge, no ownership beyond the crossing.

A drum sounded from the imperial field.

Not Ossa’s. This beat carried polished authority, four counts even enough to make the air seem squared.

Beyond the ridge, College crews had prepared a long salted rectangle toward Kesta. Step-salt shone black in the grass. White banners marked a public gallery on the south side. Anchor surveyors rolled a torso-sized stone onto the field in a bronze cradle.

At center stood Eda.

Distance changed nothing essential. Tavra recognized the way her daughter held her chin when listening through her feet. Eda wore the white-and-gray leathers of the Fifth Measure, hair bound high, travel soles strapped over narrow center shoes. Twenty supporting dancers waited behind her in two lines.

Serad Vale stood at the field’s northern edge with a score board under one arm.

Tavra’s left knee tightened. Her body remembered him before anger did.

Ruhan pointed toward a line of orchard carts gathered beyond the public gallery. “Twenty keepers stayed to witness. Others are moving east.”

“And Kel?”

“Left before dawn with the young trees. He chose the safer route.”

Tavra heard the correction and accepted it. “Good.”

Ossa adjusted the drum against her ribs. “We show the command-skin before the measure starts.”

“Serad will call it stolen and delay until the field closes.”

“Then orchard keepers hear the claim.”

“Eda needs to see the release mismatch.”

Ruhan said, “You want to walk into the salted field.”

“I can reach her before center count.”

“You can also make her choose between her company and you in front of a crowd.”

Tavra looked across the field. Eda checked each supporting dancer’s stance personally. She touched one woman’s ankle, spoke, and waited while the dancer adjusted her own buckle. Agency in small motions. Tavra felt the accusation of it.

A route officer announced the demonstration. He promised a stable approach for trade, grain, and medical travel. He did not mention survey custody or anchor orchards. Kestan spectators answered with silence.

The green-coated child from the hill pushed to the front carrying her empty graft basket.

Serad raised one hand. The drum stopped.

“Before the licensed measure,” he said, “we acknowledge Kesta’s local crossing.”

He had noticed the new ledge. Of course he had. Serad’s strength had always included seeing useful things before he explained why they belonged inside his work.

He invited the three children nearest the field to repeat their greeting outside the salt. The girl stepped heel, flat, withdraw. Two others followed. The hill path widened another fraction.

Polite applause came from College crews.

Then surveyors poured step-salt over the local ledge.

Black mineral line covered the children’s heel marks and continued straight toward the orchard carts.

Tavra started forward.

Ruhan caught only the end of her white wrist cloth, not her arm. “Do you enter with our evidence or your anger?”

“Both.”

“Which decides your feet?”

She pulled the cloth free. “I’ll know at the boundary.”

“Then you do not know enough.”

Ossa stepped between them. “We go to the gallery. We speak where people can leave.”

Tavra wanted to refuse. Eda moved into center. The anchor stone touched prepared earth. Every old scar in Tavra’s knees felt the field close.

She followed Ossa to the gallery because she had promised disclosure, not because fear had lessened.

From the public side, Tavra could inspect the whole preparation. Surveyors had placed the Kestan anchor stone in a cradle with four locking feet, a device suited to civic plazas that had agreed where their edges lay. Here, the northeastern foot already hung above a shallow depression. An apprentice kept packing dirt beneath it while the ground eased away.

The Fifth Measure’s soles showed better judgment. Each supporting dancer wore a broad bark-leather layer that could flex if the road turned. Eda’s center shoes carried narrow heel cuts and two open release channels. Tavra recognized Serad’s newest hand in the geometry: less brutal than the old Nareth squares, more willing to let a local bend remain.

That improvement complicated hatred. Serad had learned from disaster. He had not learned to surrender authorship.

Ruhan followed her gaze. “Can the score work?”

“For the road they measured yesterday.”

“And today?”

“The hill path changed this morning. The anchor cradle stands unevenly. Eda will feel both before the outer line does.”

“Tell her now.”

Tavra looked toward the white cord at the gallery entrance. “They’ll arrest me before I reach center.”

“Then tell a captain.”

“She may not trust a message.”

Ruhan’s expression went dry. “You have spent twelve years making that likely.”

Ossa intervened before Tavra answered. She called Captain Sorn by his field title and sent the uneven-cradle warning through the College officer. Sorn checked the northeast foot at once and ordered a stone shim instead of dirt.

The correction came without Tavra entering the field. It also came without Eda knowing its source. Tavra should have counted that as success. The part of her that wanted recognition from her daughter called it absence.

Ruhan addressed the orchard keepers first. He described the original command strip without asking them to trust Ossa’s copies. Tavra held up the branded hide and named the cuts: seven anchors, continuous hold, supply through next spring, no release allotment. Murmurs spread along the gallery.

A College officer approached. “That material belongs to the Imperial College.”

“Then confirm its meaning,” Tavra said.

“Return it for examination.”

“Publicly.”

His hand moved toward the strip. Ossa shifted her drum between them without striking it.

On the field, Serad gave first count.

Eda’s company stepped.

The local hill path flattened as the licensed score took hold. Stone moved beneath the salt in one smooth sheet. The approach road extended toward the orchards, wide enough for two wagons, its edges rising to drain rain. Spectators gasped. The craft deserved it. Twenty dancers crossed in exact counterweight, each supporting a small part of the road’s turn.

Eda stood at center and heard everything.

Tavra knew the moment her daughter found the mismatch. Eda’s left hand opened. Her right hip shifted back. The score expected the approach to settle on old route ground, but Kesta’s local ledge had moved since the diagram. The road sought a new line under the orchard slope.

Serad called correction.

Eda changed her stance and caught the altered pull.

The approach straightened.

It overwrote the three-step path completely.

At the field edge, the green-coated girl stared at the black salt where her greeting marks had been. “It didn’t ask.”

No one answered her.

The imperial road lengthened another twelve paces. Counter-motion crossed the supporting line and gathered toward Eda. Her company received the ordinary lift in ankles and calves. Eda took the local hill’s refusal through her right hip.

Tavra saw the joint begin to lock.

“Release now,” she said.

The College officer blocked her. “Remain outside.”

Eda called, “Fifth line, widen.”

Her dancers widened. The pressure should have dispersed. Instead the salted boundary forced the new road to maintain a straight edge while the hill continued leaning north. The support line carried direction; Eda alone carried rigidity.

Serad read the danger. He turned his score board and called a shortened release.

Tavra knew that turn. It closed the road before lifting center weight. On stable ground it protected the supporting line. Here it would drive the hill’s remaining torque into Eda’s hip for three counts.

Three counts could mineralize a joint.

Tavra crossed the salt.

The boundary snapped cold around her left boot. Her old center training took the field’s shape at once. She ran badly, stick digging holes in the new road, while officers shouted behind her.

Eda saw her.

Shock broke her stance more dangerously than the score.

“Tavra, get out!”

“Do not close the heel.”

“I know my release.”

“Not on this ground.”

Serad’s voice cut across them. “Tavra, clear the field. Eda, hold center.”

Eda obeyed the second command because twenty dancers depended on it. Tavra reached the supporting line and dropped to one knee beside Captain Sorn.

“Lift your outer heel on six,” she said.

Sorn did not move. “You have no field authority.”

“Then ask Eda.”

Tavra shouted the needed change to her daughter. “The hill greets on three. Release through the local ledge, not over it.”

Eda’s face held fury and pain. She listened through her right foot.

“The ledge is gone.”

“The possibility remains under the salt.”

Serad called second release position. One count remained before the heel closed.

Eda made her choice. “Sorn, outer heel on six. Fifth line, follow my missing two.”

The company moved with her.

Tavra cut no sole. She took no center. Eda turned the measure herself, borrowing the omitted count from her College rehearsal and the three-step greeting from the hill. The new road buckled once at its center. Salt sprayed. Pressure left Eda’s hip and traveled into a shallow curve along the approach.

The road released crooked but safe.

Eda sank onto one hand.

Tavra reached her before anyone else. She caught Eda under the arm and felt the instant her daughter resisted.

“Don’t lift me.”

“You’re hurt.”

“Take your hand off me.”

Tavra obeyed too late to make the first touch consensual.

Captain Sorn and another dancer helped Eda stand at her own nod. The company gathered around her. Tavra remained inside their circle like grit under a sole.

Serad entered the broken field without hurry. Age had silvered his hair, not softened the exactness of his gaze.

“You crossed a live boundary,” he said to Tavra.

“Your release would have put the hill into her hip.”

“I saw the load.”

“After you called the close.”

“And I would have changed it.”

“On which count?”

“Enough.” Eda’s voice shook. She stood with weight on her left leg. “You both discussed my body while I was standing here.”

The public gallery had gone silent. Every Kestan keeper, College officer, child, and rider watched the Ilen family turn a field emergency into spectacle.

Tavra should have stepped back. Instead she knelt at Eda’s right boot and reached for the buckle.

“I need to see whether the sole closed.”

Eda kicked her hand away.

Not hard. Publicly.

“I led the release,” Eda said. “You did not save me. You gave me information, then entered my company without leave.”

“The score was wrong.”

“I changed it.”

“Because I told you how.”

The sentence left Tavra before judgment reached it.

Pain changed Eda’s face less than contempt did.

Around them, the Fifth Measure dancers heard their center’s achievement claimed by the fugitive who had disrupted their line. Tavra saw the humiliation she had made and still wanted to argue that Eda remained alive.

Her control flaw never arrived wearing cruelty. It arrived carrying correct information at the worst possible time and demanding gratitude for urgency.

Ossa stood outside the salt, not rescuing her from the consequence.

Eda bent and opened her own right boot. She examined the score, pressed her hip, and tested rotation. “No fixed line. Bruising and concentrated strain. I can walk.”

Tavra said, “You should not perform tomorrow.”

“This performance ended.”

“Dominion.”

“Stillday remains nineteen days away. I decide what I can carry.”

“You cannot carry seven anchors.”

“Neither can you.”

That answer found the stone in Tavra’s knee.

Serad sent healers to Eda only after she accepted them. Then he faced the gallery and acknowledged the altered ground. “The approach score failed to include Kesta’s morning movement. Center Ilen corrected the release. The road will remain curved until local agreement determines its use.”

He gave Eda credit by name. Tavra could not accuse him of stealing it.

Ruhan raised the command-skin strip. “And the continuity hold after Stillday?”

Serad looked at it across the field. “Stolen military instructions do not define the Dominion Measure.”

“Read them,” Ossa said.

“I will examine the original under witness.”

“Here.”

“Not in a damaged field with riders and an injured company.” His refusal sounded reasonable because he understood that reason could move a crowd farther than threat.

Tavra stood with effort. “The orders omit release supplies.”

“The Dominion convoys draw from central stores.”

“Where are the replacement center soles?”

“Being cut.”

Every answer fit the visible part of truth. Serad had always built with close joints.

He stepped toward her and lowered his voice. “Come back to the College. Surrender the stolen skin and Nareth fragment. I will ask Miren for full amnesty.”

“On what condition?”

“You stop turning fear of your own failure into resistance against every hold.”

“Eda’s body pays for your certainty.”

“Eda’s skill saved this field after both our errors. You might begin by admitting she has skill beyond your protection.”

Tavra glanced at her daughter. Eda sat on a field stool while a healer wrapped her hip. She directed her company’s inspection and refused to look toward Tavra.

Serad had found the truth most useful against her.

“I won’t return,” Tavra said.

“Then you leave me fewer peaceful options.”

“Peaceful for whom?”

“For everyone whose food crosses a road.”

He did not threaten her life or sneer at Kesta. He believed a stable route could justify the bodies required to make it. Tavra had believed the same at Nareth.

A survey captain approached Serad with a slate. He read it, then looked past the damaged field toward the orchard carts.

“The eastern line moved during the demonstration,” he said. “Our anchor access no longer matches the civil grant.”

Ruhan replied, “Your grant matched ink.”

Serad’s patience ended not in anger but decision. “Secure the local anchor stones until survey custody can be resolved. No tree damage. No household detention unless they obstruct the crews.”

Eda looked up. “You said the demonstration and survey were separate.”

“They were. The field change has made tomorrow’s approach unsafe.”

“So you seize the anchors today?”

“I preserve access.”

Kestan keepers began moving toward their carts.

Ossa put one hand on her drum rim. Ruhan folded his route book and scanned the slopes. Tavra saw the coming conflict arrange itself: riders near the north exit, survey crews beside the road, orchard families spread across moving shelves. Kel had left with the youngest trees, but fifty-one orchards remained in motion beyond the ridge and twenty waited here.

Eda stood despite the healer’s protest. “My company will not assist a seizure.”

Serad’s expression registered hurt before authority covered it. “Your company will recover. Survey riders handle custody.”

“You used my measure to reach them.”

“I used your measure to make a road.”

Eda looked at the overwritten three-step path. The green-coated girl had begun scraping the imperial salt away with her heel.

Tavra wanted to cross to Eda, apologize, explain, warn, and command all at once. She managed one step backward.

“I’m sorry I claimed your release,” she said.

Eda met her eyes for the first time since the field broke. “You’re sorry in the shape of another lesson.”

Riders moved toward the anchor orchards.

Ruhan called to the keepers, not to Tavra. “Take household routes. No common line.”

Ossa struck one warning beat, plain enough to alert and incomplete enough to command no trained response.

Serad raised his hand. White cords spread along the curved approach road Eda had saved.

Tavra tightened the strap around her left knee and drew her score knife. She had reached her daughter before Stillday. Eda had rejected rescue and demonstrated why.

Now the College had turned the road under their feet into a route for seizure.

Chapter 7 — When Kesta Left the Map

Ruhan cut the first imperial salt line with Aunt Seli’s soup ladle.

The iron bowl scraped through black grit at the edge of the approach road. Its handle bent under his grip. Behind him, orchard keepers swung their tree carts away from the riders advancing along Eda’s curved paving.

“Household routes!” Ruhan called. “Choose your own crossing. Meet beyond the bell ridge if the bell ridge remains.”

Twenty keepers broke in six directions. They did not flee as one body. Families knew their trees, carts, elders, water, and draft animals better than any pass-runner could. A common escape line would let the College block them all.

The riders understood that too. Their captain divided his white cords into pairs.

Ruhan scraped a second gap through the step-salt. The held road shuddered where its boundary opened. He had no training for a live measure, but Tavra had shouted that a broken line made the field uncertain. Uncertainty belonged to Kesta more than a straight imperial road did.

“Stop damaging the boundary!” a surveyor yelled.

“Seli wants her ladle back,” Ruhan said.

A horse reached him. He dropped flat beside the road as the rider leaned from the saddle. The paving rolled under the horse’s fixed soles. It stumbled, and Ruhan escaped between orchard carts before the rider recovered.

Ossa’s warning beat traveled across the field. One stroke, space, two quick taps. Alert without instruction. Kestan bells answered from the eastern shelves, each household ringing its own pattern.

Tavra knelt beside an overturned graft cart with three strips of raw leather across her thigh. “Ruhan, bring me anyone whose soles slip on the new road.”

“No one has time for fitting.”

“They have less time for falling.”

She cut a shallow outward hook into the first strip. Her left leg stuck straight behind her, useless for balance, but her hands moved without hesitation.

Ruhan pulled the green-coated girl away from a cart wheel and sent her toward Tavra. “Her wraps catch salt.”

“I can run,” the girl protested.

“Run after she cuts.”

Tavra looked up. “Do you agree to a release notch? It will turn your foot outward if the road grips. You may fall on the dirt. You keep your ankle on the stone.”

The girl considered the riders, then held out both feet. “Cut.”

Tavra bound the improvised release soles over her wraps. The girl tested once and sprinted toward an orchard bell.

Serad’s voice carried from the demonstration field. “Survey company, preserve the anchor line. Fifth Measure, withdraw to recovery formation.”

Eda stood with her company at the broken salt. One hand pressed her bruised right hip. She watched riders use the road her measure had raised.

Ruhan had no room to wonder what she would do.

A survey crew rolled three imperial anchor stones from behind the gallery. Smaller than the demonstration stone, each fitted into a locking cradle. They salted a wide fan toward the orchards. The field overlapped Kesta’s moving paths and the road edge Tavra had released.

“Pinning field,” Tavra said. “They’ll use the local trees to complete it.”

“How long?”

“Once a cradle touches an orchard anchor, three counts.”

Ruhan opened his route book. Yesterday’s shelves no longer mattered. He marked the active salt fan, the six household routes, and the deep fold under the bell ridge. The imperial crews needed a local anchor from affected land. Every Kestan orchard wrapped one in its roots.

“Move the trees without letting a cradle touch a root basket,” he called. “If you cannot move, strip grafts and leave the anchor.”

Orchard keeper Dasin stared at him. “Leave our ground?”

“Keep your people.”

“We have carried that stone through nine generations.”

“Then choose with all nine watching. I cannot choose for you.”

Dasin cut the tree cart’s rear rope and put his shoulder under the rail.

Ruhan went to the front.

The old plum tree weighed more than its branches suggested. Its root basket held damp soil, wrapped stone, family plaques, and the bones of two children buried during a winter movement. Four people could roll it on level ground. The approach road now tilted toward the imperial fan.

Ossa ran beside them. “I can give a lifting count.”

“Not command.”

“Call and answer. They choose the answer.”

Ruhan looked at Dasin.

The keeper nodded. “Give us something our shoulders can share.”

Ossa struck a low question on the outer drum skin. Dasin answered with his orchard bell. The carriers lifted on the bell, not the drum. Ossa asked again. Another household answered from farther east. Work rhythms crossed without becoming one command.

They rolled the plum cart uphill while riders closed from the road.

The first white cord reached for Dasin’s reins. The green-coated girl flashed past on Tavra’s release soles and cut his saddle strap with Ruhan’s bent ladle handle. Not effectively. The iron glanced off leather.

“Try work suited to the tool,” Ruhan told her.

She threw the ladle into a salt line instead. Better.

The boundary broke under the horse. Paving tilted. Rider and mount slid toward a shallow ditch. Neither fell, but their path closed behind the orchard cart.

At the bell ridge, the ground gave a long northward sigh. Ruhan felt movement through the carrying rail. Kesta’s eastern shelves had begun their noon departure hours early, pulled by the pinning field’s pressure.

The College had tried to hold the country and provoked it into walking.

“Down rails,” he called.

They set the plum cart on red grass. Ruhan listened. The ridge would split along the old fold. If the cart crossed too soon, its weight might open the seam beneath it. If they waited, riders would reach them.

Tavra limped up carrying finished leather strips between her teeth. “I can cut the cart team for the release.”

“The drivers, not the cart?”

“The ground takes bodies as centers if the wheels enter scored salt. These notches let them shed the torque at the first turn.”

She showed Dasin the cut and named the likely cost: bruised outer ankles, possible falls, no promise against a full anchor hold.

Dasin accepted for himself. His daughter refused and moved to the rear rope off the salted edge. Tavra fitted only him.

Ruhan noticed the effort it cost her not to argue with the daughter’s choice.

The ridge knocked from below.

“Now,” he said.

Dasin rang his bell. They hauled the plum cart across. Stone opened behind its rear wheels. The path carried them east in a slow slide.

From the western field came three synchronized drum strokes.

Imperial anchor count.

One of the smaller cradles had reached a Kestan pear tree still trapped near the gallery. Surveyors chained cradle to root basket. The tree’s keeper lay under a rider’s knee, hands spread in the salt.

“Three counts!” Tavra shouted.

The College drum struck first.

Ossa turned and answered with a broken beat. Trained survey dancers hesitated between counts. The pinning field flickered through Ruhan’s feet.

Second imperial stroke.

Ossa hit the outer drum skin near its patched edge. The hide gave a sharp complaint. She played across the expected landing, splitting the survey company’s response.

Tavra ran toward the captured pear tree.

Her knee made running a sequence of controlled falls. Ruhan swore and followed. They crossed the splitting ridge as the third imperial stroke began.

Ossa struck first.

The outer drum skin tore from rim to center.

Sound collapsed into a dry flap. The imperial count landed clean.

Salt darkened around the pear tree. Its root basket slammed against the ground. The nearest Kestan path stopped moving. Counter-motion passed through the survey centers; two dropped, clutching their ankles. The pear tree’s branches went rigid in the wind.

One local anchor had been pinned.

Not all of Kesta. Ruhan refused the imperial habit of turning one loss into a total story.

The trapped keeper shouted from under the rider, “Cut the basket loose!”

Tavra reached the tree and understood. “You’ll lose the anchor stone.”

“Cut it.”

She sliced the root bindings above the chained cradle. Ruhan used the bent ladle to pry a wet knot free. Soil spilled. Pale roots tore from the lower basket. The keeper made a sound as if the knife entered him.

The tree cart rolled free without its oldest root mass.

The imperial anchor held the severed basket and local stone inside the pinning field. Surveyors had captured a piece of Kesta. The family escaped with the living tree.

Ruhan pulled the keeper up. “Can you walk?”

“I can push.”

He pushed.

Ossa arrived carrying the torn drum. The outer skin hung in two pieces. Under it, the inner rim and command-marked hide showed through.

“I have no public voice,” she said.

“You have lungs.”

“People cannot hear lungs across three shelves.”

“Then give the drum away.”

She stared at him.

Ruhan took one loose strip of the torn outer skin and tied it to Dasin’s orchard bell. “Every household carries a piece. They answer each other.”

Ossa looked toward the scattered bells. Her grief over the instrument lasted one breath. Then she cut the remaining outer skin from the rim into broad strips and handed them to runners.

“Two taps means salt,” she told each. “One long means riders. No pattern means command. If you hear a command cadence, make any other noise.”

Children, elders, orchard keepers, and cart drivers carried the strips away. Soon rough hide taps crossed Kesta from different directions, too uneven for trained bodies to mistake as orders.

Ruhan climbed a wagon tongue to see across the separated shelves. The fifty-one moving anchor orchards formed no column. Their bells rose from gullies, house lanes, and paths that appeared only as wheels used them. That disorder had protected most of the trees from the pinning fan, but it also hid families in trouble.

A red cloth waved from the lower nut shelf. Two carts had met nose to nose where a path narrowed between rising stones. Behind them, Seli’s kitchen rolled downhill with no rail team on its front side.

Ruhan ran there with three carriers. “Nut cart west. Kitchen east.”

“The west path ended,” one keeper said.

“It ended for wheels. Unload the graft boxes and carry them over the shoulder. Leave the cart.”

The keeper put both hands on the carved wagon board. Her family had slept beneath it during migrations. “We built this when my eldest was born.”

“Then decide quickly with your eldest.”

The young man beside her looked from the advancing salt to the graft boxes. “We take the trees.”

They stripped the wagon in under a minute. No imperial rider forced the abandonment, and no Kestan leader made it noble. They left worked timber because living grafts weighed less.

Seli’s kitchen reached the narrowing. Ruhan and the carriers caught its front rail. Inside, clay jars broke against the traveling oven.

“Soup survived,” Seli called. “I cannot speak for the turnips.”

They swung the house onto the path cleared by the abandoned cart. Its rear foot crushed the wagon board. The keeper flinched and kept carrying grafts.

Loss accumulated without becoming defeat. Ruhan marked the abandoned cart in his head because somebody would need to account for it after the ground stopped demanding immediate choices.

The pinning crews reset their count.

Eda stepped onto the approach road.

Serad caught her arm, then released it when she looked at his hand. They spoke too far away for Ruhan to hear. Eda pointed toward the pear family, the cut basket, and the survey cradles. Serad answered with the flat hand of command.

The Fifth Measure formed behind her.

Tavra stopped cutting. “She’s going to hold the escape road.”

“Which escape?” Ruhan asked.

All Kesta’s household routes converged briefly at the bell ridge before spreading beyond imperial survey lines. If the College held that shelf, orchard carts could cross without the ground breaking under their weight. The same hold would create a perfect road for riders.

“Both,” Tavra said.

Eda raised her heel.

The Fifth Measure entered a small road hold, nothing like the full demonstration. Their white soles caught the moving shelf and steadied one narrow crossing. Orchard families recognized the opportunity. Carts rolled toward it.

Serad sent riders after them.

Eda could close the hold early and drop the road before pursuit. She could keep it long enough to obey the seizure order. Her company watched her for release.

Tavra moved toward the road.

Ruhan blocked her with the route book. “Do not enter her field again.”

“I can tell her when the last orchard clears.”

“So can every bell.”

“She may not hear them through the drum.”

“Then trust her to decide what she hears.”

Tavra’s face showed the violence of restraint. She remained outside.

Ruhan took position at the crossing and counted households, not steps. Dasin’s plum. The severed pear. Three nut carts. Seli’s kitchen, somehow present, soup pot strapped inside. Eleven goat pens. The families who had waited for direct proof now carried proof in torn roots and arrested ground.

Riders gained along the approach.

Eda held.

Her right hip shook. Captain Sorn shifted closer, offering support without taking center. She refused with one small motion.

“Last carts!” Ruhan called.

A wagon carrying two elderly orchard keepers jammed at the crossing. Its left wheel had split. The road began to roll beneath it.

Tavra pulled a release strip from her belt. “Ask the driver.”

Ruhan did.

The driver accepted bruising and a likely fall. His passenger refused any scored leather. Tavra cut only the driver’s right sole. At her instruction, he stepped down beside the broken wheel and turned his foot outward. The improvised score took the road’s torque, shifted it through his heel, and released it into a spray of loose gravel. He fell hard on his shoulder. The wagon lifted enough for six people to drag it clear.

“Cost?” Ruhan asked him.

“Shoulder. Ankle stings. Still mine.”

He climbed back onto the wagon using his left arm.

The last household bell crossed the ridge.

Eda gave release count.

Then she left one deliberate beat of slack.

Ruhan heard it because Kesta lived in such intervals. Her company began the close on four, but Eda’s center heel did not land until five. During that one open count, the crossing road shifted east beneath the final orchard carts and away from the riders.

College horses met a gap where paving had been.

Two stopped. One jumped and lost its rider on the far side. Eda completed the release before the land could take more.

From Serad’s position, the late heel might look like injury. Captain Sorn looked at Eda and knew otherwise. He said nothing.

Tavra saw too. “She helped.”

“She chose slack,” Ruhan said. “Do not turn it into a message meant for you.”

Tavra closed her mouth.

Serad ordered the survey crews to extend the pinning fan. Without Ossa’s outer cadence to fracture them, three companies advanced around their cradles. They had one captured Kestan anchor and a continuous salt line toward the bell ridge.

Ruhan opened his route book to the oldest page.

His grandmother had drawn Kesta’s crossings there forty years ago, before Ruhan learned that maps aged faster than people. The orchard routes formed a loose spiral around seven seasonal shelves. Imperial copies reduced that spiral to a bounded crescent. Every survey assumed Kesta would remain somewhere inside those historical turns.

The Deep Step had opened an eighth route during the morning’s pressure.

It ran east beyond the page.

Ruhan had felt it under Seli’s kitchen, then under the bell ridge. The path did not yet exist as a usable shelf. It might open if enough household weight followed. It might divide and drop carts into separate valleys. Kesta could retreat along known routes and fight for each orchard, or leave the geography by which the empire knew how to seize it.

He climbed the bell ridge and rang the common emergency bell once.

Every household stopped long enough to listen.

“This route has no return mark,” he called. “It leaves the survey cloth east of the anchor shelves. Ground is opening under movement. Carts may separate. Water is untested. Anyone may choose the northern seasonal route instead.”

He pointed to both directions.

No speech about freedom. No demand that fear prove loyalty. Just paths and costs.

Dasin turned his old plum cart east. The pear family followed with their torn-root tree. Seli’s kitchen took the new route because Seli claimed the northern path lacked enough turnips. Other households divided. Twelve chose the known seasonal track. Thirty-eight moved east. The remainder had already crossed beyond sight or traveled with the young orchards.

Ruhan marked each choice until the ground shook too hard to write.

The east shelf began to walk.

Not a dance, though thousands of practical steps contributed weight. House carts rolled. Orchard roots gripped and released. People carried bridge planks from rear gaps to front ones. The land moved because its old pressure had found a route beyond the imperial hold.

The College salt fan reached the bell ridge.

Tavra examined the advancing line. “If they close the captured anchor against this shelf, it will shear the new route.”

“What do you need?”

“Three release teams at separate edges. Volunteers who understand they may take torque through their ankles. I can cut shallow soles.”

“Not centers?”

“No fixed center. The cuts only keep the salt gaps open while the shelf passes.”

It sounded like improvisation balanced over injury.

Ruhan called the terms. Nine experienced orchard walkers volunteered. Four refused after Tavra showed the cut depth. She accepted five, divided them among three edges, and cut each sole in public view. She named every likely cost. Ossa carried instructions by voice, not drum.

The first team stepped across a salt break. Their ankles rolled outward as the shelf moved. One woman fell and crawled clear. The gap stayed open.

At the southern edge, Tavra took a place herself.

Ruhan caught the white cloth at her wrist again. “You did not name your cost.”

“My knee already carries it.”

“That is not disclosure.”

She looked toward Eda, who watched from the released approach road under Serad’s guard. “The cut may add torque to my left knee. It may lock permanently. If nobody stands there, the line closes against the orchard carts.”

“Does someone else know the cut?”

“No.”

“Then teach while you do it.”

Tavra called one of the refusing walkers to observe from outside the salt. She explained the heel angle, the release, and the point at which to drag her clear.

Only then did Ruhan release the cloth.

Tavra stepped.

The salt break opened under her scored sole. The walking shelf pulled east while the imperial anchor dragged west. Counter-motion twisted up her left leg. She did not hide the scream. The observer repeated her instructions and hauled her out after one count.

The southern line failed to close.

Ossa’s scattered drum-skin strips rattled across the moving country. No single rhythm controlled them. They sounded like rain on many roofs.

Surveyors tried to extend their field. The captured pear anchor held one small circle of ground, but Kesta’s main shelf moved beyond its reach. Salt lines stretched, thinned, and broke. Imperial maps on the officers’ boards showed orchard marks receding toward blank cloth.

Ruhan stood on the last known ridge as it separated from the approach road.

Eda remained across the widening cut. Her hand still pressed her hip. Tavra lay on the east shelf with two orchard walkers bracing her knee.

For a moment mother and daughter saw each other without a field between them, only moving distance.

Eda lifted one hand. Not pardon. Not invitation. Recognition, perhaps, or warning.

Tavra did not wave. She nodded once and let the orchard walkers carry her away.

Serad reached the edge beside Eda. He could have ordered a new hold through her company. Ruhan watched him judge her injury, the widening ground, the scattered civilians, and the insufficient local anchor. He did not spend Eda on a failing seizure. He ordered withdrawal.

The choice saved his dancers. It also preserved the College for another attempt.

By late afternoon, Kesta had moved beyond every imperial survey line.

The new shelf divided twice. Families rebuilt crossings with house rails. One goat cart overturned; no one died. Three people carried ankle injuries from Tavra’s cuts. The driver from the broken wagon could no longer lift his right arm above his chest. Tavra’s left knee stayed locked, pale striations visible around the joint. Ossa’s drum retained only the inner rim and command-marked skin. The pear family had lost its local anchor stone and half the tree’s oldest roots.

Ruhan wrote all of it.

He did not write victory.

At sunset they gathered on a broad slope unrepresented in his book. Household fires appeared at different heights as the shelf continued moving. Twelve families had taken the northern route; runners would seek them when ground allowed. Kel and the young orchards remained west of the seizure, bound toward Dorel. Kesta existed in separated choices, not one rescued mass.

Tavra sat with her bad leg splinted between two willow slats. “You need a route keeper who knows where the College will search.”

Ruhan closed the route book. Its eastern margin ended under his thumb. “I know where they will not.”

Ossa tightened the exposed inner skin on her drum. It made almost no sound when tapped. “We still need proof beyond Kesta.”

“The empire captured one of our anchors,” Ruhan said. “Proof may travel toward us.”

Tavra looked west, where the demonstration ridge had vanished below moving stone. “I go on. Eda chose to help, but she stayed with Serad.”

“She also told you not to choose for her,” Ossa said.

“I heard.”

Ruhan believed she had. He did not yet believe hearing would alter her next fear.

He tore the obsolete eastern border from the imperial survey cloth taken off a fallen rider. Then he left his own book uncut. A wrong map could remain useful if its wrongness stayed visible.

“I’ll guide you to the capital approaches,” he said. “Not for Eda alone. Kesta needs the seven-anchor orders copied where seizure cannot bury them.”

Tavra nodded. “Your terms?”

“Every route temporary. Every cost spoken. If I say the ground has changed, you listen before cutting.”

“And if I say a score has closed?”

“I listen before walking.”

That agreement could carry them as far as the next failure.

Night came slowly because the eastern shelf kept climbing into the last light. Behind them, the captured pear anchor held a small dead-still circle on an empty ridge. Ahead, no imperial milepost stood.

At the edge of firelight, he opened his route book to a blank page and wrote the day, the injuries, the divided households, and the direction of movement.

He drew no border around Kesta. On the page below, he listed the captured anchor separately from the living pear tree so no later account could confuse ground lost with a family ended.

Part II

Chapter 8 — The Unlicensed Turn

The road ended under Tavra’s good foot.

One step carried her onto a shelf of gray stone. The next found only fern and morning air. She drove her stick into the slope, caught a root with her right heel, and stopped before her locked left knee pulled her after the vanished path.

Ruhan caught the back of her coat without asking.

“Necessary,” he said.

“I know.”

That did not make gratitude immediate.

Day Six had begun with Kesta moving south while Tavra, Ossa, and Ruhan meant to travel west. By dawn they left the separated household fires behind and followed a route Ruhan described as probable. It had remained probable for three hours. Now twenty paces of stone had walked into the ravine.

Ossa peered over Tavra’s shoulder. Her drum, stripped of its outer skin, rested against her back like an open rib cage. “Can the road come back?”

“Everything can,” Ruhan said. “Schedules struggle with the question of when.”

Across the gap, the path continued beneath bent pines. The ravine measured eight paces at its narrowest. Too far to jump. Its upper wall showed fresh scrape marks where the missing shelf had descended.

Tavra lowered herself to sit. Her left knee had loosened enough to bend fifteen degrees, no more. The willow splints prevented sideways torque. She unwrapped her boot and pressed the sole against the exposed stone.

“What do you feel?” Ruhan asked.

“A road that did not fail.”

Ossa looked at the gap. “You score-cutters have severe standards for failure.”

“It turned downward because the old imperial hold kept its western edge too rigid. The release ran along this seam.” Tavra pointed with her knife. “Our path continues below.”

Ruhan studied the ravine. “Below contains a cliff.”

“Below the visible shelf. Not the whole way down.”

“You want us to descend onto a moving road we cannot see.”

“I want you to recognize its torque before rejecting it.”

His eyes narrowed. “Teach, then.”

Tavra took a scrap of plain goat sole from her pack. She had cut most of the emergency leather at Kesta, leaving pieces barely large enough for repairs. With charcoal she marked three lines: grain direction, likely bend, release edge.

“A scored sole tells the foot what the cutter expects the ground to do,” she said. “You are not a dancer. Do not follow a cut. Read wear instead.”

She handed him her right oversole. The outer heel showed fresh abrasion from the Kestan shelf.

Ruhan turned it in both hands. “This side took more weight.”

“Because the shelf moved east while I faced north. See the crushed grain near the toe?”

“Twist.”

“Torque. Follow it backward.”

He aligned the sole with the scraped ravine wall. The wear pointed toward a narrow shadow ten feet down.

“A ledge,” he said.

“The turned road.”

Ossa leaned over. “Can we use it?”

“Without a score. We lower packs, then descend by roots. No dance.”

Ruhan returned the sole. “You knew that before the lesson.”

“Yes.”

“You could have said ledge.”

“You would have trusted my authority instead of reading the ground.”

“I asked for a route, not conversion into an apprentice.”

The accusation landed close to Kel’s. Tavra had taught by manufacturing dependence, hiding the answer so Ruhan had to earn what she already possessed.

She refastened her boot. “The ledge begins under the white pine. It slopes west and may move farther before noon. That is the answer. The reading matters if I fall and cannot give you another.”

“Better.”

They lowered Ossa first because she had two working knees and the heaviest pack. Ruhan followed. Tavra descended last, left leg nearly straight, body sliding between roots. Each drop drove stone-cold pressure through her knee. At the hidden ledge, Ossa braced her from below only after Tavra asked.

The turned road carried them beneath the broken shelf and out through a pine hollow. By midday they reached open country beyond any maintained route. Here the Deep Step showed in long stone waves. Grass grew on surfaces angled like roofs. Old mileposts lay face down where their roads had left them.

Ruhan navigated by water stains and bird flight. Tavra watched his boots. His right sole had begun wearing inward, compensating for the slope. She mentioned it without offering a cut.

“Can leather help?” he asked.

“A thin lift under the outside heel. No score.”

“Cost?”

“Blister until the hip adjusts. Less knee strain later.”

“Do it at rest.”

Consent could sound ordinary. Tavra had spent years making it ceremonial by withholding the choice.

They stopped in the shell of an abandoned route house. One wall leaned against a boulder that had entered the building after construction. Tavra cut Ruhan’s lift while Ossa rationed bread and dried pears.

Ossa put the original command-skin strip between them. “We need copies before the next imperial post.”

“The next post will search leather,” Tavra said.

“Then cloth, bark, memory.”

“Memory changes under fear.”

“So does leather under fire.” Ossa tapped the Nareth cedar box. “Spread custody.”

Tavra’s hand covered the box by reflex. Ruhan saw.

“We agreed that Kesta’s proof would travel,” he said.

“The Nareth score differs from the command strip. It contains a release no one else can read safely.”

“No one else can read because you have not taught them.”

“It took me twenty years.”

Ossa’s fingers moved against her silent drum rim. “And that fact becomes your reason to keep every decision.”

Tavra finished Ruhan’s heel lift too sharply and shaved the leather thin. “The fragment stays with me.”

“For now,” Ruhan said. “But show us how to recognize if it changes hands.”

A reasonable request. Tavra unwrapped the fragment, displayed the scorched edge, the forked southern release, and a nail hole shaped like a crescent. Ossa copied those features onto cloth. Ruhan drew them in his route book without reproducing the actual score.

Before packing it away, Tavra gave Ruhan three worn sole scraps from the route house floor. Travelers had discarded them years apart. One carried a square imperial hold, one had no score, and one bore a shallow seasonal release.

“Put them in travel order,” she said.

Ruhan examined wear, mud, and nail rust. “The plain sole crossed first. Its outer edge wore on a north lean. The held sole came after someone flattened the route. The release sole came last, after the western side began rising again.”

“How do you know west?”

He aligned the seasonal cut with water stains on the inner wall. “Rain entered from the old doorway. The wearer climbed away from it.”

Tavra had expected him to miss the doorway’s former position. “Correct.”

“Surprised?”

“Annoyingly.”

Ossa held up the copied cloth. “Can this lesson move us toward safety?”

Ruhan went outside and crouched by two faint tracks leaving the ruin. The southern track looked broad and easy, but its old wheel ruts ended against a fresh stone wave. The northern path climbed through scrub, narrowing after thirty paces.

He pressed Tavra’s discarded scraps into each surface and compared their flex. “South looks usable because carts wore it before the ground lifted. North carries fresh inward twist. If the twist continues, it joins the western drainage.”

Tavra tested his conclusion against pressure through the floor. “North.”

“You knew?”

“I suspected. You found evidence I had not.”

That mattered enough to say plainly. Ruhan marked the route without adopting a score-cutter’s confidence. He had learned one reading tool, not a new profession.

They began repacking for the northern path. Tavra separated the sole scraps and labeled where each had been found, another record distributed beyond her memory.

Hooves sounded above the route-house roof.

Three, then three more.

College riders had found the broken road’s lower exit.

Ossa extinguished their small lamp. “How?”

Ruhan pointed to the boulder supporting the wall. A fresh white mark crossed it: imperial trail chalk, likely made that morning. “Survey scouts use the abandoned house.”

Tavra listened through the floor. The stone waves outside leaned north. Six horses approached from east; another pair held west, judging by vibration.

“College riders,” Ossa said after peering through a roof gap. “White cords. Anchor satchels.”

Not ordinary pursuit. Each pair carried a fist-sized local stone in a net bag. Small anchors for an ambush field.

“They expected us to use this hollow,” Ruhan said.

“They expected me to seek old walls,” Tavra replied.

Her habit had drafted them into a trap. She wanted to move immediately and explain later. The promise from Kesta stopped her.

“Costs first,” she said. “They can salt both exits and hold the floor. If we run, crossbow risk. If we remain when they set anchors, the route house becomes center weight and may collapse. I can turn the outside road downhill before the field closes.”

Ossa asked, “Cost to you?”

“Counter-motion into my left knee and right ankle. The knee may lock worse.”

“Cost to us?” Ruhan said.

“Anyone on the road takes lateral force. Falls, ankle turns. If the cut exceeds the local bend, the road may split.”

“Other options?”

Ruhan showed them a drainage hole under the fallen milepost. Too narrow for Ossa’s drum, perhaps wide enough for a person crawling. It ended somewhere beneath the north stone wave.

“We can abandon the drum,” Ossa said.

The command evidence lived on its inner skin.

“No,” Tavra said too quickly.

Ossa looked at her. “My drum. My choice.”

Tavra corrected herself. “If you choose the hole, copy the remaining orders first. It may take longer than we have.”

A rider called from outside. “Tavra Ilen. Leave the route house unarmed. Your companions may petition separately.”

Ruhan almost smiled. “Separately sounds unhealthy.”

The first hiss of poured salt came from the east exit.

Ossa ran her thumb around the exposed inner rim. “We turn the road. The drum stays with me. I accept fall risk. I do not accept a command count.”

Ruhan nodded. “I accept the heel lift and road turn. Tell me where not to stand.”

Tavra laid the plan in full. She would cut a responsive hook into her own right sole, using the road-house threshold as a temporary anchor to catch the outside route’s existing bend. Ossa would make noise without regular cadence. Ruhan would open the west shutter and throw the fallen milepost into the salt line when the road began to turn. They would exit north across dirt, never along the moving paving.

“No one follows my feet,” Tavra said. “Watch the threshold. When it rotates, move.”

Ossa repeated the plan back. Ruhan corrected the shutter’s direction. Only then did Tavra cut.

The unlicensed turn required three strokes: a hook under the outer toes, a diagonal vent across the arch, and a heel line left deliberately open. Any College examiner would call it unfinished. Completion would force the road to hold its new direction. Tavra wanted one turn and release.

The second salt line hissed outside.

Survey dancers stepped into position. Their command officer began a four-count brace.

Ossa struck the bare inner rim with a spoon. Metal clanged against wood, ugly and irregular. She kicked a loose shutter. Ruhan slapped the wall with both palms. Noise filled the route house without offering trained bodies a rhythm.

Tavra planted her right foot on the threshold.

The score caught.

The old road’s downward torque entered her sole. She turned her toes north and kept her heel light. Outside, paving answered with a grinding sweep.

Pain entered her right ankle first, hot and immediate. Her left knee took the road’s resistance as deep cold. She saw Nareth salt for half a breath and refused the memory’s count.

“Threshold,” she gasped.

Ruhan watched the stone, not her. It rotated a handspan.

He threw open the west shutter and heaved the fallen milepost. It landed across fresh step-salt. The boundary scattered.

Ossa kept making unmusical noise.

The road turned.

Not far. Twelve paces of paving swung downslope like a gate. The east riders’ horses scrambled backward. At the west exit, an anchor pair lost its prepared line when the paving moved under their salt. The route-house threshold tried to follow, but the boulder wedged it.

Wall mortar cracked above Tavra.

“Now,” Ruhan said.

Ossa crawled through the north window with her drum. Ruhan followed, then turned to Tavra. She had to release the sole before lifting her foot. Her open heel line had filled with grit.

“Knife,” she said.

He reached for her tool roll.

“No. Your heel.” She pointed. “Press the outside edge of my sole. Not the cut.”

Ruhan planted his lifted right boot where she indicated. He could recognize torque now without performing the measure. His weight opened the vent. Tavra pulled her heel free.

The route released.

Counter-motion snapped through her left knee. She fell sideways. Ruhan caught her shoulder, and together they rolled through the window as the route-house wall collapsed onto the turned road.

A rider shouted for pursuit.

His horse refused the new angle. Others dismounted, but the broken salt and fallen wall blocked them. Tavra, Ossa, and Ruhan crawled across the north wave, staying on dirt until the old house disappeared behind stone.

They reached a scrub hollow before stopping.

Tavra’s right ankle had swollen above the boot. Her left knee would not bend at all. Ossa’s forearm carried a scrape from the window. Ruhan’s heel lift remained in place, and his gait stayed even.

He knelt to inspect Tavra’s splint. “You can’t walk west today.”

“I can with two sticks.”

“You can fall west with two sticks.”

Ossa sat opposite Tavra and laid the damaged drum between them. “You disclosed the immediate cost.”

“You sound disappointed.”

“I’m deciding whether disclosure arrived because you changed or because there was time.”

Tavra unstrapped the responsive sole. Its open heel had torn wider but held. “Does motive alter the facts?”

“Motive predicts the next field.”

Ruhan said, “You also drafted me into the release.”

“I told you where to press.”

“After your sole jammed.”

“The alternative involved a wall.”

“You knew jamming could happen.”

Tavra had known grit could close the vent. She had assigned herself the risk and treated any necessary assistance as an emergency exemption. The pattern remained: take the central danger, then make everyone else responsible for preserving her.

“I should have named the jam,” she said.

“And the chance I would have to enter your sole’s torque,” Ruhan replied.

“Yes.”

Ossa leaned forward. “At Kesta you cut volunteers after naming bruises and falls. Here you chose the turn, assigned us jobs, and kept the failure branches in your head.”

“We had riders pouring salt.”

“We had enough time for a plan. You gave us the useful trunk and hid the branches.”

Tavra looked at the swollen ankle, the locked knee, and the two people who had survived despite incomplete knowledge. Success tempted her to defend method. Nareth had survived in that temptation for twelve years.

“What do you require?” she asked.

“Not require,” Ossa said. “Agree.”

The correction cut cleanly.

Tavra placed the ruined responsive sole on the ground where all three could see it. “Before the next measure, I disclose every cost I can predict, including rescue roles if my part fails. I name what I do not know. If there is no time, I ask whether you accept acting without full information.”

Ruhan said, “And if anyone refuses?”

“I find another plan or act alone without making them recover me.”

“Acting alone can still put us in the aftermath,” Ossa said.

Tavra’s first answer rose ready: then leave. She let it die.

“Then you choose whether my solitary act remains compatible with traveling together.”

Ossa considered the tempo of the words. “Acceptable until tested.”

Ruhan drew the turned road in his book as a hinge, not a line. “Name today’s unknown.”

Tavra touched her knee. “I don’t know how much more counter-motion this joint can take. I may lose walking capacity before we reach the capital.”

Neither ally hurried to comfort her.

That absence felt honest.

They built a carrying sling from the torn route-house curtain, two pine poles, and Tavra’s spare leather. She objected once to the knot, then explained why it would slip rather than seizing the rope herself. Ruhan changed it. Ossa took the front pole. Tavra kept the score knife within reach but not in her hand.

Before lifting, Ossa made her drink and checked the right ankle for heat. Ruhan loosened the willow splints without removing them. Tavra described which touch caused stone pressure and which merely hurt. The distinction let them adjust the sling so her left leg rode straight while the swollen right ankle remained above the lower strap.

“This adds time,” Tavra said.

“It returns time later,” Ruhan replied. “A route can be shortened by not dropping the passenger.”

Ossa tested the front pole against her shoulder. “A military innovation we shamefully overlooked.”

Their care neither cured nor reduced the meaning of the injury. It changed what travel remained possible.

At sunset, they found the west route by reading wear on Ruhan’s altered heel and water stains under the stone waves. The road they had turned remained behind them, blocking pursuit for a few hours rather than forever.

Tavra lay in the sling and watched unheld country pass above her. Every dip demanded a change in grip. Ossa and Ruhan discussed each change before lifting.

For the first time since Dorel, Tavra traveled without deciding where every foot would land.

The discomfort did not feel like freedom. It felt like dependence, which might have been the necessary beginning.

Chapter 9 — A Bridge Performed in Rain

Rain erased the crossing while Ruhan stood inside its first footprint.

The gorge dropped on both sides of him, black water far below. Three local slabs formed a narrow platform from the eastern bank. Beyond them, seven more lay tilted beneath the current, remnants of a seasonal bridge that had returned to the riverbed during spring movement.

Ruhan stepped backward before the final patch of step-salt dissolved around his boot.

“The boundary’s gone,” he called.

Ossa lowered Tavra’s carrying sling onto the bank. Rain drummed against the command-marked inner skin of her instrument, producing a faint papery rattle. Tavra pushed herself upright, one hand on the locked left knee.

“How much salt remains?”

Ossa opened the oilcloth packet. Gray slurry coated the bottom. “Enough to season an unpopular dinner.”

Ruhan knelt at the edge. The old crossing route had connected two red-stone shelves over the narrowest reach of the gorge. Since the last Kestan atlas mark, the western shelf had moved six paces downstream. No bridge could join the old points now.

“The pass road lies west,” he said. “If we turn north, the next ford takes two days.”

“College riders take less,” Ossa said.

Hoofprints had appeared twice that morning on parallel shelves. Pursuit no longer pressed close enough to hear, but riders with held-road horses would gain once the rain stopped.

Tavra examined the gorge through narrowed eyes. “The submerged slabs still belong to this bank.”

“Belong is a large word for wet stone.”

“Their grain matches. The old measure lowered them but did not carry them away.”

Ruhan saw what she meant. The water broke in pale lines over rectangular shapes. Existing terrain, not summoned matter. A temporary arch could rise if the slabs accepted the western shelf’s new position.

He looked at Tavra’s legs. “No.”

“You asked no question.”

“I saved time.”

Ossa crouched beside them. “Costs first.”

Tavra did not protest. “Scale: ten slabs, perhaps twelve. Distance: fourteen paces. We do not hold against the river, only raise and join long enough to cross. Counter-motion will press downward through whoever stands at center. My knee cannot receive it safely.”

“Can anyone else?” Ruhan asked.

“Ossa can carry rhythm and one outer line. You can set and catch the local anchor without dancing. I can center from the sling with my left leg unloaded.”

“That sounds like carrying downward pressure through the rest of you.”

“It is.”

“Likely injury?” Ossa asked.

“Bruising through pelvis, shoulders, and right leg. My left knee may take spill despite the sling. If salt washes out, slabs can separate before release. Anyone on the bridge falls.”

“Unknowns?”

“The western shelf’s present torque. How deep the anchor pocket runs. Whether the old bridge score left unfinished movement in the slabs.”

Ruhan pointed north. “Alternative costs two days, more food than we carry, and probable rider contact. I choose the bridge if we can mark an exit not dependent on visible salt.”

Ossa looked at the exposed drum. “I choose it if my count follows Ruhan’s anchor calls. No command pattern.”

Tavra accepted both conditions. “Then you each choose your own shoes. I cut no score for Ruhan. He handles the anchor on dirt.”

They spent the next hour assessing local stone in rain. Ruhan found a fist-sized piece under the eastern ledge that matched both bank and submerged slabs. The old anchor pocket lay three feet from the water, a round hollow packed with leaves. He cleared it by hand.

Ossa built a boundary from what the weather would not immediately carry: charcoal mixed with wax under overhanging stones, rope along the exposed bank, willow pegs driven into dirt. Only the final lines required step-salt. She saved the remaining paste in a covered cup.

Tavra cut two soles while seated in the sling. Her own received shallow downward vents along the right heel, each left open toward the river. Ossa’s outer soles took broad flex lines and no center marks. Tavra showed every cut before binding them.

Ruhan studied the worn route marks around the gorge. “The west shelf leans upstream.”

Tavra handed him a sole scrap. “Confirm.”

He pressed it against wet stone. Water ran diagonally across the leather, but grain crush from past travelers angled the other way. “Upstream now. Downstream when the bridge last stood.”

“The slabs will twist during the rise.”

“How do we let them?”

“Build the arch crooked.”

Ruhan almost laughed. “Empire has damaged my expectations. That sounds sensible.”

He selected two rope lengths, one for the anchor and one as a crossing handline. They tied the handline to living willow roots on both banks using a thrown stone and Ossa’s stronger arm. The rope sagged into the gorge but would rise with the slabs.

Before closing the field, Ruhan crossed the eastern bank twice to inspect what their work might disturb. A fish seller’s abandoned smoking rack stood above the flood line. Three wicker traps remained tied beneath it, all empty. Farther upstream, a goat track descended to the only accessible water for half a mile. Raising the slabs would narrow the current and flood that track until release.

“We have no one here to ask,” he said.

“Then name the absent users,” Tavra replied. “And leave evidence of the change.”

Ruhan cut a warning mark into the smoking rack: temporary raised stone, crossing direction, expected release before dark. Kestan practice used no fixed symbol for a bridge performed under pursuit, so he added words in Meren trade script. He disliked deciding for unknown travelers. The best available repair involved making the decision visible and short.

Ossa tested sound against the gorge. A rim tap returned from the west wall a half count late. Any repeating cadence would echo into false instructions.

“I’ll vary material as well as interval,” she said. “Wood for lift, stone for angle, palm for release.”

Ruhan repeated the meanings until he could distinguish them through rain. Tavra then asked them to rehearse without movement. He called first weight. Ossa struck wood. Tavra shifted no foot. They repeated with the anchor lean, interruption, and forced release.

On the third rehearsal, Ruhan called angle while Ossa struck lift.

“We would have raised the wrong edge,” Tavra said.

“Again,” he answered.

They continued until each mistake appeared during practice rather than over water. The delay cost fifteen minutes. It also revealed that Ossa watched Tavra’s heel whenever Ruhan’s voice softened, a remnant of expecting the center to outrank the caller. She changed position so Tavra fell outside her direct sight.

Ruhan wrapped the anchor rope around his forearm, then removed it. A sudden pull could trap the limb. He tied a sliding waist loop instead and kept the free end in his left hand. His right hand remained available to guide the stone.

“Recoil can still reach the wrist,” Tavra warned.

“I accept ordinary pull, not a locked anchor. If the stone rises more than half out of the pocket, we release.”

Ossa touched the sagging handline. “If it parts?”

“We crawl the slabs or return east. Nobody jumps from the arch because riders arrive.”

Their plan now held limits, users, signals, and exits. It did not hold the weather.

Rain thickened. The river climbed against the lowest visible stone.

Tavra said, “We begin now or take the ford.”

Ruhan placed the local anchor in its pocket without locking it. He could pull it free if the measure tightened beyond their disclosure. Ossa stood at the outer line with a wooden spoon over her bare drum rim.

Tavra remained in the sling, poles braced across two bank stones. She planted only her right scored sole. Her left leg rested straight above the mud.

“Ruhan calls,” Ossa said.

He tested the anchor. It leaned upstream, matching the western shelf.

“First weight.”

Ossa struck the rim once.

Tavra pressed her right heel down.

The nearest submerged slab lifted through brown water. It rose flat, hesitated, then turned upstream half a pace. River poured off its edges.

“Second weight,” Ruhan called.

Another irregular tap. Tavra shifted to the outer vent. A second slab rose beside the first, lower and angled. Their edges met without locking.

The arch began as a stair leading into rain.

They raised four slabs before counter-motion reached Tavra’s shoulders. Her arms shook against the sling poles. Ruhan asked whether to release.

“Continue two,” she said. “Then reassess.”

Not command. Her threshold.

At six slabs, the crossing reached the gorge’s center. The western shelf remained eight paces away. Water struck the unfinished edge and sprayed over their rope.

Ossa’s count never repeated. Spoon against rim, palm against wood, boot against dirt. She watched Ruhan, not Tavra.

“Anchor turns,” Ruhan said.

The local stone rotated in its pocket. Upstream torque pulled at the rope around his left forearm. He loosened the line and let the anchor face west.

Tavra felt the change. The raised slabs slewed. Their crooked joins tightened into an arch pointing toward the moved shelf.

“Better,” she gasped.

A hoofbeat answered from the eastern path.

Riders appeared through rain above the gorge. Four College whites, then two survey dancers carrying salt horns. The lead officer raised a crossbow.

“Hold position!” he called.

Ruhan shouted back, “An excellent phrase for another landscape.”

The officer dismounted. “Break the measure and surrender the stolen College property.”

“Breaking it drops six slabs,” Ossa said.

“Then release safely.”

Tavra spoke without looking up. “They’ll take the anchor when we do.”

Ruhan had chosen the bridge knowing riders might reach them. He had not chosen to fight trained officers while holding a local stone.

“New cost,” he said. “Rider pressure before completion. Options?”

Ossa kept irregular sound moving. “I can fracture their approach count, but that risks the outer line.”

Tavra said, “Raise the next four fast. Higher bruising. Greater chance the arch overshoots.”

Ruhan watched the river and west shelf. “Do it. I accept anchor recoil. Ossa?”

“I accept outer-line slip.”

Tavra breathed once. “I accept more center load. Release if my right heel stops answering.”

Ruhan called three rapid weights.

Ossa struck wood, stone, wood.

Slabs seven through nine rose in sequence. The arch steepened toward the western shelf. Tavra’s right hip drove into the sling. She cried out but kept her heel responsive.

The riders spread along the bank. One survey dancer poured fresh step-salt across their retreat route, trying to capture the bridge field inside an imperial boundary.

Rain blurred his line as quickly as he laid it.

The final submerged slab stood on edge beneath the water. Its broad face caught the river. When Tavra lifted, it did not rise. It swung.

Ruhan felt the anchor jerk.

“Broken response!” he called.

The local stone tore halfway from its pocket. He threw both hands around it. The rope snapped taut.

His right wrist folded backward.

A crack traveled up his arm.

Pain erased the gorge for one breath. He kept the anchor from leaving the pocket because if it went, nine raised slabs would lose their relation to the bank.

“My right wrist,” he shouted. “Cracked. I cannot hold another recoil.”

Disclosure while the injury still happened. Tavra heard him.

“Release or transfer?” she asked.

Ossa moved toward the anchor.

“No,” Ruhan said. “Her outer line goes if she leaves.” He looped the rope around his waist and held the stone with his left hand. “One count. Then release whether the last slab lands or not.”

Tavra looked at him, waiting for certainty he did not have.

“One,” he said.

Ossa struck the drum rim with her palm.

Tavra turned her right foot upstream.

The edge-on slab rolled under water. Its top broke the surface like a shoulder. Instead of forcing it flat, the score let it remain angled. The stone met the ninth slab and leaned against the western shelf.

A crooked final step. Usable.

“Release,” Ruhan called.

Tavra opened her heel vents. Ossa stepped out of the outer line. Ruhan pulled the local anchor free with his left hand.

The arch settled under river weight. It had no hold left beyond the natural brace of slab against slab. It might stand five minutes or fifty.

The lead officer fired.

His bolt struck the sling pole near Tavra’s shoulder.

Ossa seized the front pole. Ruhan took the rear with his left hand, right arm tucked against his chest. They lifted Tavra onto the first slab.

“Go,” she said.

Riders reached the eastern boundary. Their fixed soles slipped where rain had turned step-salt into gray oil. One fell. The others abandoned formation and followed on foot.

The bridge rose steeply to its crooked center. Water drove through every join. Ossa climbed backward at the front, hauling the sling. Ruhan pushed with his left shoulder. His right wrist pulsed in time with his heart.

At the sixth slab, the third join shifted.

“Keep moving,” Tavra said. “No holding step.”

They climbed. The handline rose beside them, slick but intact. Ossa took it at the arch crest. Ruhan could not grip with his right hand, so he hooked his elbow over the rope and shoved the sling pole along his hip.

The first rider stepped onto the eastern slab.

Counterweight changed.

The arch flattened a fraction. Water swept over Ruhan’s boots. He saw the rider hesitate and recognized the same imperial expectation he had once held: that a road built for crossing should remain available to everyone long enough to be called a road.

This bridge had been performed for one passage and one escape. It carried no promise beyond consent of bodies and stone.

Ossa reached the angled final slab. She jumped to the western shelf and pulled. Tavra’s sling scraped across the edge. Ruhan stepped after it.

His heel caught the handline.

The broken wrist struck stone. He nearly blacked out. Tavra caught his coat from the sling and Ossa dragged both of them onto the shelf.

Behind them, the lead rider reached the arch crest.

The first eastern join slipped free.

The bridge lowered one slab at a time rather than collapsing at once. The rider ran back. He jumped to the bank as the third stone descended. No one fell into the gorge.

The remaining slabs returned to the river, crooked arch becoming broken stair, then pale shapes under brown water. The angled western stone held longest. It rolled away after the others and vanished.

The handline tore from the eastern willow and streamed against their bank.

Ruhan lay on his back in wet grass, right arm across his chest. The gorge separated them from the College riders again.

Ossa cut two straight sticks for a splint. Tavra directed without touching until Ruhan asked her to inspect the wrist.

“Crack, perhaps two,” she said. “No open break. Fingers?”

He moved them. Pain sharpened but they answered.

“Splint through the elbow. No carrying with that side.”

“Route book?”

“Left-handed notes.”

Ossa wrapped the wrist and forearm with the last dry cloth from inside her coat. “You’ll be slow.”

“I was slow before. I disguised it with confidence.”

He tested the splint by lifting his route book and nearly dropped it. The right hand could support no useful weight. Writing would require his left, climbing would require a rope around his torso, and any future anchor work needed another holder. He named each loss so neither companion could mistake stubbornness for capacity.

Ruhan drew the gorge crossing with his left hand. The line wandered. He marked the submerged slabs, the empty fish traps, and the temporary warning on the eastern rack. Even a failed-looking map could preserve obligations to people they never met.

Tavra lay under the rain with the crossbow bolt still buried in her sling pole. Center load had bruised her right side from shoulder to hip. Her left knee remained unchanged, which counted as luck rather than health.

They moved west only after every cost had been named. Ossa carried the drum and most supplies. Tavra returned to the sling. Ruhan took the rear pole on his left shoulder, right arm bound across his chest. The arrangement hurt all three and remained possible.

At dusk they found shelter beneath a rock shelf above the gorge road. Rain softened to mist. Ossa built no fire because riders might search the opposite bank until dark.

Tavra opened the cedar box.

Ruhan expected another lesson. Instead she took out the blackened fragment and placed it on his route book.

“The Nareth score goes with you,” she said.

His right wrist throbbed. “Why?”

“Because I cannot retrieve it from you without your cooperation.”

Ossa looked up from tightening the splint.

Tavra continued. “You are not a dancer. You can identify the fragment but cannot use it to improvise a measure. If I am captured or decide urgency excuses taking it back, custody remains outside my control.”

Ruhan examined the scorched edge, forked southern release, and crescent nail hole. “Risk to me: riders search me. Risk to the evidence: rain, loss, my ignorance.”

“Yes.”

“Alternative custody?”

“Ossa already carries the command orders on her drum. One arrest should not take both.”

Ruhan opened the oilskin pocket sewn inside his route book cover. “I accept until we place it somewhere safer. You may advise. You do not take it while I sleep.”

Tavra’s mouth tightened at the necessary insult. “Agreed.”

He slid the Nareth score into the pocket and tied the cover closed with his left hand.

Across the gorge, lanterns searched a bank with no bridge. Between the travelers, custody had changed more permanently than the stone.

When they left at first dark, Tavra knew the fragment’s route only because Ruhan chose to tell her.

Chapter 10 — The Stone in Tavra’s Blood

The fever in Tavra’s left knee boiled the rain cold before it reached her boot.
