She lay in the carrying sling beneath the eaves of an abandoned barracks and watched water strike her trouser leg, steam faintly, then run down the willow splint. The joint itself felt buried under winter stone. Every heartbeat pushed heat around something that did not belong to flesh.

Ossa lowered the front pole. Ruhan set his left end down carefully, right wrist bound across his chest.

“We stop,” he said.

“The roof has three holes.”

“The next roof lies beyond two ridges.”

“It may have four.” Ossa unstrapped her exposed drum. “We honor the superior ruin.”

The building stood above a road that had once served Nareth’s northern work companies. Faded paint over the door showed a center shoe inside a pension wreath. One wall had sunk into the hillside. The bunks inside remained dry enough for splints and sleep.

Tavra recognized the barracks pattern: twelve beds, company stove, boot hooks near the entrance, a supervisor’s desk facing everyone else. She had inspected places like it before Nareth and never asked why center-dancers slept on the ground floor while officers occupied rooms above.

Ossa cut away Tavra’s knee wrapping. The skin had gone pale from kneecap to lower thigh. Narrow gray striations spread beneath it like mineral grain.

Ruhan touched nothing. “What can we do?”

“Cool it. Keep the leg straight. No ground-dance.”

“That final instruction sounds directed at you,” Ossa said.

“It is.”

They used rainwater in folded cloths. Each fresh compress warmed within minutes. Tavra described numbness along the inner calf and stabbing pressure behind the knee. She did not call it ordinary pain. Naming the exact sensation gave the others something to monitor.

Ruhan searched the supervisor’s desk with his left hand. Most drawers had swollen shut. One opened far enough to reveal mouse nests, broken chalk, and a brass key tagged ROOT STORE.

“Nareth workers kept roots?” Ossa asked.

“Road crews stored winter vegetables below barracks,” Tavra said. “Sometimes anchor samples.”

The key turned in a floor hatch behind the stove. Damp air rose from stone steps.

Ruhan held up his splinted wrist. “I can carry a lamp or climb. Not both.”

Ossa took the lamp. Tavra should have remained on the bunk. Instead she fitted both sticks under her arms.

“No,” Ossa said.

Tavra waited until the word stopped provoking an automatic answer. “The mark over the door belongs to Nareth’s dissolved Third Company. I may know what records mean. I cannot climb safely. Will one of you brace me if I go?”

“Cost?” Ruhan asked.

“More swelling. Fall risk. No measure.”

Ossa considered. “You go down seated, one step at a time. We stop if the fever rises.”

They lowered Tavra into the cellar. The work humiliated her only when she treated dependence as a verdict. She sat on each damp step, moved her left leg with both hands, and let Ossa take her weight at the shoulders.

The root store filled two rooms. The first held collapsed bins and clay jars fused to their shelves. In the second, cedar document boxes stood on a raised platform. Water had destroyed the lower row. The upper lids still bore pension-office seals.

Ossa lifted one down.

Inside lay pension ledgers from twenty-nine years of Nareth road service. Each page listed a dancer, years at center, visible lithification on retirement, and later claims by family. Tavra turned pages while Ossa held the lamp.

Mera Sol: left ankle, two years after service.

Hadrik Ven: lumbar stone, six years.

Issa Pell: jaw and throat, eleven years, death during winter.

The official injury columns ended at retirement. Family claims continued in smaller script, often denied because deterioration occurred after active company duty.

Several pages included letters folded into the binding. A daughter asked why her father’s pension paid for an ankle brace but not the quarry chair he needed when stone reached his hips. A widow enclosed a road-company commendation beside a burial invoice. The clerk had answered both in the same careful hand: service connection unverified after discharge.

Ossa found the pension formula on the back cover. A center earned more for each documented patch of bodily stone while employed. The policy rewarded dancers for visible damage, then treated later spread as private illness.

“They paid people to stay until the first proof appeared,” she said. “Then stopped counting when proof grew.”

Tavra remembered company inspections. She had encouraged dancers to display pale ankle lines because the marks improved retirement shares. At the time, she called that honesty about cost. The ledger showed a narrower transaction: the empire priced the injury it could use and abandoned the part that outlived service.

Ruhan read over her shoulder. “They knew bodies failed years later.”

“Pension clerks knew claims existed.”

“Twenty-nine years of entries make existence difficult to dismiss.”

Tavra found names from her Nareth company. Three dancers she had believed recovered had later lost movement. One died seven years after the hold when stone reached her ribs. The claim carried a red denial mark: no direct service cause established.

Tavra put the ledger down before her hands tore it.

Ossa opened another box. Route maps filled it, each overlay showing the same northern hold across successive years. Wells disappeared from later surveys. Orchard symbols thinned. Marginal notes mentioned poor root yield, dry cellar walls, and spring relocation requests rejected because the road remained strategically necessary.

“No one joined the records,” Ossa said.

“They joined them,” Tavra replied. “They filed the join under separate offices.”

Ruhan searched the collapsed bins. His boot struck something that rang like fired clay.

He cleared rotten wood with his left hand.

Fossilized roots filled the lowest vegetable bed.

They had not rotted. Every fine branch remained, rendered in gray stone from thick crown to hairlike tips. Soil around them had hardened into a solid block carrying the shape of old moisture. Tavra touched one root and felt no life, only the smooth record of where life had once tried to grow.

The permanent hold above had fixed more than a road. Counter-motion had entered the ground below it, locking water paths, soil spaces, and root growth.

She had known bodies paid. She had built her resistance around Eda’s body, her own knees, and dead centers. The cellar expanded the cost beyond any center’s skin.

“If Dominion joins seven holds,” Ruhan said, “how much land does this repeat?”

“I don’t know.”

“Can Serad?”

“He can know projections if survey offices gave them to him. These ledgers prove Nareth. We need the capital archive for scale.”

A faint knocking came from behind the fossilized soil block.

Ossa lifted the lamp. “Water?”

Tavra pressed her palm to the stone. Cold vibration tapped in irregular bursts. A spring still pushed through its old channel, trapped behind mineralized soil.

“Leave it,” Ruhan said at once.

Tavra did not argue. “If we release it, the cellar may flood. The foundation may shift. Counter-motion enters whoever cuts. If we leave it, we preserve evidence undisturbed and the spring remains trapped.”

“Other way to document?” Ossa asked.

“Draw the roots, take a small sample, copy the ledgers. We do not need the spring to prove the hold.”

The knocking grew stronger as rain fed the hillside.

Ruhan examined the wall. “Foundation already shifts. Water has nowhere else to go. If pressure breaks it later, the whole platform and records may wash out.”

They listened. Tavra wanted to cut because a trapped thing called to every old center reflex. Want did not decide.

“I can make a hand-width release toward the collapsed bins,” she said. “No salt. The root block serves as local anchor. Likely cost: downward compression in my left knee because the spring rises. Unknown: how much pressure accumulated over twelve years.”

“You said no ground-dance,” Ossa replied.

“Yes.”

“Does evidence justify breaking that limit?”

“No. Protecting the records might. We can move the boxes first and let the wall fail without a scored release.”

Ruhan weighed the routes. “A natural break could strike any direction. A narrow channel gives us an exit. I accept moving records, then release, if Tavra does not center alone.”

“No one else can take this through an injured body,” Tavra said.

“I mean we control whether you continue. Ossa holds your shoulders off the floor. I pull the records. One cut only. If the channel does not open, stop.”

Ossa agreed after repeating the limit.

They carried the pension ledgers upstairs first. Ruhan tucked two route overlays under his coat. Ossa moved the remaining dry boxes. Tavra waited beside the fossilized roots with no knife in hand until they returned.

For the release, she cut a shallow crescent into a scrap sole and bound it over her right boot. She showed them the single channel. No hidden branch.

Ossa braced her from behind. Ruhan stood at the cellar steps, ready to call retreat.

Tavra placed her right heel against the mineralized soil.

The spring struck the score.

Water found the crescent and drove through. A fist-wide channel opened toward the collapsed bins. Mud and stone fragments burst across the floor.

Counter-motion entered Tavra’s left knee.

The fever vanished into cold.

Stone seemed to pour upward through the joint, not as liquid or thought, but as an impossible deepening of weight. The gray striations under her skin widened. Her leg locked from ankle to mid-thigh.

“One cut,” Ruhan shouted. “Release.”

Tavra lifted her right foot.

The channel remained open without further dance. Spring water rushed through the fossil bed and out beneath the collapsed wall. The barracks shifted half an inch. Dust fell from the ceiling, then stopped.

Ossa dragged Tavra toward the steps. She could not feel the inner side of her left calf at all.

Upstairs, they wrapped the knee in cold cloth again. It no longer heated the compress. Pale mineral lines reached above the joint like roots climbing the wrong body.

“You chose after disclosure,” Ossa said. “That does not make the cost wise.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Tavra looked at the ledgers stacked beside the stove. “I still thought taking it into me would simplify the choice.”

Ruhan sat with his splinted wrist across his knees. “It simplified nothing. Now we have evidence and another person to carry.”

The statement held no cruelty. Tavra accepted it without asking him to make sacrifice beautiful.

By late afternoon, they had copied six ledger pages, the delayed-failure totals, and three route maps. Ossa wrapped the original pension ledgers in barracks oilcloth. They left most boxes in place above the now-draining cellar and marked the building for road-company veterans who might know the names.

Ruhan added a custody list inside his book using his left hand. Ossa carried the wrapped originals. Tavra kept the root sample in a padded boot pouch where a fall would not grind it. Each item had a holder and a second person who knew its markings. The arrangement slowed departure, but no single capture could erase both documents and witness.

Tavra asked Ruhan for the Nareth score. Not its return, only sight of the scorched edge.

He opened the route book and held the fragment where she could compare its release turn to the spring crescent. The same principle appeared in both: do not oppose stored movement; give it a narrow exit and stop before the new direction becomes a hold.

“You keep it,” she said.

“I intended to.”

At dusk Ossa spread their two possible routes on the floor. Southeast led toward Eda and the returning Fifth Measure. West led to the capital archive through old road-company settlements.

Tavra’s fear chose southeast before anyone spoke.

She looked at the fossilized-root sample beside the ledgers. Reaching Eda without proof of continental cost would repeat the demonstration field: correct warning, private urgency, no shared basis for choice. Serad could call her frightened. Eda could call her controlling. Both would be right.

“We go west,” Tavra said. “Capital archive. We copy the pension and soil records before I approach Eda again.”

Ossa watched for hesitation hidden as command. “Reason?”

“Dominion will kill land beyond its centers. I know Nareth proves it. I do not know the scale. Evidence must travel where my custody and Eda’s belief cannot decide whether it survives.”

Ruhan closed his book over the Nareth fragment. “West requires the veterans’ road. My wrist cannot climb the direct pass.”

“Then we ask the road-company settlement for shelter and a route.”

Not demand. Ask.

They left the abandoned barracks at nightfall carrying ledgers, copied maps, a root sample, and injuries no stable road had ever listed honestly. Behind them, the released spring ran out under the wall and cut its own shallow course downhill.

Tavra traveled in the sling. Her left knee held stone from ankle to thigh, colder now than rain.

For the first time, she turned away from the shortest road to her daughter because rescue without the whole cost would only build another hidden center.

Chapter 11 — The Choir of Bent Ankles

Ossa heard the veterans before she saw their lamps.

A heel dragged across stone somewhere beyond the shuttered tollhouse. Another foot answered with a soft double tap, toe and outer edge, followed by the padded drop of a cane. None of it kept a marching count. The sounds overlapped like rain beginning on different roofs.

She raised one hand. Ruhan stopped beside her, cradling his splinted right wrist against his coat. Tavra took another step before her left knee refused. The old score-cutter caught the tollhouse wall without speaking.

“Road-company refuge,” Ruhan murmured. “Or a convincing imitation.”

The door opened inward. A broad woman with a white braid held a splitting maul at waist height. Her right boot had been built up by three finger-widths. Pale stone showed through the skin above its collar.

“You brought a drum,” she said to Ossa.

“I brought what remains of one.”

“That answer has buried people.”

“So have I.”

The woman studied her, then Tavra’s locked knee. Recognition changed her grip before it changed her face. “Ilen.”

Tavra kept her palm against the wall. “Hessa Dorn.”

“You look worse than your pension drawing.”

“I never drew one.”

“Of course not. You left the rest of us to prove we deserved ours.” Hessa lowered the maul. “Inside. Riders crossed the east shelf at dusk. If they ask, I’ll say your footprints continued north. If they search, I’ll use the maul and regret the floor.”

The refuge occupied the old tollhouse cellar, where imperial barrels had once waited for seals. Twenty-three former road workers sat among wool pallets, cookpots, and racks of altered shoes. A charcoal brazier warmed the damp without clearing it, and every cup bore a former company number scratched nearly away. Their bodies carried the work in separate dialects. One man’s ankles leaned inward as though still bracing a crowned road. A woman called Seni walked with her left foot always seeking lower ground. Two brothers shared a bench because one could rise but not turn and the other could turn but not rise. Nobody looked away from Tavra’s knee or pretended not to understand its pale shine.

Ossa set her drum beside the door. Its naked frame, missing the outer skin lost in Kesta, looked less like an instrument than an accusation.

Hessa pointed Tavra toward a pallet. “Sit before your pride adds another joint to the ledger.”

“The spring released,” Tavra said. “Counter-motion entered the knee.”

“We heard.”

“You couldn’t have.”

“Stone travels slowly. Workers’ news does not.”

A boy of perhaps twelve brought heated bricks wrapped in cloth. Tavra accepted one under her thigh, careful not to bend the knee farther. Ruhan declined help with his wrist until the boy stared him into surrender. Ossa remained standing. In a room full of damaged centers, the straightness of her own ankles felt indecent.

Seni ladled root broth into cups. “Show us what you found.”

Ruhan looked to Tavra. Tavra looked at Ossa, a small turn of the eyes that tried to make a decision without appearing to command it.

Ossa shook her head. “His custody.”

With his left hand, Ruhan removed folded pension pages from inside his coat. Tavra had copied the names at the Nareth barracks, while the original ledgers remained hidden under a split floor stone. He laid the copies on a flour board. Beside them he placed a cloth bundle holding chips from the fossilized roots.

The room tightened around the table without anyone calling a count.

Hessa picked up a root chip. “Pear?”

“Fig,” Tavra said. “The cellar owner planted it after the holding. The root reached the old salt field and hardened.”

A narrow veteran named Brin rubbed one thumb over a pension name. “I carried road seventeen with Laro Ves. College said lung fever took him.”

“His widow wrote mineral cough in the margin,” Ruhan said. “Six years after retirement.”

“She wrote to the pension office seven times.”

No one offered comfort. The veterans began naming injuries instead. A knee from the western grain rise. Three fused toes from a harbor shelf. A shoulder that had received a quarry road’s sideways drag. A jaw that clicked whenever the southern causeway tried to sink. Each account entered the room on its own tempo. Ossa listened for the habit she had once used to bind separate bodies into one obedient unit. She refused it. Their pauses belonged to them.

“College called mine age,” Hessa said, tapping the built-up boot. “Age arrived in one afternoon after we held a tax bridge.”

Seni lifted her trouser hem. Stone traced both shins in pale forks. “They called this hereditary.”

“My mother climbed until she died,” said Brin. “I had to be carried from my last measure.”

The accounts did not form harmony. They rubbed against one another, rough and unresolved. Ossa understood then why the College preferred ledgers. A column could flatten every limp into service years. This room gave each cost a gait.

Hessa opened a cabinet whose shelves held the shoes the veterans had worn on their last assignments. None resembled a keepsake. Salt had furred the seams. Leather scores had split where the ground refused their intended limits. Each pair carried a brass pension tag, though several tags bore fresh chisel marks where the College had revised a service category.

“Those marks arrived after retirement,” Hessa said. “A center gets full stone credit. A brace gets half. A worker whose joint hardens after release gets no credit unless three licensed witnesses saw the first discoloration.”

Seni drew out a child-sized pair. “Maro Quill entered as a water carrier at thirteen. By fourteen he could feel a bridge settling before trained feet noticed. They put him beside the center without licensing him, so his pension record calls him auxiliary labor.”

“What happened to him?” Ruhan asked.

“He lives above the western lime pits. His spine bends toward every bridge he hears.”

Tavra examined a separated sole. The old score had routed pressure toward the wearer’s heel instead of the official center mark. “Somebody altered this after cutting.”

“I did,” Hessa said. “The bridge began taking the crew sideways. Our cutter followed the approved diagram. I added a spill channel with a nail.”

“You saved the line.”

“I saved six bodies and lost my license. Then the company listed the alteration as proof that my ankle damage came from misconduct.” Hessa returned the shoe to its shelf. “That is how the College keeps every outcome useful. Obey, and the road proves the system. Improvise, and the injury proves the worker.”

Ossa knew the same arithmetic from military discipline. A cadence officer who kept a line received honor. One who broke count to save a stumbling marcher accepted responsibility for every later gap. The rule made obedience appear safer by assigning all visible blame to refusal.

Brin lifted his trouser cuff again, this time exposing a stamped iron ring above the ankle. “Pension examiner sealed that on me after my fifth year. Said it would measure whether the bend progressed.”

The ring had sunk into mineralized flesh. Ossa swallowed against the remembered sight of marching irons, though this device carried the neat College flower instead of an army number.

“Does it come off?” she asked.

“With the foot.”

Nobody smiled.

Ruhan turned one of the revised brass tags over in his good hand. “Could these prove concealment?”

“They prove alteration,” Tavra said. “Miren’s clerks will claim correction. We need the earlier entries beside the later ones, plus bodies willing to testify.”

“You keep saying bodies,” Seni replied. “Try names.”

Tavra’s face tightened. “Maro Quill. Brin. Hessa Dorn. Laro Ves.” She looked around the cellar and continued until she had repeated every name offered. Some took her more than one attempt. Nobody rescued her from the work.

Ossa watched the old reflex in Tavra strain toward a plan. The score-cutter wanted to arrange testimony, match injury to route, turn suffering into a shape that officials could not evade. Yet she closed her mouth and waited for Hessa to decide what the cabinet meant.

Hessa shut the doors. “We did not keep these for an imperial hearing. We kept them because new crews deserve to know what the diagrams leave out. If your archive route opens, take the pension pages. These shoes stay with their owners.”

“Agreed,” Tavra said.

The answer came promptly, but it came without correction. Ossa marked that small restraint and refused to praise it aloud.

Hessa pushed a second board across the table. Seven charcoal circles crossed a map of imperial routes. Dates crowded their edges.

“Scheduled anchors,” she said. “Permanent crews have been moving stones under repair warrants. We unload the carts. We know the difference between granite from a bridge and anchor stone wrapped in wet felt.”

Tavra leaned forward. “Seven holds.”

“Seven we’ve seen. The capital counts as another sort of knot.” Hessa touched three circles. “Rehearsal orders put these companies in place within ten days. The notices promise double pension credit.”

“Paid in stone,” Seni said.

Ossa recognized the routes. Hadrun lay beneath the southern circle, where grain roads crossed the ridge Pel had died upon. She had known the command skin named seven anchors. She had not known the College had recruited retired workers to train their replacements.

“You asked why we opened the door,” Hessa said. “Not for Tavra. Half this room still wants to throw her into a ditch.”

“Choose a soft ditch,” Tavra muttered.

A few mouths tightened. Not laughter, but room enough for breath.

Hessa continued. “We opened because riders came last week asking which of us could still keep a rehearsal count. They offered arrears. Then workers at Jalen sent the dates. Your evidence gives the dates their purpose.”

Ossa touched the inner rim of her drum. The erased command cuts remained under her fingertips. “There is something your evidence needs.”

Tavra’s head turned sharply. She knew what Ossa intended. That knowledge almost stopped her.

Ossa carried the drum to the center of the cellar. “Seven years ago, at Hadrun, I held cadence for the imperial forced march.”

Brin’s cup struck the table. Seni put one hand over his before he could rise.

Ossa did not soften the words. “Refugees occupied a ridge marked for a grain road. The order said move them east. The ground had turned south during the night. We marched anyway.”

“You marched people?” Hessa asked.

“Trained road crews. The command cadence took their practiced response before fear could break it. They drove the ridge. The civilians had nowhere level to go.”

Her brother’s face did not come to her whole. Pel returned as details: a damp curl against his temple; his left thumb taping the drum rim before every parade; the foolish red laces he wore because regulation dye bored him. The ravine had taken those details apart.

“My younger brother, Pel, served in the third line. He heard the ground slipping and left his mark to pull two children clear. I struck the recall. Then I struck command again when the captain ordered me to recover the line.”

Silence reached no clean count.

“How many?” Seni asked.

“Forty-one civilians found. Nine workers. More remained beneath the ridge. Pel remained there.”

“And you deserted,” Brin said.

“Afterward.”

“Convenient.”

“Yes.”

He stood with Seni’s help. His left ankle could not flatten, forcing his weight onto the outside edge. “My daughter carried that grain road for five years after your company made it. You let her train on ground packed with bodies.”

“I did.”

Tavra started to speak. Ossa held up a hand without looking at her.

Brin came close enough that Ossa smelled broth on his breath. “Did you know?”

“Not her name. I knew companies maintained the road. I chose not to learn who.”

His palm struck her cheek. The blow had little force. His balance cost him more. Ossa could have caught his elbow before he stumbled, but Seni reached him first.

“No command from you,” Brin said. “Not here.”

“None.”

“Not even a clever broken beat that lets us pretend your choice belongs to us.”

“None.”

She returned to the wall and sat on the floor beside her stripped drum. Shame often tempted movement: flee, confess faster, offer a plan, make usefulness purchase permission to remain. She kept still while the room decided whether to expel her.

The veterans argued for nearly an hour.

Some wanted the evidence carried directly to civil courts. Hessa answered that Chancellor Miren’s clerks had redirected injury petitions for eleven years. Others proposed breaking anchor stones. Tavra warned that an unscored break could leave stored torque in every dancer attached to the field. Brin wanted to warn active crews and let them choose. Seni asked how warning mattered when refusal cancelled grain rights and pension years.

Ossa said nothing until Hessa addressed her.

“You know command schedules. If a company delays rehearsal without breaking its anchor, what happens?”

“Depends on the delay.”

“That sounded like the beginning of an order.”

Ossa placed both palms on the floor. “Then ask a narrower question.”

Hessa considered. “How much time can one crew steal?”

“If the first center reports an uneven salt line, inspectors grant half a day. A drummer who loses tension can claim another quarter. Anchor survey takes longer if three witnesses dispute the local stone.”

Brin stared down at the map. “Different excuses.”

“Different choices,” Ossa said. “A repeated failure exposes coordination. Separate defects look like ordinary road work.”

“A choir of bent ankles,” Seni said, “each missing a different note.”

This time the room did laugh, quietly and without kindness.

Tavra shifted the heated brick under her thigh. “Delay does not stop the Measure.”

“No,” Hessa replied. “It gives you time to reach the archive. It gives active crews time to see these names.” She tapped the copied pension ledger. “After that, they decide.”

“You risk every pension in this cellar.”

“We already spent our bodies earning promises the College edits after death.”

Ruhan bent over the schedule. “The east wall reaches its inward step tomorrow near dawn. If we enter with the refuse carts, the wall carries us toward the archive quarter.”

“Gates inspect carts,” Tavra said.

“Gates do,” Hessa answered. “A moving wall has other habits.”

She brought out a mason’s rubbing. It showed an old relief: a stair, six hands raised beneath a broken cliff, and a thin boy on the highest step.

“College workers receive this story before their first center training,” she said. “Oris. Sixty-three years ago the town moved under winter rock. Six hundred dead. Serad Vale survived on a temple stair. He tells apprentices that unheld ground killed his family because nobody accepted authorship.”

Ossa had heard shortened versions in barracks: Oris as warning, Serad as the boy who learned to keep roads faithful. “He believes it.”

“Belief can build an honest grave,” Hessa said. “He took a disaster and made it permission.”

Tavra studied the rubbing. “He showed me the stair after Nareth. Told me my failure proved the ground punishes hesitation.”

“What did you tell him?” Ruhan asked.

“I believed him long enough to leave my daughter in his College.”

The admission landed differently from Ossa’s confession. No blow followed. Hessa rolled up the rubbing and returned it to its tube.

“Oris explains his hands,” she said. “It does not place ours inside them.”

Before dawn, the veterans made their choices one by one. Seni would miscut a replacement heel at the northern rehearsal yard. Brin would visit his daughter and show her Laro Ves’s pension entry. The brothers would file competing claims over an anchor’s quarry origin. Hessa would send no central instruction. She marked each volunteered delay on separate scraps, then burned the list after everyone had memorized only their own part.

Ossa watched discipline take shape without cadence. It looked untidy. People repeated questions. Two withdrew. One changed from sabotage to warning. Nobody matched breath.

Hessa gave Ruhan a clay token stamped with a mason’s open palm. “Show this at the washerwomen’s shrine inside the east wall. Leave the Nareth score there if pursuit closes. The shrine moves with the old masonry, not the gate schedule.”

Ruhan tucked the token into his left sleeve. “Who retrieves it?”

“The wall decides where the shrine passes, but washerwomen decide who reaches inside.”

Tavra tried to stand. Her left leg buckled. Ossa moved, then stopped before touching her.

“May I?”

Tavra’s annoyance flared. Beneath it, something loosened. “Under the right arm. Don’t pull the knee.”

Together they raised her. Ruhan shouldered the evidence bag with his sound hand. The Nareth fragment remained wrapped against his ribs, outside Tavra’s reach by her own prior choice.

At the cellar door, Brin blocked Ossa. Morning had thinned the dark behind him.

“I do not forgive you,” he said.

“I didn’t ask.”

“You came here intending to use us.”

“Yes.”

“Are you still?”

Ossa listened to Seni file a heel in the rear room, each stroke deliberately uneven. “No. I am going where you gave us leave to go.”

Brin stepped aside.

The fugitives entered the grey road toward Meren. Behind them, twenty-three damaged workers began missing twenty-three different beats, and not one belonged to Ossa.

Chapter 12 — The Capital’s Moving Walls

The eastern wall took its hidden step while Eda stood beneath it.

Mortar whispered above the gatehouse. A carved lion shifted half a thumb-width out of alignment with its paws. Six apprentices in grey rehearsal coats bent together, soles grinding into salted boards, and forced the stone back before the waiting dignitaries could notice.

Eda noticed. She felt the correction through the platform under her right foot: a lateral pull, a pause, then counter-motion driven down through six young bodies. The smallest apprentice trembled at the knees.

Master Chorearch Serad Vale continued his welcome to the provincial delegation. “Meren has held this approach through three reigns. What moves beyond our walls need not disturb the grain scales within them.”

Polite hands struck polite palms. Eda kept hers at her sides.

The capital had never seemed fixed to her. As a child, she had learned its secret corrections by sleeping against College floors. The north wall leaned inward during wet months. Western stairs added a shallow rise every seventh morning. The east gate preferred to travel south whenever the distant chalk country turned. College measures cancelled these motions before citizens reached breakfast.

Yet the correction beneath her carried twice its usual force.

The smallest apprentice lost her heel mark. Another caught the shift, taking extra weight through his hip. His mouth opened without sound.

Eda stepped away from Serad’s line of honor and onto the salted board.

“Center,” the supervising cutter hissed. “Return to position.”

“Your fourth brace has no exit channel.” She pointed to the apprentice who had caught the load. “Release him on my count.”

“We are under display order.”

“Then display competence.”

Eda set her left sole beside the damaged mark. She did not enter center. She offered a short outer brace, enough to divide the pull while the cutter carved a release notch with shaking hands.

“One,” she said. “Weight inward. Two. Fourth brace, lift. Three. Let the wall have the finger-width.”

Stone moved overhead. The lion’s shoulder separated from its painted outline. Several dignitaries looked up.

The apprentice sagged into his neighbor’s arms. Eda felt the wall settle in its preferred place, no longer pretending perfect alignment.

Serad finished his speech without raising his voice. When the delegation passed under the gate, he came to stand beside her.

“You exposed a visible deviation,” he said.

“I prevented a broken hip.”

“The relief will be reset tonight.”

“By different apprentices?”

“By a rested company.” His gaze went to her improvised brace mark. Genuine approval warmed it, which made her anger harder to keep simple. “Clean judgment. You felt the secondary pull before I did.”

“You were speaking.”

“I was watching you choose whether to intervene.”

The answer could have meant trust. It could also have meant a test built from somebody else’s joints.

Eda stepped off the board. “Why has the east correction doubled?”

“Worker disruptions on the outer holds. Nothing dangerous.”

“The wall moved against six centers.”

“Six braces. The designated center stood inside the gate.”

The apprentices carried a seventh dancer past on a litter. His bare feet had gone grey to the ankle.

Serad watched Eda watch him. “Counter-motion stains. He will recover.”

“Will he?”

“The infirmary has treated worse.”

That reply did not answer her question. Eda had spent sixteen years understanding the difference between assurance and measure.

A civil secretary arrived with Chancellor Miren’s ivory seal. Serad glanced toward the gate, where masons already hung canvas over the displaced lion.

“Miren wants both of us,” he said. “She has received news from Jalen, and Tavra has crossed the capital boundary.”

Her mother’s name still found the old hollow under Eda’s ribs. She stepped around it.

“Then we should not keep the chancellor waiting.”

Meren built its government on a hill that migrated in a narrow circle beneath the city. The Still Court crowned its upper shelf; ministries occupied terraces below. Porters adjusted ramps each morning while official plans continued to show straight stairs. Eda climbed beside Serad and counted fresh wedges hammered under the civil archive.

At the second terrace, a survey crew had opened the pavement around an older foundation. The exposed stones leaned west in a graceful course that no imperial mason would have designed. Painted measure marks crossed them in layers: red from the previous reign, black from Serad’s first wall stabilization, fresh white from this week.

Eda stopped to read the cuts. “This foundation has shifted six times since spring.”

Serad did not slow. “Minor corrections.”

“Each correction crosses the last one.”

“Which proves the necessity of consolidation.”

It proved that stone continued to move beneath every claim of completion. Eda crouched beside the trench. A mason had chalked a private warning on the lowest block: LEFT FOOT SLIPS AT THIRD COUNT. The official score assigned no dancer to that position.

A foreman recognized her coat and hurried over. “Center, the trench has not been cleared for inspection.”

“Who wrote this warning?”

He looked toward Serad’s retreating back. “A wall tender.”

“Name.”

“Lemi Sarn.”

“Where is she?”

“Infirmary. The block took her foot yesterday.”

Eda traced the crossing measures without touching them. Every new hold forced the next correction into less accommodating stone. The capital’s hidden labor did not preserve one fixed arrangement. It accumulated restraint until workers had to drive each change through the scars of older changes.

“Why not release this course and rebuild the terrace one stride west?” she asked.

The foreman stared as though she had suggested moving the palace into a ditch. “Property seals follow the current wall.”

“Stairs can turn.”

“Tax rooms cannot.”

Behind them, a porter dropped a wedge under the archive ramp. The moving wall beside him travelled a hair’s breadth and pinched the wood hard enough to split it. He swore, replaced the wedge, then painted over the crack before an official could see.

Eda rose. “Send me Lemi Sarn’s injury report.”

“To the Fifth Measure?”

“To me.”

The foreman bowed without promising. By the time Eda caught Serad at the next stair, two clerks had covered the chalk warning with a clean slab.

“You delay for local defects while the road web approaches disorder,” Serad said.

“Local defects carry names.”

“And leadership carries scale. You will need both if you mean to surpass me.”

He intended the words as confidence in her. Once, that confidence could have lifted her through a week of bruised rehearsals. Today it felt like another score laid beneath her feet, flattering in the direction it allowed.

They crossed the terrace as a file of laborers hauled the split ramp away. A delivery cart waited below because the new angle no longer met its wheels. Citizens flowed around it with the practiced irritation of people trained to call constant adjustment stillness.

Chancellor Miren waited in a receiving room whose windows had been bricked twice and reopened twice as the wall shifted. She stood over a table crowded with grain tallies. Her silver hair had escaped its pins.

“Three rehearsal companies have reported defective salt,” Miren said before greeting them. “Two anchor surveys dispute quarry origin. A drummer at north Jalen claims damp skin after nine dry days.”

“Worker mischief,” Serad replied.

“Mischief that has delayed southern grain guarantees.”

“Replace the crews.”

“With whom? Retired centers are refusing recall. If I strip active road maintenance, market routes will wander before your convocation.” Miren turned to Eda. “Your mother has help among company veterans.”

Eda examined the tally nearest her. The delayed routes did not yet show shortage, only uncertainty. Red circles marked contracts that would fail if roads shifted beyond agreed toll stations.

“What evidence did she show them?” Eda asked.

Miren’s mouth thinned. “Pension complaints from Nareth. Dead workers acquire remarkable political usefulness when someone needs a crowd.”

“Were the complaints false?”

“Authenticity does not make them relevant to present route stability.”

Serad moved one grain marker back into line. “Tavra uses old grief to disrupt a measure she has not read.”

Eda thought of the apprentice’s grey feet. “You could publish the full injury allowances.”

Miren gave a dry breath. “And invite every road company to calculate private risk while the continent changes under them?”

“That sounds like asking them to know what they carry.”

“It sounds like government ceasing while thousands bargain with weather.” Miren’s finger pressed on the Hadrun tally. “Two missed deliveries empty capital reserve bins within sixteen days. I cannot feed districts on moral clarity.”

Eda understood that arithmetic. She did not accept the missing column.

“What happens to Hadrun if its roads hold beyond their seasonal release?”

Miren looked to Serad. He answered with care. “Local strain. We have designed spill measures.”

“Where does the spill go?”

“Into prepared centers.”

That answer matched the College doctrine she had mastered. It also matched the grey feet on the litter.

A wall bell sounded three uneven notes. Servants caught the picture frames before the room shifted north. Miren braced the grain table with both hands. Nobody commented on the movement.

Serad waited until the floor settled. “Accelerate eastern and southern rehearsals. Full company tomorrow.”

“Stillday remains fourteen days away,” Miren said.

“Worker delays make gradual preparation unreliable.”

Eda looked at him. “You want my Fifth Measure on the east field.”

“At dawn. Then southern transfer.”

She heard the unspoken sum: Tavra near the wall, workers delaying, time narrowing. “I need Folio Seven tonight.”

“You have its assigned sections.”

“The east field touches two anchor lines. I need the center transitions.”

His expression softened into the patience he had used when she was thirteen and furious over a failed turn. “After rehearsal. Not before.”

Miren dismissed them to separate duties. Eda left by the service stair rather than descend beside Serad. Halfway down, the stair carried her east.

The movement came gently, one old block sliding against another. A seam opened behind a hanging prayer cloth. Cool air touched her wrist.

Eda stopped.

The College listed no shrine in this wall. The cloth showed washerwomen raising wet sheets, its blue pigment polished by generations of fingers. Behind it, a narrow cavity moved with the masonry. A clay lamp burned inside.

Someone breathed beyond the opening.

Eda entered sideways. The passage turned twice and widened into a chamber no larger than a laundry stall. Water bowls lined one shelf. Offerings of buttons, soap, and broken shoe buckles crowded another. A man stood at the far wall with a wrapped strip of leather in his left hand. His right wrist lay splinted against his chest.

She recognized him from Kesta: Ruhan Tal, the pass-runner who had guided Tavra beyond the survey lines.

He recognized her at the same moment. His left hand dropped toward his belt knife.

“You would lose that exchange,” Eda said.

“I have lost exchanges on better ground.”

“The wall carries this room toward a guard post in four minutes.”

“Then we should be quick.”

His route calm irritated her because it declined the fear her uniform deserved. She saw mud dried to his hem, exhaustion around his eyes, and a mason’s clay token on the shelf.

“What are you hiding?”

“Something your wall has agreed to keep.”

“Walls do not agree.”

“Washerwomen do. This wall shrine belongs to their route.”

He slid the wrapped leather behind a loose votive stone. Eda crossed the chamber before he could replace it. She caught his left forearm, turned him toward the wall, and took the bundle. He did not draw his knife. With only one working hand, he could not safely contest her without dropping both balance and secrecy.

The wrapping smelled of cellar earth. Inside lay a torn sole-score, dark with old oil. Nareth marks ran across it.

Eda’s grip loosened.

Tavra’s trial records included diagrams of the failed holding, but none carried the original cuts. This fragment showed a release turn severed by a later command incision. The official copy had presented that turn as Tavra’s unauthorized alteration. Here, the older leather told a different sequence: release first, imperial override crossing it afterward.

“Nareth score,” Ruhan said. “Your mother kept the fragment.”

“She kept evidence while letting the College call her a coward.”

“She kept evidence while hiding from you. Those choices can both condemn her.”

Eda looked up sharply. She had expected defense, perhaps worship of the fugitive cutter who turned roads. Ruhan offered neither.

“Why give it to you?”

“She could no longer trust herself not to trade it for your safety.”

The words struck harder than an accusation. They sounded exactly like Tavra: seeing control clearly only after arranging everyone around it.

Footsteps entered the outer passage.

Ruhan reached for the fragment. Eda kept it.

Through a mortar gap she saw two College guards stop beside the prayer cloth. One carried a hook for searching wall cavities. The shrine shifted another handspan toward them.

“Dancer?” called the nearer guard. “Did you enter there?”

Eda rolled the fragment once, memorizing the broken release and the command cut. She returned it to its cloth, placed it behind the votive stone, and pushed Ruhan into the shallow recess beside the water bowls.

“I dropped a hip clasp,” she called. “Unless you wish to explain searching me to Master Vale, continue your wall check elsewhere.”

The hook scratched the opening.

“Apologies, center.”

Boots withdrew. Eda waited through twenty breaths. Ruhan remained close enough that his splint pressed her sleeve, but he did not mistake concealment for trust.

“You read enough,” he said when the wall carried them away from the guards.

“Enough to know the trial copy lied about sequence.”

“Enough to let us pass?”

“I let this shrine keep what belongs to Nareth. I have not joined Tavra.”

“No one asked you to.”

That irritated her more.

He took the mason’s token and left the score hidden. “The wall returns here near sunset. Washerwomen will move the fragment deeper before then.”

“You are walking away from it?”

“Custody does not always require a pocket.” He glanced at her uniform shoes. “You might tell your College that.”

Eda stepped aside. “The southern drain opens when this passage turns. Avoid the archive stair. Serad doubled its guard.”

Ruhan paused. “Why?”

“Because the original score contradicts his record.”

“That did not answer me.”

“No. It is all you get.”

He disappeared with the next shift, leaving the Nareth fragment behind the votive stone. Eda remained until the passage opened onto a laundry court.

An elderly washerwoman waited on the other side with wet sleeves rolled above her elbows. She neither bowed to Eda’s coat nor looked surprised by the hidden door.

“You frightened my runner,” she said.

“He drew no weapon.”

“He knows better than to cut someone in a room full of soap. Blood never leaves linen honestly.” She handed Eda a basket of damp uniforms. “Carry these if you intend to stand in my court looking official.”

Eda accepted the basket. “The shrine holds evidence from Nareth.”

“The shrine holds buckles, prayers, two unpaid laundry chits, and whatever else women put there while walls pass useful places.”

“Guards may search it.”

“Guards search fixed holes. Ours moves.” The washerwoman began pinning shirts to a line. “Your man with the broken wrist says the fragment must reach Hadrun after someone reads it.”

“He does not belong to me.”

“Good. Men carried as property wear out the handles.”

Eda looked back. The opening had already narrowed behind stone. “Can you keep it from College inspection?”

“I can move it among six shrines. I cannot promise a seventh. That honest enough for a center?”

It offered less certainty than a College vault and more truth than Serad had given her all morning. “Yes.”

“Then set those uniforms by the blue vat. When the wall reaches the dyers, someone will carry your evidence out in a soap barrel.”

“Not mine,” Eda said.

The washerwoman glanced at her. “Learning quickly.”

Steam hung above rows of sheets. Across the yard, beyond three moving partitions, Tavra limped between Ossa and a cart stacked with ash barrels. Her left leg stayed nearly straight. Each step pulled her mouth tight. She looked older than she had at Kesta and more dangerous because Eda could now see the cost she had been concealing.

Tavra glanced toward the laundry court.

For one suspended moment, wall gaps aligned. Mother and daughter saw each other through the moving masonry.

Tavra took half a step forward. Eda raised one hand, palm out. Not welcome. Not alarm. A demand that Tavra leave this choice to her.

Ossa touched Tavra’s shoulder. The ash cart rolled on. Stone crossed the sightline.

A College runner found Eda before she reached the Fifth Measure dormitory. “Master Vale orders full east rehearsal at first bell. All center transitions committed from memory. No folios on the field.”

“Tell him I understand.”

Inside her chamber, Folio Seven waited in its locked cedar case. The stitched center section made a narrow ridge under the leather cover. Serad had forbidden her to open it. That morning she had treated the prohibition as a test of discipline. Now it resembled the canvas hung over the shifted lion, a clean surface placed before inconvenient movement.

Eda set her smallest sole knife beside the stitches.

She did not cut yet. First she barred the door, filled the washbasin to conceal the sound, and removed tomorrow’s rehearsal shoes from their rack. Then she placed Folio Seven between them and chose where the first thread would part.

Chapter 13 — The Dance of Open Ground

Tavra cut the first sole beneath a cabbage awning while College bells sealed the common.

The vendor kept selling around her. Copper weights struck the scale pan; a knife thudded through stems; customers argued over bruised leaves while Tavra braced the leather against her good thigh and opened a shallow turning channel. Every sound travelled through the market stones into her left knee.

The common wanted to widen toward the river. Imperial holds had denied that movement for eighty years. Pressure gathered under the western stalls, where paving blocks had begun climbing over one another like badly stacked plates. A conventional measure would drive the plaza flat and send the counter-motion through a trained center. Tavra had no center, no licensed company, and no right to spend the vendors’ bodies.

She had forty minutes before the search closed every lane.

“Again,” she told the cabbage seller. “Show me how you move the full basket.”

The woman, Yali, planted her left foot and swung a reed basket from hip to scale. Her right foot followed without taking weight.

“You do that all morning?”

“Until cabbages learn to walk themselves.”

“Any old injury in the left ankle? Stone staining? Pain after the market shifts?”

Yali’s suspicion deepened. “You buying vegetables or feet?”

“Neither. I need one step you already take. I will tell you the cost before I ask you to take it.”

Ossa stood at the awning edge with the stripped frame of her drum under one arm. Without its outer skin she could make only a dry rim knock, too weak to command and useful precisely for that reason. Ruhan watched the north arcade, his splinted right wrist pinned inside his coat. Ash from their cart streaked his cheek.

“Riders at the spice lane,” he said. “Four, perhaps six behind.”

“Then buy something fragrant,” Tavra replied. “You look conspicuous when you merely lurk.”

He came back with a fist of cloves he did not want.

Tavra turned the sole toward Yali. “This cut lets your basket swing take a finger-width of rise from the western stones and return it when you set the basket down. You may feel a pull in the calf. If the pull reaches the knee, stop. You take it once. No repetition.”

“What happens if I refuse?”

“We find another route. The riders catch us sooner. Your stall continues leaning until the city company resets the common.”

“So refusal may get you caught.”

“Yes.”

“And you will not call that my fault?”

“No.”

Yali wiped her knife. “Put it on.”

The altered sole strapped over her market shoe. Tavra marked the stone beside the scale with a pinch of step-salt, charcoal, and oil. Rain had not touched the capital for two days, but market washwater had already blurred old licensing lines. She left the new mark open on its river side.

At the next stall, a potter used a backward heel press to stop his wheel. A fishmonger shifted a tub by taking weight through the outside of her right foot. Two porters lowered bales with matching movements, but Tavra accepted only one of them. Matching bodies tempted matching demands. The other porter swore at her and left.

She explained each cut separately. Direction. Limit. Likely strain. Stop signal. Exit.

Some vendors refused after hearing the risk. One agreed until his daughter objected, then removed the trial sole. A flour seller wanted payment. Ossa gave her the last silver College button cut from the inner drum skin. Nobody received a lecture about courage.

Tavra’s knee burned as she crouched. Pale mineral lines had climbed above the kneecap during the night. She hid none of it. When people asked what ground-dance could do to a body, she pulled up her trouser leg.

“This,” she said. “At a scale far beyond one market step, held too long and released too late. Your cut cannot take that load unless I lie about the boundary. Look at the salt. If anyone closes the river gap, step out.”

A bread seller named Omel studied the marks. “Who receives what we return?”

“The ground does, if I cut the exits correctly.”

“If you do not?”

“The western participants take bruising. I take the central spill through this knee.”

“That sounds like a center.”

“It is a last fault, not a design.”

Ossa heard the evasion. Her mouth flattened.

Tavra amended it. “I do not know whether a fault will open. This has worked across one road turn, not a plaza. I believe the separate steps keep the load small. Belief does not make it safe.”

Omel nodded once. “Now I believe your warning.”

At the center of the common, the imperial anchor rested beneath a bronze fountain. Tavra did not touch it. She salted a loose crescent around the fountain and left three gaps wide enough for a handcart. The trial needed local stone to answer, but binding the civic anchor would alert every College dancer in Meren.

Ruhan unfolded a scrap of his atlas on a fruit crate. The page showed no stable streets, only yesterday’s wall movements and places where residents had refused passage. “If the common widens, the south arcade narrows.”

“How much?” Tavra asked.

“Enough to trap a loaded cart, not a person. Unless the dyers’ wall arrives early.”

“Can you keep that lane empty?”

“I can ask. I cannot keep.”

“Ask.”

He went toward the dyers, cloves perfuming his escape.

Ossa tapped the drum rim twice, spaced too far apart for trained feet to mistake as command. “Search at west lane.”

Tavra counted participants. Eleven. Too few for the common’s full breadth, enough to test the western rise. Each wore one added sole. No two cuts matched. A flower seller’s toe pivot angled pressure toward the open salt. Yali’s basket swing would meet it without mirroring. The potter’s heel stop lay third, but only if he chose to take it after seeing the first stones move.

Tavra climbed onto the fountain lip. Her left leg could not bend enough to sit. “Listen before you decide. One simple step each. Take only the movement we agreed. If your mark cracks, do nothing. If another person falls, do not repeat your step to help. Ossa will knock once to begin and then make no count. You may leave now or after any movement.”

A butcher unstrapped his sole and walked away. College bells sounded from the spice lane.

Ten remained.

“This is not a hold,” Tavra said. “We let the common move a little and return what it cannot carry. We are crossing it, not curing it.”

“Your speech is longer than my step,” Yali called.

“Most honest warnings are.”

A rider entered the west lane. Behind him, two civil watchmen tried to keep customers from surging toward the narrow exits, but neither dared cross Tavra’s fresh salt. The crowd had already begun making its own routes between stalls.

Ossa struck the rim once.

Yali swung her cabbage basket.

Her left sole pressed the cut. The paving block beneath her dipped, not down but riverward, passing its stored rise into the open line. She set the basket by the scale. The stone eased half a finger-width and stopped.

Nobody else moved.

Tavra felt the plaza waiting through her bad knee. Not alive in thought. Responsive in weight, carrying old directions beneath imposed flatness.

The potter chose his heel press. His wheel slowed. Three blocks turned around the fountain, opening a seam that breathed dust.

The rider shouted.

The fishmonger put her shoulder to the tub and took her outside-foot step. Water slopped silver over the paving. A ridge travelled under two stalls, low enough that cups rattled but did not fall.

Participants acted from their own work, not Tavra’s count. A porter lowered his bale. Omel leaned into the oven peel. The flower seller pivoted away from a customer. Between movements came uncertain pauses that no licensed company would tolerate. Those pauses saved them. Stone settled, offered another fraction of torque, and found a different foot.

The rider urged his horse onto the common. Its front shoes struck the loose crescent and skidded. He dragged the reins hard, turning the animal before it fell.

“Illegal measure!” he called. “Clear the field!”

“Already clearing,” Yali said, and handed a cabbage to a customer.

A second rider reached the fountain from the north. Behind him marched College braces in blue-soled shoes, trained to flatten any local deviation. Tavra saw their intended route in the set of their shoulders. If they entered together, they would force every loose block back into imperial alignment and pour the common’s released pressure into the nearest active cuts: the vendors.

“Ossa, no second beat.”

“I heard your rules.”

Tavra jumped down. Her knee failed on impact. Pain drove white through her vision. She caught the fountain basin and stayed upright.

“Those who stepped, remove the added soles,” she called. “Now. Trial finished.”

The flower seller fumbled her strap. Ossa went to help, then held out the knife handle and let the woman cut it herself. Yali kicked free of her sole before the first brace crossed the salt.

A College officer pointed at Tavra. “Take the cutter. Hold the common.”

His braces entered on a command count.

Their first unified step struck the plaza like a lid. The western stones drove back against their climb. Pressure ran toward the fountain and split around Tavra’s open gaps. Two streams escaped beneath empty handcart lanes. A third met a salt clot where spilled fish water had gathered charcoal into paste.

The clot closed the release.

Counter-motion hit Tavra’s left knee.

She dropped to both hands. Mineral cold shot into her thigh. The common tilted riverward by the width of a fingernail, then stuck between the vendors’ partial release and the College hold.

Ossa grabbed her coat. “Can you stand?”

“Wrong question.” Tavra forced air into her lungs. “Break the fish-water clot.”

“With what?”

“Your heel. No score. Scuff it, don’t step through.”

Ossa looked at the College braces closing. “Cost?”

“Bruised heel if the pressure catches. Broken ankle if you cross the line.”

That answer took a heartbeat they did not own. Ossa chose. She dragged her heel through the wet salt and jumped back.

The release opened. Stone flexed under the advancing braces. Their shared alignment lost its flat reference. One dancer lifted early; another compensated; the command count frayed without Ossa striking anything.

Tavra hauled herself upright by the fountain. “Participants out by the south arcade. Do not take another step from the trial. Ordinary walking only.”

Yali supported Omel. The potter abandoned his wheel. Ten vendors left by routes they chose, carrying no obligation to save the fugitives.

Tavra stayed long enough to watch the last added sole come free. A strap had tightened around Omel’s swollen instep, and Yali’s cabbage knife could not reach it without cutting skin. Tavra opened the leather from the outer edge while the baker gripped the fountain lip.

“Calf?” she asked.

“Pulled.”

“Knee?”

“Mine, still.”

“Any cold under the bone?”

“No.” Omel watched the College braces struggle across the loosened paving. “Was that success?”

“The plaza moved. You remained standing. The release nearly closed and my knee took the spill. Call it a useful warning.”

“You promised a crossing, not a victory.”

“I promised risk. I hoped for victory.”

“That hope belongs on the warning next time.”

Tavra cut the last strap. “Yes.”

Omel tested his bare market shoe and limped once toward the arcade. Yali took his oven peel in addition to her basket. Neither waited for Tavra’s thanks.

A child crouched beside the discarded trial soles, reaching for the flower seller’s scored leather. Tavra swept them all into a produce sack.

“Why can’t I try?” the child demanded.

“Because these cuts belong to other feet on ground that has already changed.”

“My feet are better.”

“Almost certainly. They are still not hers.” Tavra handed the sack to a refuse collector sheltering beneath the fountain. “Put these in your stove. Do not sell them.”

The collector weighed the leather. “Good goat.”

“Bad fire if College catches you with it.”

He tucked the sack under ash. That destroyed Tavra’s only record of the experiment. Her craft instinct recoiled. A master cutter kept patterns, marked corrections, built improvement from repeatable cuts. Yet a copied sole might recruit an absent body into a second trial without warning. She memorized principles instead: separate work movements, open edge, single use, no shared count. The precise cuts could die.

College braces spread in pairs across the common. Their officer had learned from the first unified failure. Two dancers approached each raised seam from opposing angles, reducing the plaza section by section. It worked. Every forced flattening sent a narrow tremor through nearby stall legs.

“They will put it all back,” Ossa said.

“For today.”

“And load their centers doing it.”

Tavra watched one brace’s lips pale with each correction. The experiment had shifted cost, not abolished it. By leaving, she preserved the volunteers and abandoned trained dancers still caught under orders. Every route out carried somebody’s body behind it.

She approached the nearest open salt gap and drove her knife into the charcoal paste. One short cut toward the river widened the exit without forming a new score. The next College correction bled part of its pressure away. A fish stall settled crooked rather than flat.

The officer saw her. “She is altering the field!”

Three braces broke toward the fountain.

“That purchased perhaps two corrections,” Tavra said.

Ossa caught her under the arm. “Then spend them moving.”

They hauled Tavra behind a row of hanging rugs as College braces recovered their count. The fabric slapped their faces, carrying dye and market dust. A cutter’s hook tore through the rug one handspan behind Tavra’s shoulder.

At the rug seller’s bench, an old man sat calmly restringing his awning. “Your plaza rise snapped my red line,” he told Tavra.

“I’m sorry.”

“Sorry does poor knot work.” He tossed her a cord. “Pull.”

She looped it around her wrist and leaned while Ossa secured the upper spar. The delay cost ten breaths. It restored the awning over two customers who had never agreed to a measure and still had to live with its result.

“Enough,” the seller said. “Now run badly somewhere else.”

Tavra obeyed.

Ruhan appeared atop a dyer’s cart. “South lane clear. East wall arrives in nine minutes.”

“Then we need eight.”

“You need a knee.”

“I misplaced one years ago.”

He jumped down, landing on his left hand and boots while protecting the splint. Together he and Ossa took Tavra under the arms. They moved behind a row of hanging rugs as College braces recovered their count.

The common no longer lay flat enough for cavalry. The loosened paving had formed shallow ramps between stalls. Vendors who had refused the trial continued trading from these new levels, shouting prices over the pursuit. None joined the measure. None needed to. Their carts and awnings turned the changed ground into obstacles.

Tavra cut left behind a spice booth. A rider reached for her collar. Ruhan threw cloves into the horse’s face. The animal sneezed and shied, carrying its furious rider into a display of copper pans.

“Worth the price,” Ruhan said.

They crossed the pottery row. Behind them, the College officer ordered his braces to hold every seam at once. A center screamed as the common transferred three generations of denied movement into his shin.

Tavra stopped.

Ossa pulled her forward. “He entered under command.”

“He still has a leg.”

“You cannot release their measure from here.”

“I can cut the anchor spill.”

“You cannot reach the anchor.”

Tavra hated her for being right. The fountain stood behind two lines of braces. Any return would recapture the vendors in the field. She let Ossa drag her on.

At the southern arcade, a squad closed the lane. Ruhan turned toward the dyers’ passage, but the moving wall had arrived early. Masonry slid across its mouth.

“We wait for it,” he said.

“We get arrested by it,” Ossa replied.

A blue-soled dancer dropped from the wall stair between them and the common. Eda landed with her weight perfectly divided, Folio case absent, right hand empty.

Tavra’s body recognized her daughter’s balance before her mind found speech.

“Go,” Eda said to Ossa and Ruhan.

Ossa shifted her grip on Tavra. “Unlikely.”

“The officer wants Tavra. The dyers’ wall opens a drain in thirty breaths. Take it when it does.”

Tavra looked at the squad behind Eda. “You are giving them a route.”

“I am giving him one.” Eda nodded to Ruhan. “You stay.”

Ruhan read the wall, then Tavra. “Your choice.”

That phrase hurt more than the knee. Tavra removed her arm from his shoulder. “Take Ossa. Get the pension copies south.”

Ossa’s expression promised a later quarrel. She obeyed now because the evidence mattered beyond Tavra. They slipped between rugs as the wall opened a dark drainage slot.

Eda faced the College squad. “Search the common. The fugitive drummer entered the drain.”

“What about Ilen?” the captain asked.

“I will bring her.”

Her rank carried them away. When the last blue sole vanished, Eda seized Tavra’s coat and pulled her into an alcove formed by the moving wall.

“Do not mistake that for rescue,” she said.

“I wouldn’t dare.”

“You always dare.”

The wall carried them east, hidden for perhaps a minute. Its movement pressed Tavra’s shoulder against warm brick. Eda stood close enough that Tavra could see a twitch beneath her right eye, the sign that had appeared whenever a childhood exercise frightened her and she refused to admit it.

“You let Ruhan leave the shrine,” Tavra said.

“He showed me the Nareth fragment.”

Tavra’s pulse changed. “Then you saw the release.”

“I saw an imperial cut laid across it. I did not see twelve years of context. Give me that without deciding what I should do afterward.”

The demand left no place for a protective lie.

Tavra put her weight against the wall. “Serad ordered the Nareth road held while the hillside had already begun to migrate. My first score included a release toward the empty quarry. His command revision crossed it and sent us forward. I felt the change before the third count.”

“You continued.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“I believed breaking company would collapse the city road. I believed Serad had information I lacked. I believed skill meant carrying doubt without showing it.”

“Houses went under.”

“A hillside district. The official count named eighty-six dead. Residents said more seasonal workers lived beyond the census.”

Eda’s jaw worked once. “And your knees?”

“Took the road’s lateral counter-motion after the hillside slipped. I cut the release too late. It saved part of the lower street and buried the rest faster.”

“You kept the score.”

“A fragment. Enough to prove sequence, not enough to clear me.”

“Then you ran.”

“Yes.”

“From Serad?”

Tavra made herself answer the sharper truth. “From you. I looked at ten-year-old Eda and saw every company that might use her to reach me. Serad offered College custody and protection. I told myself leaving removed the danger.”

“You never asked what I wanted.”

“No.”

“You never wrote the truth.”

“No.”

“You let me build my life around your silence.”

“Yes.”

Eda stepped back. The alcove narrowed around them as the wall continued its route. “Do not make agreement from my anger.”

Tavra closed her mouth.

At last Eda asked, “What did you do on the common?”

“A limited release. Local work steps, each separately cut. Ten participants. Nobody repeated.”

“You used untrained people.”

“With disclosure. Some refused. They left.”

“And if your spill gap had failed?”

“It nearly did. Fish water closed one line. Counter-motion entered my knee.”

“So you built another plan where your body pays for every error.”

The accuracy found bone. “I left a fault path.”

“You left yourself beneath it. That differs in wording.”

A guard’s voice sounded beyond the brick. Eda waited until it passed.

“The method let the plaza flex,” Tavra said. “Small scale only. It did not require one trained center.”

“It required you to design every sole.”

“Yes.”

“Then you still owned the choices before anyone stepped.”

Tavra looked toward the moving seam. She had thought disclosure solved command. Yet every participant had entered a shape only she understood. “I don’t know how to cut what I don’t own.”

“Learn.”

The word carried neither pardon nor invitation.

Tavra reached inside her coat and removed a narrow strip of uncut leather. She incised six words with the point of her knife, using the wall as a brace.

NO CENTER WITHOUT AN EXIT.

Eda read it. “A maxim did not save Nareth.”

“I wrote it afterward.”

“Of course you did.”

“I am not asking you to flee with me.”

“You came to Meren to remove me from the Dominion Measure.”

“I came to keep you alive.”

“By choosing my exit.”

Tavra held out the leather. “Then take only the words. Decide whether they matter.”

Eda did not accept the strip. “Say it.”

“No center without an exit.”

The wall opened onto a service yard. Eda stepped into sunlight and pain flashed across her face. Her right hand went to her hip.

Tavra saw the slight catch in her next turn. “You’re hurt.”

“Stress from the common. I intercepted the College hold before I found you.”

“Let me see.”

“No.” Eda lowered her hand. “You do not get my body because you recognize damage.”

The rebuke stopped Tavra more effectively than a guard.

Eda took the leather strip at last and slid it under her wrist cuff. “I return to Serad. I have questions he will answer only if he believes I still choose him.”

“Do you?”

“I choose to read before either of you closes the ground around me.”

She pointed to a refuse stair descending from the yard. “That reaches the river kilns. Ossa will wait two turns beyond the smoke gate. The shrine keeps the Nareth fragment. Do not retrieve it until the washerwomen move it.”

Tavra accepted the route without improving it. “Your hip needs rest.”

“It needs information.”

Eda walked toward the College arcade. The right hip shortened every seventh stride, a stress line hidden from anyone who did not know her measure.

At the top stair, she paused without turning. “The common is still moving.”

Tavra felt it through the injured knee: the plaza easing around the imperial hold, no longer flat, not yet safely released.

“I have to finish it,” she said.

“You have to ask.”

Eda continued uphill.

Tavra descended toward the kilns, where Ossa and Ruhan waited beyond the smoke gate. She would need the vendors who had already paid one step, but she would not ask them for another. The changed common required different feet, different choices, and a release she had not yet designed.

Behind her, market bells resumed at mismatched heights. The plaza had survived its first small freedom. It had also kept the cost of Tavra’s mistakes, and this time she carried the knowledge toward other people instead of hiding it.

Chapter 14 — What the Dominion Measure Costs

The first stitch refused Eda’s knife.

Serad had chosen waxed tendon, not ordinary book thread. Its fibres gripped the blade and drew the leather covers tighter when she pulled. She held Folio Seven against the floor with her left foot, eased pressure off her injured right hip, and sawed through one strand at a time beneath the sound of running water.

Her washbasin overflowed. Water spread across the tiles and carried scraps of black wax under the bed.

At the fourth stitch, the hidden section opened.

No title marked the first page. A foot diagram filled it from edge to edge: Eda’s center position at the Still Court, seven radial anchor paths, and a dense field of counter-motion channels converging on both hips, spine, jaw, and heels. The notation carried Serad’s hand. She knew his cuts as she knew her own balance, each limit drawn with spare confidence, each release tapered to preserve the outer companies.

The center diagram had no release.

Eda turned the page.

A table paired duration with bodily conversion. Eight hours: likely mineral invasion of both femoral joints. Twelve: partial fixation of the lower spine. Twenty: cardiac encasement probable. The planned hold did not end at twenty hours. A notation in Miren’s clerk hand read CONTINUANCE ASSUMED AFTER CIVIL TRANSFER.

Permanent.

Her right hip clenched. She sat before it could fold, keeping the folio above the water.

Serad had told the College the Dominion Measure would secure the principal roads through one unstable season. Public notices promised a convocation hold, followed by staged release. These pages described no release. They treated her death as a timing uncertainty.

She read every line twice.

The next projection concerned companies. Seven anchors required four hundred trained centers and more than six thousand braces, cadence workers, salt crews, and cutters. Best case losses listed twelve hundred lasting joint conversions, three hundred severe mobility impairments, and fewer than ninety immediate deaths. Median projections doubled each figure. A margin note advised pension reserves sufficient for dependants but excluded unlicensed support labor from the estimate.

Eda thought of Lemi Sarn’s foot beneath a foundation block. Auxiliary bodies did not disappear from the ground merely because a table excluded them.

She turned another page and found maps.

Hadrun’s seasonal release valleys appeared under crosshatched grey. Kesta’s recorded anchor orchards carried fixed circles despite having moved beyond those locations. The southern fig country showed spring paths narrowed into straight channels. Beneath each map, soil estimates projected progressive mineral binding.

Three provinces would lose root penetration within six years. Springs would fail earlier on high shelves. Grain output would rise for two fixed seasons, then decline as held soil hardened around old water routes. Serad’s summary called that decline “acceptable regional exhaustion under central reserve mitigation.”

Eda read the phrase until its elegance became obscene.

A packet stitched to the final map held witness extracts. Retired centers described stone cough, fused ankles, mineral lines entering the pelvis years after service. One copied report named Nareth fig roots turned to stone beneath a cellar. The date preceded Tavra’s discovery by four years.

They had known.

Eda spread the provincial maps across her floor and matched their marks to the center table. The damage did not appear as one dramatic collapse. It arrived through ordinary failures spaced far enough apart for officials to rename them. A spring at first produced half its usual water. Fig roots stopped reaching beyond the old salted boundary. A release valley kept one grain road beautifully level while hillside soil above it hardened into plates. Each local report could be blamed on age, poor rain, or improper cultivation. Only the hidden folio placed them in one choreography.

A second packet carried relocation plans. Hadrun households would receive numbered plots beside the new western channels. Kestan families appeared as “unsettled orchard labor” because the imperial survey recognized no moving province. The proposed settlements stood on fixed shelves distant from the anchor orchards that organized Kestan kinship and burial. Administrators had calculated roof tile, grain allowance, and cart capacity. No column measured what happened when a walking country could no longer walk.

Eda found her own name in the center provisions.

EDA ILEN: ceremonial remains subject to survivability. If mineral conversion prevents removal from Still Court, center platform to be sealed and incorporated into civic monument.

A monument. Serad had planned language for her body after the score finished using it.

Below that entry, another hand had proposed delaying public notice of center death until road compliance reached ninety percent. Serad had crossed out the sentence. In the margin he had written: NO FALSE REPORT. HONOR COST OPENLY AFTER COMPLETION.

The correction carried his integrity and its limit. He would not lie once truth could no longer interrupt him.

Eda searched for alternate center names. Three appeared beneath hers, each less suited to simultaneous anchors. Relay projections raised deaths in the outer company. One model divided the work among seven provincial centers but rejected it because “independent release discretion compromises unity.” The objection did not concern bodily impossibility. It concerned seven people retaining seven choices.

Her anger became cold enough to read with.

She copied figures onto scrap in her own shorthand. Four hundred centers. Six thousand supporting workers. Three provinces. Seven anchor fields. She separated confirmed injuries from projected ones and marked every uncertain range. If she carried the evidence beyond the College, she would not turn Serad’s omissions into omissions of her own.

The Nareth witness packet included a letter from a cellar keeper. The writer described digging around a fig tree whose roots rang under the shovel. An inspector had taken samples, closed the cellar, and offered tax forgiveness in exchange for silence. The letter ended with six names of dancers who had coughed pale grit after the old holding. Two names matched the pension copy Ruhan had shown at the shrine.

Eda folded the letter open beside the Nareth foot diagram she remembered. Tavra’s fragment proved sequence. This report proved consequence. Neither absolved Tavra’s obedience or abandonment. Evidence did not need to make her mother innocent in order to make the College guilty.

At the bottom of the packet, Serad had added one sentence: NARETH LOCALIZATION UNRELIABLE; CONTINENTAL HOLD REMOVES REPEATED EDGE STRAIN.

He had looked at fossilized roots and concluded that the answer required a larger boundary.

Not every outcome carried certainty. Serad marked wide ranges, disputed assumptions, routes that might shift less than forecast. He had not falsified the arithmetic inside the hidden folio. He had simply confined it where the bodies affected could not read it.

A knock struck her door.

“Center?” called her chamber attendant. “First bell in half an hour.”

Eda closed the folio over her hand. “I need a hip wrap.”

“Should I call the infirmary?”

“No. Bring linen and heated salt.”

The attendant hesitated, then left.

Eda lifted the page that carried her likely death and held it beside her rehearsal shoe. Its outer sole bore the center sequence she had memorized. Inside, beneath the removable felt, a thin leather shoe lining could take a copy. She needed a rubbing, not words alone. Any accusation Serad made against Tavra could survive paraphrase. His own cuts needed to stand against him.

She dried the floor, closed the stitched section without repairing it, and wrapped Folio Seven in an ordinary rehearsal cloth. Then she went to find him.

Serad waited in the east practice court while the Fifth Measure warmed through ankle turns. Dawn had not reached the lowest windows. Lamp flames leaned whenever the wall corrected behind them.

He saw her shortened right stride at once. “You are injured.”

“Stress line. The common shifted under a College hold.”

“Did Tavra cause it?”

“The company entered an unstable field.”

His concern sharpened. “Sit. I will assign another center for the east rehearsal.”

“No.”

“Eda.”
