She placed the wrapped folio on the scorer’s table and opened it to the hidden center diagram.

Every warm-up step stopped.

Serad dismissed the company with one gesture. “Outer court. Continue turns under Cutter Solen.”

When the doors closed, he looked at the severed tendon. “You opened a restricted section.”

“You planned my corpse in twelve-hour intervals.”

His eyes returned to her face. He neither denied the page nor pretended surprise. “Those projections assume uninterrupted load at median resistance.”

“Your score requires uninterrupted load.”

“The outer holds may settle early.”

“Then why omit release?”

“Because a premature center exit would cast seven anchor loads back through the companies.”

“So I remain until completion.”

“Yes.”

“Define completion.”

He took a breath. “Stable transfer to the civic holds.”

“Permanent transfer.”

The wall shifted. A rack of practice shoes tapped against its pegs.

Serad came around the table. His left hand hovered near her shoulder and stopped before touching. “I intended to tell you after the southern rehearsal.”

“After you had fixed the full sequence into my trained response.”

“After you understood the scale well enough not to mistake fear for judgment.”

“I understand the scale. Four hundred centers. More than six thousand company workers. Median losses above three thousand. Three provinces with roots bound out of soil.”

“You read estimates without their administrative provisions.”

“Pensions?”

“Resettlement. Grain reserve. Water works. Replacement routes for communities whose current practices cannot continue.”

“Current practices.” She put one finger on the Hadrun map. “You mean living there.”

“I mean seasonal releases that endanger every downstream grain contract. I mean orchards wandering beyond winter supply roads. I mean towns waking on the wrong side of a pass.”

“And you mean Eda dying at your center.”

Pain entered his face without spectacle. “I mean preventing Oris from happening in a hundred places at once.”

She had heard the College version since childhood. A boy on a temple stair, snow packed around the bodies below, six hundred dead because no company held the town. Yesterday the story had explained Serad’s hands. Today those hands had drawn her heart inside a table.

“Did your family consent to the ground moving?” she asked.

“No.”

“Then you answer one unchosen disaster with another.”

“I answer repeated disaster with finite cost.” His voice remained level, but his fingers pressed the table edge. “You have crossed held roads all your life. You ate grain carried over bridges that would not exist without centers. Stability already costs bodies. The empire hides too much of that cost, yes. I have fought Miren over pensions and service limits for twenty years. The Dominion Measure ends the repetition.”

“By making the damage permanent.”

“By paying once.”

Serad opened a cabinet beside the practice court and removed a roll of current route notices. He did not choose only dramatic reports. He showed her a village that had lost winter access when a pass turned three days early, a bridge company crushed during an unscheduled bank rise, and grain carts stranded while two provinces disputed which moving road carried an old treaty obligation.

“These arrived this month,” he said. “Forty bridge workers dead. Seven villages on reduced ration. Two border companies armed because their markers no longer meet. Motion has costs whether the College touches it or not.”

Eda read the notices. The seals appeared genuine. Grief did not become false merely because Serad used it.

“Where are the local proposals?” she asked.

“One village requests a seasonal ferry. Another asks for an anchor company it cannot pay. The provinces request judgment against each other.”

“Then fund the ferry.”

“And next season? Another pass, another ferry, another emergency levy. The empire spends its strength chasing ground that will not keep faith.”

“Ground never signed your treaty.”

“People did. Children cannot eat the philosophical distinction.”

She set the notices beside the provincial maps. His evidence showed urgency, not inevitability. Every disaster in his hand became an argument for one permanent answer because he had already discarded answers that left authority divided.

“Your projections count what current motion may kill,” she said. “They reduce what your hold will kill to provisions. That does not make one set of bodies more finite.”

“Who chose the people who pay?”

“I did.”

The directness struck her. Serad did not retreat into committee language. He claimed authorship exactly as his teaching required.

“You chose me,” she said.

“You can hear two anchors at once. No other center can adjust seven transitions with your accuracy.”

“That explains why I can do it. It does not give you my answer.”

“I know you.”

“You trained me.”

“I raised you when Tavra left.”

“And therefore?”

His calm broke at the edge. “Therefore I know you would not abandon thousands to preserve one body, even your own.”

Eda felt the trap close through praise. He loved the part of her that reached for impossible work. He had fed it, disciplined it, given it rooms where her skill mattered. Now he treated that cultivated courage as consent already owed.

“You never asked,” she said.

“I am asking now.”

“No. You are asking after schedules, anchors, public promises, and trained responses make refusal catastrophic.”

“The catastrophe exists whether we discuss it kindly or not.”

“The alternatives in this folio all begin with permanent hold.”

“Because temporary holds have failed.”

“Tavra let a plaza flex yesterday.”

His expression closed. “She recruited market workers into an illegal field and injured you.”

“She disclosed the risk. People refused and walked away.”

“An uncontrolled trial on a common cannot answer continental famine.”

“No. It proves your alternatives are incomplete.”

Serad turned toward the high window. Beyond it, Meren’s roofs stepped down to roads held straight through districts that would otherwise migrate apart. “When I was fourteen, Oris moved three hundred strides before dawn. The temple stair stayed because one old dancer held it alone. She died under me. Every adult who survived spent years arguing which local custom had failed. No one had possessed enough authority to hold the whole town.”

Eda waited.

“I promised never to leave that scale of choice to frightened fragments again,” he said. “You call that arrogance because Tavra has shown you ten vendors and an open salt line. I call it memory with responsibility.”

“Your memory does not own my hips.”

“No.” He faced her again. “The people on these routes do.”

A rehearsal drum sounded in the outer court. Eda’s body prepared for the next turn before thought. She let the trained response begin, then held her heel down. The refusal sent pain along the new stress line.

“What happens if I walk out now?”

Serad answered after too long. “I assign centers in relay. Loss projections rise. Control precision falls. The Measure proceeds.”

There it stood: her choice permitted only if she accepted blame for whoever replaced her.

Eda closed the folio. “Then you do not have a center. You have a hostage who can improve your figures.”

“I have a center who has not yet let anger finish speaking.”

She could denounce him in the outer court. Guards would close the College before she reached Miren. The hidden pages would return to Serad’s vault. Tavra’s fugitive account would compete with an institution holding every road notice. Anger offered a clean exit and a useless one.

Eda eased her weight onto the injured hip until her face went still.

“What provisions exist for Hadrun water?” she asked.

Serad watched the change in her. Hope and caution arrived together. “Three new channels from the western snow shelves. Reserve wells at each district seat.”

“And the centers’ families?”

“Full hereditary pensions, not service review.”

“You will publish the body projections before Stillday.”

“Miren will not.”

“I asked what you will do.”

He looked down at the closed folio. “I will argue for publication after the southern anchors confirm resistance. Premature figures could cause the very refusal that makes them worse.”

Not publication, then. Another later.

Eda nodded as if arithmetic had steadied her. “Postpone the east rehearsal one hour. Wrap my hip. Show me the full center transition tonight. If I enter, I enter knowing the sequence.”

Serad’s shoulders loosened by a fraction. “Agreed.”

He believed skill had reclaimed her from anger. That belief hurt because she had once shared it.

Back in her chamber, Eda locked the door and removed the heated salt wrap. The stress line burned deep in the right hip, narrow now, dangerous under repeated load. She placed thin paper over the hidden page and rubbed charcoal across it with the flat of her thumb. The center cuts emerged in reverse.

She transferred the rubbing onto calfskin shaved nearly transparent. Every line had to remain exact: seven anchor paths, the sealed center, the projected duration table, the three province marks. Sweat darkened her collar. Twice she stopped when corridor steps passed.

The calfskin fit beneath the felt of her left shoe. Eda lifted the shoe lining, spread a film of cobbler’s paste, and pressed the copy flat. She replaced the felt, then walked six careful steps. Nothing shifted.

She repaired Folio Seven with fresh tendon from her score kit. Her knots differed from the originals. Serad might notice if he searched closely. She rubbed black wax into them and scuffed the cover against the floor.

At first bell plus one hour, she entered the east field in full rehearsal shoes.

Serad waited beside the anchor board. “Your hip?”

“Finite,” she said, using his word.

Pain crossed his face again. She let him mistake her cruelty for acceptance.

Eda took center position. Beneath her left foot, the copied page moved with her body. Above it, the official score directed every turn. She bowed to Serad, placed her heel on the opening mark, and began to learn exactly where his measure could be broken.

Part III

Chapter 15 — Across the Backward Mountains

The ledge moved east while Ruhan climbed west.

His left hand held a root no thicker than a cord. His boots scraped shale. The splinted right wrist rested useless against his chest, wrapped in oilcloth against the morning mist. Below him, Ossa pressed her back to the cliff, and Tavra stood on a shelf that had been broad when they reached it.

Now the shelf narrowed under her bad leg.

“Higher,” Tavra called. “There’s a seam above your shoulder.”

“I see it.”

“Then use it.”

“It is travelling toward Meren.”

“So are the riders.”

Ruhan reached anyway. His left fingers caught the seam. The mountain took another slow eastward step. Rock dragged under his nails and pulled his shoulder wide. His broken right wrist struck the cliff.

Pain removed the world.

He dropped to the shelf between Ossa and Tavra. Loose stone followed, ticking against their coats before falling into cloud.

Ossa crouched over him. “Can you move the fingers?”

Ruhan tried. The splint answered with heat and three reluctant twitches.

Tavra looked up the route he had failed to take. “If we cut a brace into the shelf, I can hold it long enough to reach the upper path.”

“No,” Ossa and Ruhan said together.

Tavra scowled. “I was describing an option.”

“You were dressing an order as weather,” Ossa replied.

The backward mountains rose between Meren and Hadrun in three long spines. During late summer they travelled east, opposite anyone fleeing the capital. Imperial maps showed two held passes, both guarded now. Ruhan had chosen an unheld shepherd route recorded six years earlier. The mountain had since folded it upward and carried it half a day from his page.

The obvious response involved climbing faster than the range moved. His wrist had ended that argument.

He sat against the cliff until nausea eased. Then he used his left hand to unfasten the atlas from his pack.

The cover had swollen with rain and travel. Inside, pages overlapped in no proper scale. Charcoal ridges crossed notes from Kestan orchard walkers, Hadrun shepherds, fugitives, tax crews, and one offended beekeeper. Many marks did not describe passage. They described refusal.

NO CATTLE AFTER FIRST FROST.

ASK LERU AT THE RED CAVE; NO ARMED COMPANY.

SOUTH SPINE CLOSED WHILE THE DEAD ARE MOVED.

IMPERIAL SURVEY TURNED BACK HERE.

Between those refusals, Ruhan had recorded permissions with equal care. A widow near the salt caves allowed travellers to use her roof only when they carried news from the east. Hadrun shepherds opened a spring route after lambing and closed it before first ice. Kestan orchard walkers shared a night field with anyone who left no fixed marker. None of these agreements survived as a broad line. Each belonged to a season, a host, and a question asked at arrival.

Tavra tapped a faded arrow. “This one reaches the western descent.”

“It reached it four years ago for a funeral party.”

“Could we use the same cut?”

“There was no cut. The village stopped its release dance for one hour while the mourners crossed.”

“Why record it if nobody can repeat it?”

“So the next traveller knows whom to ask and what the village once considered possible.”

Ossa settled beside them, keeping watch uphill. “Military maps mark wells, bridges, forage. We called that complete enough to move a regiment.”

“Complete for the regiment,” Ruhan said. “Less complete for the people whose wells became forage.”

He showed them a page where an imperial road ran in confident black from Kesta to the mountain foot. Around it clustered small notes: bridge refused tax carts after flood; orchard families crossed north; burial shelf moved; road company held an empty approach for eleven months because its destination had walked away.

“A fixed map records the success of whoever forced the line to stay,” he said. “My aunt taught me to record where a line stopped deserving belief.”

Tavra examined the empty margin beyond the three-tooth crag. “And this blank?”

“Could mean no route. Could mean a route whose keeper never spoke to us. Could mean my aunt spilled soup over the page.”

Ossa rubbed the stained paper. “Lentil.”

“Then one uncertainty has yielded.”

The brief humor eased nothing about the cliff, but it returned their attention to what they actually knew. A pass had crossed beneath the crag after moonset in one late summer. The mountain now moved in the same season and direction. They could wait where that old account began, or gamble their bodies on an upper seam already travelling away.

Tavra lowered herself with one leg straight. “Your atlas says the pass existed.”

“It says a shepherd crossed six years ago.”

“You drew a line.”

“I drew his account.”

“Lines imply routes.”

“Only to people who think ink gives orders.”

Ossa leaned over the page. “What do the blank places imply?”

“That nobody I trust has spoken for them.”

The shelf moved another finger-width east. The upper seam moved with it, widening the distance from Hadrun.

Ruhan turned pages until he found a note written by his aunt Savi. Her hand sloped downhill even on level paper.

WHEN THE GREY SPINE WALKS EAST, WESTBOUND FEET SLEEP. PASS ARRIVES AFTER MOONSET BELOW THE THREE-TOOTH CRAG.

He looked through the mist. Three broken pinnacles showed above them, dark teeth against white sky.

“We climbed past the route,” he said.

Tavra followed his gaze. “The pass lies below?”

“It will.”

“When?”

“After moonset, according to a woman who disliked clocks.”

“We lose a day.”

“We gain a mountain.”

“College riders gain the same day.”

“Riders think as you do. They will climb.”

Her face shut around the insult. Ruhan let it stand. Tavra could read pressure through leather better than anyone he had met, yet pursuit still made her treat terrain as a problem that yielded to superior effort.

They descended.

Going down one-handed required Ossa to manage Ruhan’s safety cord. She tied it around his waist without using a military knot. Tavra moved last, lowering her straight left leg from ledge to ledge while mineral stiffness held the knee. Twice she tried to hurry. Twice the mountain shifted and made haste irrelevant.

At dusk they reached a bowl beneath the three-tooth crag. No pass opened there. A wall of slate blocked the west.

Ruhan found an old shepherd hearth under an overhang. He checked for recent use, then left a strip of dried meat beside it in case the unknown owner returned. Ossa gathered thorn brush. Tavra studied the slate wall.

“You expect that to move,” she said.

“I expect my aunt saw it move once.”

“That distinction could kill us.”

“So could pretending it will obey because we are urgent.”

They lit no fire. College riders showed at sunset on the upper shelf, small figures moving against cloud. As Ruhan had guessed, they climbed west. Their lead cutter salted a brace line into the rock. Six horses strained while dancers forced a temporary ramp upward.

The range accepted the pressure and returned it elsewhere.

A distant crack rolled through the bowl. Slate rose between the riders and their lower supports. One horse screamed. The imperial line scattered before the unfinished measure trapped them.

Tavra watched from the overhang. “They need a release.”

“They brought cutters,” Ossa said.

“They brought a plan.”

Ruhan opened his atlas across his knees. With his left hand he marked the failed ascent, the riders’ number, and the hour. He did not draw a route through their danger. A record of refusal could save the next traveller from mistaking force for access.

His right wrist throbbed inside the splint. The bridge injury had embarrassed him at first. A pass-runner without two hands could not climb his usual lines, catch an anchor, or lower another traveller securely. He had treated the splint as a delay between his competent past and its return.

Tonight the limitation had corrected his reading. With two sound hands, he might have reached the upper seam and led all three into the riders’ broken measure.

Ossa noticed him looking at the splint. “Regretting the bridge?”

“Regretting that it needed to break me before I listened.”

“Tavra may have a lecture for that.”

“She charges too much.”

Across the bowl, Tavra had sat with her knife but no leather. She traced possible cuts on her palm and erased them with her thumb.

Ruhan went to her. “What are you designing?”

“A way to take pressure off the pass when it opens.”

“You have not seen it.”

“That limits the detail.”

“It should limit the cutting.”

Her left knee remained extended beside her. Pale lines showed even in moonlight. “If the pass arrives carrying torque, somebody receives it.”

“Or we wait until it finishes arriving.”

“And if riders descend first?”

“We ask the pass keeper.”

“There is no keeper.”

“Then we do not invent one in your body.”

She looked at him for a long time. “Eda said something similar.”

“Your daughter sounds exhausting.”

“She learned from a master.”

The dry answer almost became a smile.

After moonset, the bowl tilted.

No drum announced it. Pebbles rolled east. The slate wall lowered by spans as the grey spine travelled beneath them. What had seemed a barrier revealed its upper surface, a broad sloping back worn by old hoof marks. The pass had not opened. It had arrived under their feet.

Ruhan woke Ossa and Tavra. “No salt. No score. Follow the old marks only while they remain.”

They packed in darkness. Ossa took the evidence bag. Tavra tried to take Ruhan’s atlas so he could balance, but he handed it to her only after she asked.

The crossing required stillness more often than movement. They walked twenty paces while the slate levelled, then stopped when its eastward travel steepened the west edge. Ruhan listened through his soles for the low grinding that meant one layer slid beneath another. Each time it deepened, they crouched and let the range carry them.

Behind, lanterns descended from the failed imperial ramp. Riders had discovered the bowl.

Ossa touched her drum rim. “I could knock them into expecting a count.”

“They may charge early,” Tavra said.

“They may wait.”

Ossa removed her hand. “Then they choose their mistake.”

Halfway across, the old hoof marks divided. One branch climbed toward a narrow west notch. The other descended into fog.

Tavra held up the atlas. “Your aunt marked no fork.”

“She crossed before it formed.”

“Which way?”

Ruhan studied lichen, droppings, and the fresh scrape of slate. The upper path looked safer because it looked like a pass. No recent hoof had polished it. The lower branch carried crushed thyme.

“Down.”

Ossa peered into fog. “Based on?”

“Sheep.”

“Finally, an authority Tavra cannot outrank.”

They descended. The fog thinned after thirty steps, exposing a shelf that moved west relative to the spine beneath it. For several minutes, walking down carried them toward Hadrun faster than climbing could have done.

The riders took the upper notch.

A command drum sounded once. The notch rose with the backward mountains, carrying the imperial party east. Their lanterns dwindled above the cloud until rock hid them.

Dawn found Ruhan’s group on red soil.

A line of low stone posts marked no border. Each bore a seasonal date and an arrow showing where Hadrun’s grazing release had last passed. Three people waited beside them in felt-soled boots. The eldest held a shepherd’s staff cut with sole-court witness marks.

“I am Dena Cor,” she said. “You crossed on Savi Tal’s sleeping route.”

Ruhan glanced at his atlas. “You know my aunt?”

“She owes me two jars of plum salt.” Dena looked at his splint. “And now you owe the mountain a better note.”

He opened to the crossing page. Instead of drawing the lower shelf as a permanent line, he wrote: ARRIVED AFTER MOONSET ON DAY THIRTEEN. SHEEP TOOK LOWER FORK. UPPER FORK REFUSED RIDERS. ASK AGAIN.

Dena read over his shoulder. “Incomplete atlas.”

“Accurately.”

Her companions searched Ossa’s evidence bag but did not take it. Tavra presented the copied pension names and described the fossilized roots beneath Nareth. Ossa named the seven anchor schedule and offered her Hadrun confession before Dena could ask.

“The original Nareth fragment?” Dena said.

“In a Meren wall shrine,” Ruhan answered. “Washerwomen route it through soap barrels. A message should reach your western court by tomorrow.”

“You ask us to hear evidence we cannot yet touch.”

“We ask for a room and witnesses. Touch it when it arrives.”

Dena studied Tavra. “Imperial notices call you traitor.”

“They are early,” Tavra said. “I have not yet testified.”

“Hadrun prefers charges after breakfast.” Dena turned toward the red valley road. “Sole courts gather at the kiln hall tomorrow. You may speak. They may hand you to the College. They may copy your evidence. I promise only the hearing.”

Tavra shifted against her rigid knee. Every instinct in her face wanted a promise of outcome.

“A hearing is enough,” she said.

Dena started down the valley. Ruhan followed with his atlas open in his left hand. Behind them, the pass rose into morning and removed the route by which they had come.

Chapter 16 — The Score-Cutter’s Treason

Tavra stood trial with her boots on the table.

Dena Cor had ordered them removed before the kiln hall filled. Thirty-seven score-cutters sat in a broken circle around the evidence boards, their benches arranged by no rank Tavra recognized. Hadrun sole courts placed the accused person’s shoes where every cutter could inspect wear, hidden channels, false stitching, and the history a mouth might edit.

Her left boot leaned outward from years of sparing the lithified knee. Fresh cracks crossed the heel after Meren’s common. The right sole turned inward under load. Neither offered dignity.

Dena struck her staff once. “Tavra Ilen, former center of the Imperial College, unlicensed cutter, named in a capital notice for treason against roads and civil grain. Do you accept this court’s witness rules?”

“What are they?”

“Answer what you know. Name what you infer. Say when memory may favor you. Any cutter may challenge craft claims. Any Hadrun resident may challenge consequence. We cannot pardon imperial treason. We can decide whether your evidence deserves copying.”

“I accept.”

A grey-haired cutter opposite her lifted Tavra’s left boot. “You have altered this sole since Kesta.”

“Three times.”

“Unmarked alterations.”

“I had no court stamp.”

“You had a knife.”

Tavra looked at the shallow release channel she had cut beside the mountain bowl. “Then I failed to mark them.”

Murmurs moved through the benches. She had expected argument about the Dominion Measure. Instead, the court began with her habits.

Another cutter found salt burned into the toe seam. “This line routes central spill toward your injured knee.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“To keep it from other feet.”

“Did they choose that arrangement?”

“They chose their own steps. I kept the fault path.”

“That does not answer.”

“No. They did not choose where the final spill went.”

The cutter returned the boot to the table. “Record that.”

A clerk pressed charcoal to paper.

Ossa sat behind Tavra with the evidence bag between her boots. Her stripped drum frame rested against the wall. Ruhan had removed his wrist splint at dawn for the first time since the bridge, but swelling forced it back on before the hearing. He stood near the kiln door and watched routes instead of testimony.

Outside, Hadrun road companies gathered in the red dust. Imperial notices had reached them before breakfast. Tavra’s name hung on the kiln gate beneath a promise of grain suspension for any district sheltering her.

Dena pointed to the copies from the Nareth barracks. “State their source.”

Tavra described the abandoned worker quarters, split floor stone, pension ledgers, and fossilized roots beneath the cellar. She distinguished what she had seen from what the margin notes claimed. She named each copied worker. Seni’s correction followed her from the refuge: names, not bodies.

A miller rose from the public benches. “My grain road uses a hold from the same year. Are my fields poisoned?”

“I do not know.”

“Can you test?”

“Dig beyond the oldest salt boundary. Check fine roots before thick ones. If they ring under a knife or snap like fired clay, stop digging and keep the sample.”

“Will release cure it?”

“No. Stone already inside dead root does not become living wood.”

The miller sat with that answer.

Dena lifted Ossa’s copied anchor schedule. “Permanent holds. Seven. Does this prove a continental plan?”

“It proves seven companies received permanent anchor instructions,” Tavra said. “Their routes join at Meren. The command skin names coordinated commencement. I infer the Dominion Measure intends to bind the joined web beyond a seasonal hold.”

“You do not possess the Master Score.”

“No.”

“You have not read its center.”

“No.”

“Then keep inference separate.”

“Yes.”

A commotion sounded outside. Ruhan opened the kiln door only wide enough for a woman carrying a soap barrel. Steam and lye smell entered with her. Two Hadrun witnesses rolled the barrel to the evidence table.

The washerwoman from Meren’s court had sent no name. Inside wet linen lay a votive buckle, a mason’s clay token, and the Nareth fragment wrapped in waxed cloth.

Tavra’s breath stopped.

She had not touched the leather since giving it to Ruhan before the rain bridge. Even wrapped, it carried the shape of twelve years hidden beneath her Dorel bench. Possession had felt like punishment and insurance together. Now thirty-seven cutters leaned close.

Dena did not hand it to her. “Identify without touching.”

“Layered goat leather. Inner left sole from the Nareth lead center pair. Torn below the quarry release.”

“How do you know?”

“I cut it.”

The court unwrapped the fragment. Oil had blackened the edges. Serad’s later command incision crossed Tavra’s release turn at a harsh angle, both histories occupying the same leather.

Dena chose three examiners who had not spoken. They traced sequence by depth, blade direction, and wear. The oldest, Saro Pell, looked up after a long silence.

“Release cut first,” he said. “Cross-cut made after fitting, likely while the sole remained on the shoe. Different blade. Heavier right hand.”

Tavra’s mouth had gone dry.

“Give your Nareth testimony,” Dena said.

She began with the third count.

The hillside had started moving south before Serad’s revised score allowed change. Tavra felt the road pull across her knees. She signalled uncertainty with the permitted shoulder turn. Serad, observing from the command platform, gave forward continuation.

“Did command cadence compel you?” a cutter asked.

“No. I knew the response. I obeyed it. I could have broken my mark.”

“What did you believe would happen?”

“That the city road would buckle under occupied houses and kill more people.”

“Was that possible?”

“Yes.”

“Certain?”

“No.”

She described cutting the quarry release into her sole before the measure, anticipating some lateral shift. Serad’s field cutter crossed it after the first alarm and bound the company forward. Tavra still had one chance: turn her heel hard enough to tear the sole and break center. She did not.

The road held. The hillside district travelled into the lower retaining wall. Houses folded against stone. Tavra cut release at last, directing part of the road toward the empty quarry. The delayed turn accelerated the lower collapse while preserving the main street.

“How many deaths?” asked the miller.

“Official count, eighty-six. Seasonal workers lived outside the census. I do not know their number.”

“How many company injuries?”

“Thirty-one recorded. More appeared years later.”

“What entered you?”

Tavra pulled her trouser leg above the left knee. Pale stone branches reached into the thigh. “The road’s lateral counter-motion. Both knees took it. The left has worsened since a spring release four days ago.”

Saro Pell examined the lines without touching. “And then?”

“I kept the fragment. I let the College publish the crossed release as my unauthorized sabotage.”

“Why?”

“Because exposing the sequence also exposed that I had felt the shift and continued. I wanted proof without testimony.”

Dena’s staff rested across her knees. “Proof that could accuse Serad without requiring you to accuse yourself.”

“Yes.”

“What did you do with your daughter?”

The question did not belong to craft, yet consequence sat within the rules.

“I left Eda in College custody. She was ten. I told myself my presence endangered her. I did not ask.”

A younger cutter spoke from the circle. “Why should your late honesty guide Hadrun now?”

“It should not guide you. Examine the leather. Dig your salt fields. Ask active centers what their orders say. My confession cannot make those facts cleaner.”

“And if we copy it, you gain a defense against treason.”

“I gain a more accurate charge.”

The clerk’s charcoal paused.

Tavra faced the public benches. “I broke license law. I concealed evidence. I obeyed a command after local ground invalidated the score. If treason means refusing the College now, I accept it. If loyalty means repeating Nareth across seven provinces because roads carry grain, then loyalty describes a larger burial.”

Dena struck the staff before applause could form. “No speeches to purchase a verdict.”

Tavra sat.

The sole court divided.

Licensed cutters argued that publication would trigger company refusal before replacement routes existed. Hadrun independents answered that the College had used grain access to prevent informed choice. One elder feared every village would improvise dangerous releases after seeing Tavra’s fragment. Saro proposed copies limited to craft courts. The miller demanded public names because his children worked a grain hold.

Dena called four working cutters to the center and gave each the same problem: a seasonal Hadrun road due for release in nine days, ordered into permanent rehearsal tomorrow, with three hill villages dependent on its grain carts. Their answers revealed the split more clearly than speeches.

The first would obey long enough to bring stores through, then break the anchor after winter. Tavra warned that months of held torque could kill the release company. The cutter replied that hunger also killed without waiting for craft purity.

The second would refuse immediately and send carts over a shepherd shelf. Ruhan asked whether the shelf keeper had agreed. The cutter knew no keeper and had assumed the route lay unused.

The third proposed a false rehearsal: salt the field, march the company, leave the anchor unbound. Ossa noted that trained inspectors would hear the empty counter-beat before the second turn.

The fourth, a young bark-sole cutter named Imri, placed both hands on the Nareth rubbing. “Copy everything. Let each road crew decide whether its ground resembles this one.”

“And if they lack skill to judge?” Saro asked.

“Then send judges, not commands.”

“Thirty-seven cutters cannot reach seven provinces in six days.”

“Thirty-seven copies can.”

A licensed woman named Bera raised Tavra’s right boot from the evidence table. “This workmanship proves why unrestricted copying is dangerous. Ilen can cut a spill line by feel. Most village repairers cannot. A fragment showing one release turn may look like permission to cut it into every held road.”

“Then copy the limits beside it,” Imri said.

“Words do not create skill.”

“Secrecy does not create it either.”

Dena turned to Tavra. “If an ordinary cutter copies your Nareth release onto another sole, what happens?”

“The cut may do nothing. It may route pressure into the wearer’s knee. It may open stored torque toward occupied ground. Nareth’s turn belongs to Nareth’s old quarry angle.”

“Should we conceal it?”

“No. Mark it as evidence, not instruction. Include depth, sequence, location, and failure. Leave out no cost.”

Bera held up the boot. “You ask us to trust labels while imperial roads fail.”

“I ask you not to confuse withholding with safety. The College withheld the fossilized roots and repeated permanent cuts elsewhere.”

A grain factor from the public benches demanded leave to speak. Dena nodded.

“My contracts feed two towns,” he said. “If crews refuse the grain road, carts stop. Does anyone in this hall accept responsibility for empty bins?”

Silence followed.

Tavra recognized the old temptation to fill it with a comprehensive plan. She resisted until Dena addressed her by name.

“I accept responsibility for bringing evidence that may contribute to refusal,” Tavra said. “I cannot accept choice for the crews or promise a route I have not walked. Hadrun already practices seasonal releases. Ask each valley what temporary crossings it can offer, what loads they carry, and for how long.”

“That takes time.”

“Yes.”

“People may go hungry while councils talk.”

“Yes.”

“You have no answer.”

“I have no answer that removes cost by putting it in a hidden center.”

The factor swore and sat. Dena did not rebuke him.

Saro set three sheets beside the fragment. “I propose a layered copy. First sheet: verified cut sequence and examiner marks. Second: named testimony, including Ilen’s failures. Third: warnings against reuse and questions each crew must answer before refusing. Craft courts receive all three. Public boards receive the testimony and anchor schedule, not a sole pattern detailed enough to imitate.”

Imri objected that limiting the cut preserved guild power. Bera objected that any public image invited imitation. For another half hour, they argued over blade-depth notation, witness names, widow privacy, and whether the imperial seizure notice itself belonged in the packet.

Tavra listened as her evidence ceased to be hers. Every change annoyed her. Some improved accuracy. Others protected people she had not considered. When Dena asked whether the court might black out the cellar keeper’s address, Tavra said yes without demanding another route to credit.

Ossa remained silent until asked about timing.

“Companies rehearse within six days,” she said. “Separate delays have slowed them. Secrecy now does not preserve deliberation. It preserves command.”

A cutter pointed at her stripped drum. “You used command at Hadrun.”

“Yes.”

“Why trust your distinction?”

“Do not. Ask the workers who hear the cadence in their sleep.”

Outside, horses entered the kiln yard.

Ruhan called, “College at both valley roads. Twelve riders, eight on foot.”

Dena stood. “This court has not reached decision.”

The kiln doors burst inward.

College agents wore civil grain sashes over cutter coats. Their captain held a sealed seizure order. “By authority of Chancellor Miren, surrender Tavra Ilen, all Nareth material, and instruments used to obstruct imperial roads.”

Dena read the seal from where she stood. “This hall recognizes craft custody during hearing.”

“Craft custody does not supersede treason.”

“Then wait outside and dislike us lawfully.”

The captain signalled.

Agents crossed the public benches. Hadrun cutters did not form a fighting line; many had knives, none designed for battle. They lifted evidence boards and passed them hand to hand toward the rear kilns.

Tavra reached for the fragment. Dena caught her wrist. “Court custody.”

An agent vaulted the table and swept the leather into a document tube. He kicked Tavra’s boots aside, then drove toward the door under cover of two short-bladed guards.

Ossa moved before any drum sounded.

She rolled her stripped frame across the floor. It struck the agent’s ankles. He stumbled, but one guard turned and slashed. Ossa raised her left arm. Steel opened the forearm from wristward muscle to elbow, shallow at first and deep near the middle.

Blood hit the Nareth testimony.

Ossa caught the document tube with her right hand. The injured left arm failed when she tried to turn. Tavra took the tube, shoved it inside her coat, and dragged Ossa behind the brick evidence plinth.

“Grip?” Tavra asked.

Ossa flexed her fingers. Two moved. The others curled late. “Weak.”

Tavra tore linen from the soap barrel and bound pressure above the cut. “Not tight enough to kill the hand.”

Blood welled between the first wraps. The blade had opened muscle along the outside of the forearm without severing the main wrist cords. Tavra could judge that much, no more.

“Sensation?” she asked.

Ossa touched thumb to each fingertip. The third missed twice. “Present. Slow.”

“Can you hold the drum?”

“With the right.”

“Can you strike?”

“Not while you ask foolish questions.”

Tavra added folded linen directly over the deepest point and made Ossa press it with her sound hand. The command-skin material tucked inside the stripped frame remained dry. Ossa’s future work depended on a grip now pooling blood into white cloth. Injury changed more than pain; it changed which promises a body could keep.

A Hadrun healer crawled behind the plinth with a jar of boiled thread. “I can close it here or after we leave.”

“After,” Ossa said.

“Here lowers blood loss.”

“After lowers capture.”

The healer looked to Tavra, expecting command from the older cutter. Tavra shook her head. “Her arm.”

Ossa chose a tight temporary pad and delayed stitching. The healer tied the wrap to survive running, then marked the time in charcoal on Ossa’s sleeve so whoever treated her later would know how long the cut had remained open.

“Your bedside manner has improved,” Ossa said.

“Lying down would improve yours.”

Another agent reached them. Ruhan hooked the man’s knee with his staff, using the left hand while his splinted right arm stayed against his chest. The agent fell without breaking the wrist again.

At the rear kilns, cutters fed copied pages through drying vents to waiting apprentices outside. Each apprentice ran in a different direction. The court had reached its decision through attack.

Dena climbed onto the table. “Copy all testimony. Copy the fragment face and cut depth. Original leaves by south ash.”

Tavra pulled the document tube free. “You said court custody.”

“It remains court custody. You will carry it to the ash door because my knees object to heroics.”

“Give it to Ossa,” Ossa said through clenched teeth. “Tavra needs both hands to move.”

The judgement cut Tavra’s pride and preserved the evidence. She placed the tube under Ossa’s right arm and tied it across her ribs. Ossa’s left forearm hung bound against her body.

They escaped through a kiln mouth while wet clay jars shattered in the hall. Heat struck Tavra’s face. Workers pulled them over a low ash wall and into a cart trench.

At the grain road, imperial criers had already nailed fresh notices to every post.

TAVRA ILEN, SCORE-CUTTER AND FORMER CENTER, CONVICTED IN ABSENCE OF TREASON AGAINST THE STABILITY OF MEREN.

No trial had occurred in Meren. The notice listed shelter penalties, grain suspensions, and authority for immediate seizure.

Dena tore one down and handed it to the clerk. “Copy that too.”

Around the valley, apprentices carried charcoal rubbings of the Nareth sole. Some went to licensed shops. Others went to mills, road barracks, and seasonal release houses. Nobody waited for Tavra to tell them where.

Ossa leaned against the ash cart, blood darkening her bandage. The original rode under her sound arm. Tavra’s boots remained somewhere inside the occupied kiln hall.

Barefoot on warm red soil, Tavra watched her treason leave in thirty-seven different hands.

Chapter 17 — A Province Off the Beat

The imperial drummer lifted both sticks, and Ossa’s wounded hand closed on an answer it could no longer give.

Pain opened along her left forearm. The healer’s stitches pulled beneath linen. She forced the fingers flat against her thigh before trained memory could raise them to match his count.

Across Hadrun’s eastern grain road, three hundred company workers stood inside fresh step-salt. Their center lines faced a torso-sized anchor stone newly lowered from an imperial cart. Grain wagons waited behind them, wheels loaded deep into red dust. On the road post, Chancellor Miren’s order snapped in the wind.

FULL REHEARSAL REQUIRED. DISTRICT ACCESS TO IMPERIAL GRAIN SUSPENDED UPON FAILURE OR REFUSAL.

The order allowed no distinction between a crew that could not safely hold and one that would not. Children watched from the mill roofs. Storekeepers had already begun counting flour sacks.

Dena Cor stood outside the salt with Tavra and Ruhan. Tavra wore borrowed kiln sandals with uncut soles. Without her altered boots, every uneven patch jarred the left knee. Ruhan’s right wrist remained wrapped, though the splint had been shortened to let his fingers move.

Ossa stood among Hadrun drummers she had met that morning.

“Cadence officers to opening mark,” called the imperial inspector.

The company drummers looked at Ossa. She shook her head once. Not yet. The choice belonged at the moment each of them could see its consequence.

The inspector read the penalty aloud. Grain toll seals would close at sunset. Company pensions would enter review. Any drummer breaking assigned count faced military seizure. Any cutter altering rehearsal soles faced the treason provision already applied to Tavra Ilen.

“Questions?” he asked.

A center named Jori raised her hand. “Duration of final hold?”

“Today’s rehearsal runs forty counts.”

“The final hold.”

“Dominion provisions follow after successful alignment.”

“That does not answer.”

“Questions relevant to today only.”

Ossa heard anger ripple through breath. An angry company accelerated. Command training had always used that fact: provoke, then give rhythm somewhere to go.

She stepped onto an overturned grain basket, keeping both feet outside the salt. “You have copies of seven permanent anchor orders,” she called. “You have Nareth names. Do not move because I speak. Read your own soles.”

The inspector pointed. “Seize the deserter.”

No one approached her. His eight guards could arrest one person. They could not safely cross an active field while three hundred trained bodies remained uncertain.

Jori knelt and removed her left shoe. The sole carried a new center cut, deep enough to bind past rehearsal release.

“This has no forty-count exit,” she said.

Other workers checked. Cutters began naming hidden continuance marks.

The imperial drummer struck his sticks together. “Opening positions.”

Conditioned bodies shifted. He had not touched the drum, yet shoulders squared and heels found marks.

Ossa climbed down from the basket. “We have little time. Local drummers, gather your own crews. Ask who wants instruction in a safe miss. Anyone may choose ordinary rehearsal instead. Anyone may leave the field, though the inspector has named the cost.”

A young drummer with blue cord around his rim stared at her bandaged arm. “You cannot demonstrate.”

“Correct.”

“How do we trust a miss we cannot see?”

“You test it without salt.”

They moved behind the grain wagons. Six local drummers each brought fewer than fifty workers. Ossa refused a single assembly. One command could seize one crowd; six conversations made seizure harder.

Before teaching footwork, she asked every group to name the sound that most compelled them. The answers differed. Older road crews reacted to the deep opening stroke. Former military braces moved at the stick click before any skin sounded. Mill teams had trained under handbells and watched a foreman’s shoulder instead of a drum. One company followed the intake of breath beside them.

“Then there is no single safe miss,” Ossa said. “Anyone promising one has failed to listen.”

She sent the groups far enough apart that their practice rhythms overlapped without aligning. The blue-cord drummer clicked his sticks and half his workers stepped before he struck. Another group heard that motion and nearly followed the wrong crew.

“Stop,” Jori called.

Everyone froze.

“Freezing puts weight into the locked leg,” Ossa said. “Walk out of the practice patch. Ordinary feet.”

Several people looked ashamed as they dispersed.

“Do not be embarrassed by conditioning,” she told them. “It kept road lines from trampling one another for years. We are not proving you weak. We are finding where the response enters before you decide.”

A broad brace named Kesh raised his heel. “Mine starts in the jaw. I bite when the sticks lift.”

“Can you open your mouth before the count?”

He tried and laughed at how foolish it felt. On the next signal, his heel remained down.

A woman from the mill team turned her back on the foreman’s shoulder, then lost balance because she could not see adjacent workers. Her crew moved into a crescent so she could watch feet instead. Jori’s center line practiced saying each person’s own name at count three, not in chorus. Their voices collided and prevented a unified trained breath.

The imperial inspector sent a clerk to record participants. Dena placed Hadrun witnesses beside him and recorded the recorder’s name. He objected that civil officers needed no witness.

“Today everyone does,” Dena said.

Ossa watched fear change the crews’ choices as penalties became personal on paper. Nine workers left when the clerk wrote their company numbers. Five who had planned ordinary count shifted toward a missed beat after reading their soles. Two refused to state a choice in advance.

“You may decide on the field,” Ossa told them.

“How do we coordinate if we do not declare?” asked a cutter.

“We do not coordinate beyond safety. Give them open spill and room. Their refusal belongs to the beat when it comes.”

That answer frightened her too. Military work removed uncertainty because uncertainty among weighted bodies could maim. Yet advance commitment here would let the inspector identify every gap and command around it. They needed enough preparation to avoid collision, not enough to make refusal predictable.

One test almost failed. A southern crew tried missing count four while two workers returned early on the same foot. Their shoulders collided. One fell across another’s ankle.

Ossa stopped the practice and knelt beside them. Her left arm throbbed uselessly in the sling while Jori checked the ankle.

“No damage,” Jori said.

The fallen worker sat up furious. “Your miss made us less safe.”

“Our version did,” his drummer replied before Ossa could. “We choose another or take ordinary count.”

They moved their gap to eleven, the place where their seasonal release training already expected outward weight. The next trial held.

Ossa used her sound right hand to draw foot marks in dust. “The opening cadence conditions weight on count four. Do not miss four by freezing. A fixed leg takes the next worker’s motion. On three, soften the heel. Let four pass through the empty shoe. Return on five only if your neighbor remains balanced.”

“That shifts the road,” Jori said.

“Yes. A little.”

“How much?”

“I do not know. Each crew’s ground differs. Your cutters must open local spill before anyone chooses.”

Tavra limped between groups and inspected soles only when invited. Hadrun cutters broke the continuance channels, not the anchor lines. A complete break before stored pressure entered could let the inspector replace them. A missed count after engagement would make the anchor fail to capture the road.

A mill worker named Seli crossed his arms. “If the grain road shifts, those wagons may not reach the western towns.”

“If it holds permanently, your valley roots may harden,” Tavra said.

“May. Hunger this week carries less uncertainty.”

Dena answered from beside him. “The north shepherd route can take half loads for three days. Western villages have offered flour from release stores. Not enough for ordinary ration.”

“So my children eat less because Meren hides costs.”

“Yes,” Ossa said. “Refusal does not clean that.”

Seli looked at the rehearsal field, then the wagons. “I take ordinary count. My family cannot carry another promise.”

“Your choice,” Ossa said.

Some people cursed him. She turned on them. “No. If you punish refusal of your refusal, you have learned only a new cadence.”

Seli joined the inspector’s full-count line. Twelve followed him. Thirty-seven left the field entirely. The remaining workers practiced the softened heel on bare dust.

Ossa moved among local drummers. Her injured fingers twitched whenever they struck the opening phrase. She kept the left arm in its sling. She could advise breath, show stick angle with her right hand, and hear when a miss became a stumble. She could not embody the pattern for them. That limitation denied her old authority and made space for six local versions.

One crew chose to miss count four. Another chose count eleven, where its seasonal release normally began. Jori’s center line chose to divide: alternating dancers would leave an empty heel on four and five, reducing the chance that one weak ankle caught the whole gap. No crew copied another exactly.

The inspector’s drum sounded across the field.

“Salt lines close in ten minutes.”

Tavra came to Ossa. “The western spill crosses the wagon lane. If the road turns sharply, first carts tip.”

“Tell the carters.”

“They may pull wagons off now.”

“Tell them that too.”

The carters argued. Four unloaded sacks beside the field. Nine moved wagons to the northern track. Three remained because their contracts penalized deviation. Nobody had time to resolve it.

Dena climbed the road post. “This district has received temporary crossing offers from five valleys. Ration drops by one fifth if the grain road closes for more than two days. Children, infirm houses, and road crews receive full shares first. These terms last three days, then must be agreed again.”

The specificity quieted people more effectively than courage could.

At noon, the rehearsal began.

The imperial drummer struck opening cadence. Three hundred bodies took first weight. Anchor stone sank a finger-width into red soil.

Ossa heard every trained breath align. The sound once had filled her with power: not vanity alone, but relief that hundreds of risks could move under one reliable count. Hadrun had taught her what reliability could carry into a ravine.

Count two passed cleanly.

At three, local heels softened.

Four arrived.

Jori’s line let the beat pass through empty shoes. The movement did not look like rebellion. It looked like a company breathing at the wrong moment. The anchor shuddered.

The imperial drummer increased volume. Conditioned shoulders jerked toward recovery. Ossa’s left hand clenched hard enough to pull a stitch.

“Choose again,” she called. “Every beat.”

On five, half Jori’s line returned. The others waited. A second crew missed its chosen eleven. Farther south, another drummer dropped count four entirely and entered on six.

The province went off the beat one road at a time.

Ground responded. The eastern grain road slipped north by half a cart width. Wagons left on the lane tilted; one spilled sacks without overturning. The northern shepherd track widened as seasonal release pressure found it. A held bridge downstream rose at one end and lowered at the other, still passable to people but not loaded teams.

The anchor stone tried to follow all these directions. Its salted boundary cracked.

“Full hold!” the inspector shouted. “Centers, receive!”

Seli and the ordinary-count line obeyed. Counter-motion drove into their ankles because the larger company no longer matched them. Ossa saw Seli’s knees buckle.

“Release ordinary line!” Tavra shouted to the imperial cutter.

He froze between order and visible injury.

Jori stepped out of her own missed count, crossed one pace, and cut Seli’s heel strap with a work knife. His sole tore free. The interruption spilled his load into open salt instead of bone. Other local cutters followed, asking names before cutting each worker out.

The imperial drummer beat command harder. Ossa’s body tried to answer. She had once believed trained response could only be defeated by a stronger competing rhythm. Instead, local drummers set down their sticks. Without a rival cadence, workers heard the command naked.

Some obeyed. More missed.

The anchor rose from the ground.

Not dramatically. Red soil loosened around it, and the stone tipped onto one edge. It had failed to capture the grain road. The road continued north, carrying three empty wagons around the mill and leaving the imperial cart stranded inside a broken salt oval.

Cheers started near the roofs. Ossa raised her right hand.

“Count your costs first.”

Seli had a bruised ankle and could stand. Two braces suffered knee strains. A carter broke three fingers when sacks shifted. No one had taken mineral staining. The western towns now faced reduced grain. Imperial guards retained their weapons and fury.

Dena sent runners to count what remained usable. Of the sixteen grain wagons, nine could reach the northern track if unloaded to half weight. Three stood stranded beyond the road’s turn. One had lost a wheel. The rest could return east, though the inspector threatened seizure if carters changed destination.

The carters formed their own circle beside the spilled sacks. They agreed to divide loads by the three-day terms Dena had named, but two refused western travel without written protection from contract penalties. A mill clerk drafted the protection on the back of Miren’s rehearsal order. Local witnesses signed before Dena did.

Families from the roof line came down with baskets. Ossa expected a rush toward free grain. Instead, Jori placed Seli at the first scale because his choice for ordinary count had helped cause his injury and had not removed him from the district. He protested that others would call it reward for obedience.

“It is treatment for an ankle hurt in our field,” she said. “Your politics can wait behind the bandage.”

The decision drew argument but held. Children carried split sacks away from damp ground. Millers marked every measure removed from imperial contract stock. Nothing about the work resembled liberation. It resembled a province accepting the account after refusing the command.

The inspector ordered his guards to stop distribution. None moved. Their ration came from the same wagons.

Dena ordered the public evidence boards raised before he could restore authority.

Saro Pell read the Nareth examination aloud: original release, later cross-cut, eighty-six official dead, thirty-one recorded company injuries, delayed stone sickness in named pension files. The miller split a root sample over the board. Its interior shone grey. He held up the fossilized roots from the old salt boundary and named the field where he had dug them that morning.

Then Dena displayed seven permanent anchor orders.

“This court distinguishes proof from inference,” she said. “These orders say permanent. They join at the capital. The public convocation notice says seasonal. Both statements cannot govern the same measure.”

A courier from the kiln hall pushed through the crowd. She carried a calfskin scrap folded inside laundered linen. No signature marked it. Black wax from Meren sealed the edge.

Dena read the contents privately, then gave it to Saro. His face changed.

“Anonymous evidence,” he told the crowd. “Not yet authenticated. It claims to copy center projections from the Master Score: four hundred trained centers, more than six thousand supporting workers, lasting injury above three thousand at median resistance, permanent root binding across three provinces, and no exit drawn for the principal center.”

Tavra went still. “Eda.”

Ossa heard hope trying to become certainty. “You do not know.”

“No.” Tavra took the correction. “I do not.”

Dena placed the anonymous extract beside verified testimony rather than over it. “Believe its numbers only after examination. Believe the permanent orders because thirty-seven cutters copied them under witness. Meren intends a lasting hold and has told the public otherwise.”

Bell crews carried the statement outward.

Hadrun used grain bells to announce road openings from valley to valley. Today each station rang the permanent orders, the evidence distinctions, and the refusal result. Drummers relayed between hills where bells did not reach, but each local caller used different phrasing so no military cadence took hold. By late afternoon, the message crossed the backward mountains through worker stations still delaying rehearsal.

At Meren, civil recorders received Hadrun grain reports. Miren would hear that her seasonal notice contradicted permanent field orders. She would hear it beside the immediate cost: grain delayed, ration reduced, anchor not captured.

Ossa’s bandage had bled through. She sat while the healer reopened the wrap and stitched the pulled section. Around her, people argued over wagons, flour priority, and whether the anonymous figures deserved ink on public boards. Refusal had not united them. It had made their disagreements possible after command failed.

Near sunset, every imperial road bell struck one sustained note.

The inspector looked toward Meren. Color left his face. Around him, even the guards stopped pretending this remained an ordinary rehearsal dispute.

“That signal belongs to Stillday,” Dena said.

A second note followed, low enough to shake flour from the mill eaves, then three anchor calls months of rehearsal had taught every company to recognize.

Serad had answered Hadrun’s missed beat by beginning the Imperial Stillness early.

Chapter 18 — The Imperial Stillness

The Hadrun grain bell missed its fourth stroke, and Serad returned to the stair at Oris.

For an instant he stood fourteen again with snow packed inside his shoes. His mother lay below the temple with one arm around his sister. The town had moved three hundred strides in darkness. Houses met the winter cliff before anyone agreed who could order a hold. An old center named Vessa took the temple stair alone and kept it level until six children climbed above the slide.

She died with both feet mineralized to the step.

Memory never gave him the whole town. It kept pieces selected by a frightened boy: snow on his sister’s eyelashes; temple tiles standing at an angle against the new slope; a man arguing that the cliff could not move before midwinter because his father’s calendar said so. The adults had possessed local knowledge. Shepherds knew the high shelf had cracked. Masons knew the temple stair sat on older stone. The town cutter knew no licensed center could arrive before dawn. Each fact remained true and insufficient while families slept below the cliff.

Vessa had come from a funeral company. She wore mourning soles meant to lower a burial shelf, not hold a temple. Serad remembered her cutting those soles with a kitchen blade while people shouted six incompatible routes. She asked for volunteers. Three stepped forward. Two fled when the first house struck the cliff. Vessa did not condemn them. She took the stair because children had already reached it and no wider agreement could form in time.

After rescue, officials turned her choice into a town myth. They carved Serad at the top of the stair, though two other children had stood above him. They erased the names of those who ran. They described Vessa as appointed center, though nobody had appointed her. Serad knew every correction to the public story. He had used the story anyway because institutions required a line clean enough to teach.

At twenty, he returned to the relocated Oris households. One survivor struck him for proposing a fixed winter shelf. Another took both his hands and begged him never to let a town move unheld again. The shelf measure he designed lasted nine seasons, saved three harvest roads, and stiffened twelve workers’ feet. He recorded the injuries, increased their pensions, and built release allowances into every later score.

The improvements had seemed to prove that authority could learn. One cost named, one cut corrected, one larger field prepared. Yet each correction required another body willing to stand where uncertainty converged. The Dominion Measure grew from that accumulation, not from delight in command. He wanted to finish the sequence of preventable repairs.

Hadrun’s bell pulled at the oldest terror in him because it carried disagreement as virtue. He could admire the courage of refusal and still hear roofs breaking beneath it. Ossa Marr had taught crews to miss one beat safely. She had not answered what happened when seven provinces missed seven different beats beneath shared grain roads.

Then the Still Court came back around him: seven anchor boards, three hundred waiting company officers, lamps burning in the afternoon, and Hadrun’s broken report echoing under the dome. Ink still shone wet where a clerk had written the first refused field beside the western board.

Permanent orders exposed. Eastern grain road shifted. Provincial crews refusing command. Public bells carrying unauthenticated center projections.

A clerk waited at Serad’s elbow. “Master Chorearch?”

“Ring the Stillday signal.”

The clerk’s face changed. “Convocation stands sixteen days away.”

“I know the calendar.”

“Chancellor Miren has not transferred civil authority.”

“Ring it.”

The great bell sounded above Meren. Its note entered the floor through the capital foundation anchor. Dust loosened from the painted dome and settled on seven boards whose stones had been quarried years apart. Companies across the road web would hear what apprentices had rehearsed for three years: readiness, convergence, permanent engagement.

Serad did not confuse the decision with legality. Miren could strip his office if the roads remained connected long enough for her order to reach every station. Hadrun’s message had already damaged that connection. One province refusing rehearsal could become seven provinces improvising under public fear, each shifting grain, bridges, and borders without knowledge of the others.

Fragments, arguing while the cliff descended.

He crossed to the western board. “Call anchors one, two, and five to active hold. Remaining stations prepare, no engagement.”

Deputy Chorearch Leth did not touch the cords. “Those fields have not completed final body review.”

“They completed technical review.”

“Center complements remain below projection.”

“Use brace reserves.”

“Brace reserves increase local conversion.”

“Yes.”

Leth swallowed. “The Fifth Measure has not assembled. Eda remains under hip treatment.”

“She does not enter today. Three anchors can settle through regional centers.”

That truth cost him less to say than it should have. Eda’s skill belonged at the final convergence. He had delayed telling her the full price, then persuaded himself delay protected judgment. Her questions that morning had exposed the cowardice inside that kindness.

He still believed she would choose completion once anger ceased to govern her. He also knew he had arranged the alternatives until refusal carried other bodies.

Both things could be true. Leadership rarely offered clean truths; it required choosing which contradiction killed fewer people.

The Still Court doors opened. Chancellor Miren entered without ceremonial staff, followed by six civil guards and two grain ministers. Dust streaked the hem of her robe.

“Cancel the signal,” she said.

“It has reached the first stations.”

“Then ring false commencement.”

“Crews would break preparation at different counts.”

“You began without my seal.”

“You ordered grain penalties against Hadrun. Their refusal made gradual alignment impossible.”

“I ordered rehearsal, not permanent engagement.”

“Hadrun has published the permanent field cuts. In six days every anchor crew either defects, sabotages its sole, or locks itself before its neighbors. We cannot manage seven independent failures.”

Miren put a public broadside on the board. It carried the sole court’s distinction between proven orders and anonymous projections. “Did you author permanent instructions?”

“Yes.”

“Did you tell my office the convocation hold would release by season’s end?”

“I told your office civil transfer would begin after convergence.”

“That language allowed my clerks to write seasonal.”

“You asked for a notice that preserved markets.”

“I asked for an accurate limit.”

Serad looked at the grain ministers. “How many days in reserve after the Hadrun road shift?”

The elder minister answered reluctantly. “Capital bins, fifteen at ordinary ration. Western districts, between three and eight. Temporary crossings may extend them.”

“May.”

Miren’s anger sharpened. “Do not turn my caution into your authority.”

“I am turning a closing interval into a decision.”

“You have no center.”

“I have three hundred regional centers at their marks.”

“You do not have Eda’s consent.”

“Eda does not enter today.”

“And on convergence?”

Serad did not answer with a lie. “She has read the center cost. She requested full transition access and returned to rehearsal.”

“Request is not consent to death.”

“No.”

Miren stared at him. She had supported fixed roads for twenty-two years, signed pension restrictions, and moved villages from unstable shelves. Her objection did not make her innocent. It made the remaining difference between them decisive.

“Delay one day,” she said. “Let civil messengers authenticate Hadrun’s evidence. Let me publish rations before you freeze the alternative roads.”

“One day gives every field time to cut its anchor.”

“It gives people time to understand.”

“Oris had an entire winter of arguments. The cliff required one hour.”

“This is not Oris.”

“No. It is larger.”

He signalled Leth. The deputy pulled three anchor cords.

Far bells answered in sequences: north chalk, western pass, southern fig terraces. Each station reported salt complete, anchor present, center line under command. Serad placed both palms on the Still Court board.

The board contained stones from every field. It did not show him distant scenes. It transmitted direction and resistance through joined anchors, the way a shoe sole transmitted ground without explaining what stood upon it. Reports supplied what touch could not.

“Anchor one,” Leth called. “Opening count.”

Drums began below the court and travelled outward by company relays. Serad felt the north chalk road resist first. Its natural motion carried east and slightly upward. The permanent score opposed both directions, routing counter-motion into one hundred twelve centers along the field.

Resistance climbed.

“Center six uneven,” a caller reported.

“Shift two braces inward,” Serad said.

“Brace two already at skin limit.”

“Use reserve fourteen.”

The western board stirred. Anchor two caught a road junction near the backward mountains. The field wanted to shorten toward Hadrun. Serad held the score’s fixed length and felt pressure travel into the station stone.

He left Leth at the boards long enough to descend into the lower court. Fifty-two capital braces worked there as the central relay, each wearing soles that carried only a fraction of distant resistance. They knelt in concentric arcs around the foundation anchor. No one performed spectacle. Sweat darkened collars. Heels shook. Cutters moved between rows, widening spill channels whenever mineral pallor appeared above a shoe.

A brace called Nemei caught Serad’s sleeve. Protocol required her to address an officer, not the architect, but every officer nearby had entered count.

“My sister centers at north chalk,” she said. “Bell report named uneven six. Which six?”

“I do not know yet.”

“Can you find out before second engagement?”

“Yes.”

Serad sent a clerk to the north board and waited beside Nemei until the answer returned. Center six belonged to a quarry company, no family name yet transmitted. Not her sister.

Relief crossed Nemei’s face, followed by shame.

“Do not apologize for wanting one person spared,” Serad told her. “Return only if you can carry your assigned share.”

“Will another body carry it if I leave?”

“Yes.”

She closed her eyes. “Then I stay.”

He could have named that consent. The word felt dishonest when replacement stood behind it. Still, if every worker left because alternatives carried pressure, the anchor loads would not vanish. They would break through fewer bodies or escape into occupied roads.

At the next arc, a cutter showed him grey rising through a young man’s heel. “Release him?”

The board above signalled anchor one nearing bite. Removing the brace would increase strain on four neighbors.

“Ask him,” Serad said.

The young man heard the choice and chose release. Serad ordered two reserves to take the load before the cutter opened the sole. Both reserves received the projected strain and entered voluntarily after hearing it. The exchange cost three minutes and pushed north chalk closer to uncontrolled engagement.

Compassion had duration. Duration had bodies. This did not make compassion wrong, but it did prevent Serad from treating delay as harmless.

When he returned to the upper boards, Leth reported the north line within one count of permanent bite and anchor five requesting postponement for root resistance.

“Five may postpone four counts,” Serad said. “One proceeds. Two maintains.”

A runner entered with Eda behind him.

She had wrapped her right hip beneath the rehearsal coat. Her stride remained even by discipline, though Serad saw the shortened turn.

“You started without me,” she said.

“I kept you out of the early load.”

“You kept me out of the decision.”

Miren gave a humorless laugh. “He has been generous that way today.”

Serad did not release the board. “Three anchors only. We cannot wait while field cuts spread.”

“The public learned because you hid permanence,” Eda said.

“The public learned numbers no one has authenticated.”

“They learned your orders.”

“And Hadrun moved the grain road. Western towns lose ration tonight.”

Eda stepped close enough to read the anchor gauges. “Anchor two carries lateral strain into a pass release.”

“It carries into prepared centers.”

“Not all of it. The pass has changed since survey.”

He followed her finger. The western station stone pulled in irregular bursts rather than smooth resistance. A local fold had entered the salted field.

“Send suspension,” Eda said.

“Suspension leaves torque in anchor two and anchor one. Release requires both to reverse together.”

“Then reverse both.”

“North chalk has already taken permanent bite.”

The phrase silenced the court.

Anchor one had passed the point where ordinary release could restore prior motion. Its road lay fixed. Soil around the station would begin accepting that arrangement as strain, then as mineral binding.

The Imperial Stillness had begun.

A north runner arrived forty minutes later, boots chalk-white. “Province station reports movement ceased across the primary shelf. Survey poles have held exact marks through two local turns.”

Relief moved among company officers. Decades of drifting toll lines, collapsing retaining walls, and winter routes lost before harvest had ended on that shelf.

“What of centers?” Serad asked.

“Eleven grey ankles. Two knee conversions. No deaths.”

Below median projection.

“What of springs?” Eda asked.

The runner hesitated. “Upper spring stopped after hold. Lower channel runs at one third.”

Miren gripped the board edge. “A province cannot drink survey poles.”

“The spring may find a fixed channel,” Serad said.

“Your own projections say high springs fail first.” Eda’s voice remained quiet.

Serad heard accusation and fact. “Send water crews. Open reserve cisterns. Do not release the anchor.”

Miren ordered her grain minister to record the command under civil protest.

Anchor five engaged across the southern fig terraces. Its centers reported heavy root resistance, then steadied. Anchor two continued to jerk beneath Serad’s hands.

A western bell rang casualty sequence.

One. Pause. Four. Pause. Road closure. Pass collapse.

Leth sent three runners to meet the written report. They returned after dark. The first carried a broken field gauge. The second had blood on his sleeve that belonged to someone else. The third read while a clerk copied.

“Anchor two fixed the eastern shoulder ahead of local completion. A westbound pass continued moving and closed against the held shoulder. Twenty-six travellers caught between shelves. Four confirmed dead, nine fractures, remaining bruising and exposure. A fugitive party used a kiln drainage cut to escape before full closure.”

Serad’s hands remained on the station stones. “Names.”

“Two dead identified: Mara Ven, carter; Olin Sef, apprentice cutter. Others pending. Injured include Dena Cor, shoulder dislocation. Tavra Ilen, right ankle fracture. Ossa Marr, reopened left forearm. Ruhan Tal, no new injury reported.”

Eda made no sound. Her body changed around the silence.

“Where is Tavra?” Serad asked.

“Taken toward an abandoned glaze kiln beyond the field. Hadrun residents blocked pursuit.”

“Was the pass inside the latest station survey?”

“No. Survey used last year’s held approach.”

Leth’s face had gone grey. “We fixed against an absent road.”

“We fixed the junction the grain web requires,” Serad said.

Even to him, the sentence carried the cold of a ledger.

The report continued. Springs above the western pass had dropped within two hours. A release channel ended against newly fixed stone and flooded three kiln yards. Local road crews refused assistance until imperial centers left the anchor field.

Serad looked at the names again. Tavra’s right ankle. Eda’s mother, injured by his early order. Oris memory offered no absolution. The children on the stair had not asked Vessa to die, and Vessa’s act had not granted him ownership of every later body.

He knew that. He had always known it in private.

Outside private knowledge lay the roads. North chalk no longer moved. Releasing anchor two alone would cast its stored counter-motion into centers already carrying the fixed junction. Releasing all three might reopen the northern shelf violently, with cistern crews and residents now walking on the assumption of stability.

“What remains reversible?” he asked Eda.

She read the board despite the pain in her face. “Anchor five has not taken full bite. Two may release if one shares the return. One cannot safely return tonight.”

“And if we release five and two?”

“Western pass may reopen. Southern terraces resume. North remains fixed. The joined score loses convergence.”

“The remaining provinces act independently,” Leth said. “Hadrun refusal spreads.”

Miren faced Serad. “Take the partial outcome. One fixed shelf, one ecological crime. Do not multiply it.”

He imagined six days from now: anchor crews cutting their soles, grain roads shifting under frightened hoarding, border companies claiming passes before neighbors could respond. He imagined Oris repeated not because land moved, but because every authority waited for agreement while danger narrowed.

He also imagined Eda’s name on the center provision he had approved.

“Release five,” he said.

Eda’s shoulders lowered a fraction.

“Hold two. Prepare convergence of the remaining anchors within six days.”

Her relief vanished.

Miren stepped back as if he had struck her. “You saw the spring report.”

“Yes.”

“You saw Tavra’s injury.”

“Yes.”

“And you continue.”

“I reduce the interval in which uncoordinated holds can fail.”

“You make every refusal more dangerous, then call danger consent.”

Serad signed the continuance order. His hand remained steady because he had trained it to remain steady while other bodies shook.

Anchor five began release. Anchor two stayed fixed against the closing pass. Across the western board, pressure settled into a hard, continuous pull.

Six days remained for the other anchors, Eda’s center, and every road Serad still believed he could save.

Chapter 19 — The Step Nobody Owned

Tavra carved a master score while Dena pulled her broken ankle straight.

The pain made the cuts beautifully simple. Seven outer steps. Three spill channels. One central body receiving whatever the others could not return. By the time the Hadrun healer wrapped clay splints around the right ankle, Tavra had designed a smaller Dominion Measure on the back of a kiln invoice.

She stared at it until the lines stopped looking like rescue.

“Burn that,” she told Ruhan.

He took the invoice with his left hand. His right wrist, freed from the full splint at last, remained swollen and weak. “Any final wishes for your masterpiece?”

“May it heat tea.”

He fed it into the glaze kiln. Flame took the center first.

That design joined four others already burned. Tavra had begun the first while kiln workers carried her in from the closing pass. It assigned trained cutters to every salt gap and placed Ossa’s drum at the outer boundary. Ossa had glanced at it once.

“You made my silence a command position,” she said.

The second replaced cadence with hand signals. Ruhan pointed out that every participant still watched Tavra. The third divided authority among five local centers, each chosen by sole courts. Dena asked what happened when a village refused the court’s center. Tavra added a withdrawal clause, then saw that withdrawing one center collapsed pressure into the remaining four.

The fourth score looked different enough to deceive her for an hour. It used no named center. Each participant routed surplus to the next sole clockwise, with the final spill returning through an open edge. On paper, nobody owned the measure. In practice, the clockwise rule forced every person to accept the next body before seeing who stood there. Tavra had replaced a central master with a circle that could not say no after beginning.

She cut that page into bandage strips.
