Failure exhausted her more than drawing. Every design started from a real danger: unmatched feet colliding, salt exits closing, untrained hands cutting too deep, a field changing after agreement. Each danger invited a rule. Rules accumulated into one author, even when she removed her name.

“Maybe the College is right about one thing,” she said.

Ossa watched the fire through the stripped drum frame. “Choose carefully. I have one usable hand and can still hit you with it.”

“Complex ground-dance needs somebody to see the whole field.”

“Seeing does not grant ownership.”

“No. It grants the duty to warn.”

“Then warn.”

“And when warnings conflict?”

“People choose which risk they carry.”

“While stone moves under them?”

“Sometimes.” Ossa lifted the bandaged forearm. “Command exists because danger does not wait. That fact made it useful. It did not make what I did at Hadrun clean.”

Tavra reached for another invoice. Ruhan put the blank paper beyond her arm.

“You are resting until you can describe the problem without solving it,” he said.

“I can describe it.”

“Try.”

She looked toward the dry settling trough outside. “Anchor two has prevented the kiln field from taking its ordinary evening slope. Water cannot reach the drain. Stored pressure remains low but grows. People need the field to move without driving its hold into one person.”

“What part do you not know?”

“The final direction. The number of safe feet. Whether the shed foundation shares the hold. Which exit will remain open under runoff. How quickly local pressure changes.”

“Who knows those things?”

“Different people.”

Ruhan returned the invoice. “Then a score written before they speak begins with false ground.”

Tavra did not draw. The blank page irritated her until irritation became an admission: she had treated uncertainty as empty space awaiting her craft. Kesta treated it as a place where another person might possess the next fact.

The abandoned works stood beyond the western anchor’s fixed field. Three long sheds surrounded a yard of cracked firing stone. Springs above the pass had failed, leaving the settling troughs dry. Hadrun residents carried water from a lower channel while imperial patrols watched the main road.

Tavra lay on a door removed from its hinges. Her right ankle throbbed inside clay and willow slats. Her left knee would not bend past a shallow angle. Standing had become impossible between them.

Ossa sat nearby while a healer changed the dressing on her left forearm. The cut had reopened during the pass collapse. Her grip closed halfway and stopped. The original Nareth fragment remained in its document tube beneath her sound arm.

“Again,” Tavra said.

Ossa did not hand it over. “What will you do differently?”

“Remove the center.”

“You said that about the first three.”

“The first had a reserve center.”

“You.”

“The second divided center among cutters.”

“Chosen by you.”

“The third rotated spill.”

“Back to you at the final turn.”

Tavra resented accurate listeners.

Around them, Dena’s people prepared for the fixed field to worsen. Kiln workers shifted unfired pots off shelves that had stopped settling with the season. A miller dug beside a dry trough and found moist soil one armspan down, trapped where the held stone prevented its old upward seep. Every consequence offered Tavra another reason to hurry and another excuse to make the choice alone.

“Give me the fragment,” she said.

Ossa considered. “For how long?”

“Until you ask for it back.”

“Where does it remain?”

“On the board between us.”

“Who may alter it?”

“Nobody.”

Ossa slid the tube into the open.

Tavra unwrapped the Nareth leather without lifting it from the board. Court examiners had cleaned no oil from its surface. Serad’s cross-cut still drove through the earlier quarry turn. She traced beside the grooves with a blunt awl.

For twelve years she had treated the Nareth release as proof of sequence. Now she read it as failed craft.

Its direction belonged to an empty quarry west of the old road. Its depth assumed Tavra’s weight, her left-foot dominance, and the hillside’s first southward pull. None of that could move into another field. Copying the cut would repeat the College’s central error: mistaking one diagram for authority over changed ground.

Under those particulars lay a simpler act. The release did not force motion opposite the hold. It cut one edge free and let stored pressure choose the quarry because the quarry offered room.

“An exit without a destination,” she said.

Ruhan looked over from the kiln. “That sounds like a destination with better manners.”

“The cut names no final position. It names where the score stops claiming.”

Ossa leaned closer. “Can a sole do that without routing everything into its wearer?”

“One sole cannot.”

“Then stop drawing one.”

Tavra took a fresh sheet and divided it into sixteen sole shapes.

Ossa closed her eyes.

“What?” Tavra demanded.

“You removed one master shoe and drew sixteen obedient shoes.”

“They need compatible exits.”

“Do they need your compatibility?”

“Yes, unless you want broken legs.”

“Those are not the only choices.”

Dena entered with her left arm bound against a dislocated shoulder. “Arguing again? Good. The spring is still dry, so nobody has won.”

She set thirty-seven court rubbings beside the fragment. Each examiner had copied the cuts slightly differently. Tavra’s first instinct called twenty-nine of them errors. Then she saw what variation revealed. Where every rubbing agreed, blade sequence stood certain. Where they diverged, oil wear or torn leather made certainty false.

“Layer them,” she said.

Ruhan pinned the sheets against a kiln window. Daylight passed through thin paper. The common release turn appeared dark where all copies agreed. Its final direction spread into a fan of disputed charcoal.

Tavra marked the shared portion only. “We know the release opens here. We do not know its exact last angle.”

“The court report already says that,” Dena replied.

“I ignored the uncertainty because I remembered cutting it.”

“Memory giving itself a craft stamp.”

“Yes.”

Outside, two children greeted each other on the sloped kiln yard. Each touched one heel down, turned the toe toward the safer side of the crack, and stepped away. The motion pulled Tavra back to Kesta.

Kel had copied the village children badly at first, too eager to make their three steps match a score. A Kestan girl had laughed and reduced the greeting until he could manage it: one foot offering a direction, the next person choosing whether to answer. No joined measure. No center. Local knowledge carried in a step simple enough to refuse.

“One-step greeting,” Tavra said.

Ruhan followed her gaze. “Kesta uses dozens.”

“Show me one for approaching uncertain ground.”

He stood at the yard edge. With his good left side forward, he placed one foot near a crack and rolled weight only to the ball, leaving the heel free. “This asks whether the next surface carries downhill.”

“Who answers?”

“Whoever comes after, or nobody.”

Ossa tapped the kiln brick with one right fingertip. “No opening count.”

Tavra looked from the Nareth fragment to Ruhan’s uncommitted heel. “The sole needs only the shared opening. Each person cuts the last angle after seeing the ground.”

“Untrained people cannot cut under load,” Dena said.

“Then cutters prepare several simple endings. The participant chooses one after the field moves.”

Ossa’s stare sharpened. “Still your endings.”

“Then local cutters make them.”

“Still cutters.”

“Ground-dance requires scores.”

“It requires scored leather. It does not require one class to keep every knife.”

Tavra almost rejected that as unsafe. Kel’s quick hands returned to memory, learning shallow sole marks at Dorel without ground-dance training. Ordinary people could not design a complex measure. They could cut one disclosed notch under supervision before stepping.

“Too deep and it catches,” Tavra said. “Too shallow and it does nothing.”

Dena picked up a bark scrap. “Give us limits, not a pattern.”

They worked through afternoon.

Tavra taught knife depth by layers: score through the oil skin, stop before the pale fibre. Dena changed the explanation to a bend test because kiln workers knew clay, not leather. Ruhan insisted every trial sole carry three visible exit choices and one uncut refusal edge. Ossa rejected any drum phrase repeated enough to become command. Local drummers offered isolated sounds: rim knock, hand clap, kiln paddle on brick, or silence.

Each contribution made Tavra’s original diagram less recognizable.

The trial field lay behind the glazing shed where anchor two had fixed a patch of clay soil. Before the hold, kiln workers said the ground tilted a little south every evening, helping rainwater drain. Now it stayed level. Water from washing pots pooled against the wall.

Tavra could not inspect it standing. Workers carried her on the door and set her outside the salt boundary. She read the field through a plain leather pad beneath her left palm. The held pressure felt thin compared with a road, but continuous. Enough to bruise a few feet if released badly.

Dena invited twelve residents. Tavra objected to the word invited until Dena threatened to hit her with the good arm.

They explained the trial separately.

“The field may tilt,” said kiln worker Mavi. “Pots could fall.”

“We moved them,” another answered.

“My house wall joins that shed.”

“Then you may refuse and ask us to choose another patch,” Dena said.

Mavi refused. Two others left because permanent patrol notice threatened their grain share. Nine remained: four kiln workers, a miller, two carters, a shepherd, and the healer who had set Tavra’s ankle.

Nobody called them dancers.

Before cutting leather, each participant received a blank and made a practice notch in waste bark. Tavra demonstrated depth on her own strip from the door. She could not reach their hands and therefore could not correct by taking the knife away. Dena bent each sample until fibres showed. Ruhan asked the maker to explain which boundary route had led to that notch. If the explanation named Tavra’s instruction rather than a local feature, the sample returned to the waste pile.

The carter with the largest feet failed three times. His knife pressed through the pale fibre whenever his injured fingers tightened. He chose to let the kiln worker beside him cut the trial sole after both agreed where the notch should end.

“Does that make it her step?” he asked.

“It makes the cut hers and the choice to wear it yours,” Dena said. “If either of you dislikes that division, stop.”

They continued.

Mavi watched from beside the shed and challenged the salt boundary before anyone laid it. The proposed southern gap crossed her handcart lane at an angle likely to trap a wheel if the ground tilted. She required it widened by half a cart. Tavra had not known the cart carried glaze jars at dusk because no imperial map marked work hours.

That correction altered two participant notches. Both were recut with fresh leather rather than patched. The discarded soles went into a marked basket so nobody could later mistake them for usable patterns.

Each chose a shallow greeting sole cut from local bark leather. Each sole opened at one shared point modeled on the Nareth release, then offered different outward notches. Participants selected the final notch after walking the field boundary in ordinary shoes. Ruhan recorded which routes they refused. No mark indicated a central position.

Tavra described the known cost. “Likely calf pull. Possible ankle bruising. If more than two people answer the same movement, pressure may gather. Take one step only. You may remove the sole before or during the trial. If the field does not move, we accept failure.”

“What happens if it moves too far?” the healer asked.

“Open salt on all four sides lets pressure leave. Trained cutters stand outside with release knives. I am not a fault path.”

Ossa looked at her.

Tavra corrected herself. “Nobody has agreed to serve as a fault path. If pressure gathers, everyone exits. We do not save the trial.”

That passed.

Step-salt outlined an uneven circle with gaps at every path residents needed. Tavra’s craft instinct wanted clean arcs. Mavi, watching from the shed, demanded another gap for a handcart although she had refused participation. They opened it.

“Who begins?” asked a carter.

“No one,” Ossa said. “Stand outside until you choose.”

For several breaths, nobody moved.

Tavra felt urgency claw at her. Anchor two remained fixed. Serad had six days. Eda remained inside Meren. She could tell the shepherd his chosen notch best matched the field. She could begin the trial with one sentence.

She bit the sentence closed.

The miller entered first. He placed his greeting foot and rolled weight toward a notch pointing at the dry trough. The ground gave a faint scrape.

He stepped out.

No drum followed.

A kiln worker chose a different edge. Her one step opened a hairline crack beside the pooled washwater. The shepherd waited until the water seeped into it, then selected his uphill notch. Pressure changed beneath Tavra’s palm.

The field began to tilt south.

Not smoothly. One corner stayed fixed against the glazing shed. Two carters entered at once, saw each other, and both withdrew before taking weight. The healer chose silence as her signal and stepped toward the stubborn corner. Clay trembled under her heel.

A sharp pull crossed the field. Three participants gasped.

“Out if you choose,” Tavra called.

One left. Two remained without repeating.

Dena asked, “Need a release cut?”

Tavra examined the pressure through her palm. The old answer would have sent a cutter in to correct the corner. “Ask the people beside it.”

Mavi, still outside, knelt by the shed. “That foundation has a drain stone under the plaster. We blocked it last winter.”

“Will you open it?” Dena asked.

“My wall. My choice.” Mavi took a hammer to the plaster, not a scored sole. The old drain gap appeared.

Water ran.

The stubborn corner lowered without another step. Released pressure left through the salt gaps and drain rather than entering a center. The yard settled on its old southern tilt. Nothing dramatic moved beyond it. Nine people stood with bruised calves, muddy shoes, and no mineral pallor.

The held field had returned to motion.

Tavra waited for triumph and found inspection instead. One greeting sole had split too deep. The shepherd’s notch had contributed nothing. Two salt exits had merged under runoff, which could prove dangerous at larger scale. Participants argued whether the drain opening, not their steps, had done the decisive work.

“All of it did,” Tavra said, then stopped. “That is inference. We would need another field to know.”

“What do we call the packet?” Dena asked.

“Do not make a master copy,” Tavra said.

“A name is not a master copy.”

“It can become one.”

Ruhan wrote on his atlas margin: OPEN: NINE STEPS, ONE DRAIN, FOUR SALT GAPS USED, THREE REFUSALS, NO CENTER. ASK AGAIN.

Ossa read it. “Open Measure.”

The phrase moved through the circle without a proclamation. A measure with boundaries open to ground and choice. A practice whose final shape could not be cut before local people entered it.

Tavra looked at the rejected diagrams burning in the kiln. “Then mark every copy incomplete.”

Dena ordered the trial sheets divided among participants. Tavra kept none.

At dusk, workers carried her door back inside. Her right ankle had swollen against the clay splints. The left knee burned from reading the field through her palm. Neither pain dimmed the calculation she had avoided all afternoon.

The new practice could distribute release. It could not enter the Dominion center quickly enough to spare Eda. Someone trained would still have to take that position while others broke the anchors.

Tavra asked Ossa to close the kiln door.

“When we reach Meren,” she said, “Eda leaves center. I take her place.”

Ossa’s wounded hand rested on the document tube. “Did today teach you nothing?”

“It taught me not to own everyone’s step.”

“You still think you own hers.”

“No.” Tavra looked at the splinted ankle that could not bear weight, then at the score knife beside her. “I think I can offer a replacement she may refuse.”

“And if she refuses to leave?”

Tavra had no answer she trusted.

Outside, the kiln yard continued its slow southward settling. Workers moved the water trough to follow it while nine trial soles travelled away in nine separate hands.

Chapter 20 — When Eda Chose the Drum

Eda pressed her burned thumb into the chalk mark and made it crooked.

The apprentices circling the eastern anchor copied every line she drew. They had been trained to regard precision as safety, and none of them looked closely enough to see that her third curve opened toward the release aisle instead of the center. Their soles whispered over the black floor of the Still Court. Each foot landed with the careful confidence of a body taught that obedience and balance meant the same thing.

Serad watched from the first gallery, one hand resting on Folio Seven.

The Court had no roof. Dawn had not reached it, but the administrative hill threw back enough lantern light to reveal the seven salted fields arranged around the central disc. Three anchor stones already sat in their iron cradles. Their holds had begun two days ahead of the public promise. Eda felt them through the right hip, three quiet pressures that never eased when she shifted her weight.

“Again,” Serad called.

Eda raised two fingers. The eastern company reset.

She gave them the opening count with her heel. Sixteen trained bodies moved. Their first turn drew counter-motion toward her, a familiar pressure entering along the scored channels of her soles. On the fifth beat she shortened her left step by half a thumb. The error traveled outward. It did not break the measure. Instead, it sent a thin share of strain into the empty release aisle and left a pale crack in the salt.

One apprentice glanced down.

“Keep your eyes level,” Eda said.

The girl obeyed. Eda hated how quickly she did it.

By the twelfth beat, the eastern anchor had shifted in its cradle. No more than the width of a fingernail, but Serad saw. He descended the gallery stairs without haste.

“Your hip,” he said when the company halted. “You are protecting it.”

“I am using it.”

“You opened the fifth line.”

“Salt settled into an old seam.”

Serad crouched and touched the crooked mark. His fingertip came away black. “The floor under this Court has been held since my predecessor’s childhood. It has no old seam.”

“Then we have made one.”

A few apprentices looked at Eda. She kept her shoulders loose. Serad could punish insolence, but he could not punish a center for describing the ground under her feet. Not in front of people who would have to trust her body at dawn.

He stood. “Clear the field.”

The company filed into the north passage. Eda remained inside the salt. Leaving without a release would drive the unfinished turn into their ankles. She drew the strain back through her right side, waited until pain shone white behind her eyes, then stepped out on the heel.

Serad caught her elbow.

“You have slept four hours in three nights.”

“You advanced the rehearsal.”

“I advanced necessity.” His grip gentled without loosening. “The Hadrun roads have ceased receiving command. Two grain trains are stranded. Three towns have opened arguments over crossings that kept them alive all winter. You know what follows when a road becomes a vote held during hunger.”

Eda looked toward Folio Seven. He had tied its cover shut with red cord, though he no longer pretended its center pages held nothing. “I know what follows when the person counting leaves bodies out of the number.”

His face tightened. Not anger. Hurt would have been easier to dismiss if it had been false, but Serad had praised her first clean center, sat beside her fever bed after a hill measure, and taught her how to fall without feeding a fracture into the ground. His care had always contained a use. That did not make the care imaginary.

“The body projections account for every trained participant,” he said.

“They account for us as material.”

“They account for a cost I would carry myself if skill permitted.”

“No. You would choose it yourself. That changes the count.”

He released her arm. “Rest until second bell.”

“Will the western company rehearse?”

“They can work without a center.”

“That is not what I asked.”

Serad’s gaze moved to the cracked salt line. “I will center them.”

His shoulder had begun to slope since the first anchors engaged. He believed this answer proved something. Eda could not decide whether it proved love, vanity, or merely the habit of putting his own body at the top of an arithmetic he had written for everyone else.

She bowed as a Fifth Measure center should. Then she walked to the robing chamber without limping until the door shut behind her.

Inside, she took off her right shoe.

The lining had rubbed a raw stripe along her arch. Beneath it, cut into a thin piece of goat leather, lay the copied page from Folio Seven. She had written small enough to preserve the seven counter-motion columns, the estimates for the center company, and the projected sterility of three provinces. A line of sweat had blurred the figure beside Nareth, but the words permanent root fixation remained clear.

She put the leather against her ribs. The robing chamber had one narrow window above head height and two guards outside. Three weeks earlier she would have considered those facts proof that leaving could not be done. Tavra would have treated them as a sole problem. Ossa would have listened for a weak beat. Ruhan would have asked why anyone assumed the door deserved to remain where it stood.

Eda had spent years refusing to become her mother’s copy. She saw now how refusal could imitate its object. Tavra fled the College because every corridor told her where to put her weight. Eda had remained because Tavra’s leaving told her the same thing.

A drum sounded beneath the western gallery.

Not the broad public voice of a complete field drum. This beat came thin and dry through the stone, struck on a rim or an inner skin. Two quick taps, a rest, then one.

Eda held still.

The cadence belonged to no current College instruction. It did belong to an old correction sequence, one Ossa Marr had used when apprentices crowded a center’s blind side. Eda remembered Ossa at seventeen, unsmiling beside a practice drum, making the whole company repeat a turn because Eda had landed beautifully and dangerously.

Two quick. Rest. One.

A question.

Eda crossed to the water basin and lifted its bronze cup. The guards heard her drink. She set the cup down on the second beat, dragged it once against the basin after the rest, then struck it twice.

Not yet. Eastern passage.

Silence followed.

She wondered whether she had imagined the rhythm. Then a heel crossed the corridor outside in Ossa’s old walking cadence, deliberately uneven. The sound passed the door and faded north.

Eda retied the shoe. Choosing Ossa felt like stepping onto a bridge built by someone who had once used rhythm to march people into death. It also meant trusting a woman who had confessed what she had done and changed how she carried the drum. Serad had confessed costs only after Eda found them, then preserved the score.

At second bell, a guard escorted her back to the Court.

The western company had taken their marks. Serad stood at center with Folio Seven open on a stone lectern. Sweat darkened the collar of his gray practice coat. His left shoulder shook once before he quieted it.

“You will observe,” he said.

Eda looked at the western anchor. “The cradle has turned.”

“A settlement correction.”

“It points inward.”

“The foundation lines converge here.”

Every Court score converged at the central disc, but this cradle pointed beneath it rather than toward it. A second pull waited under the marked ground, faint as a held breath. Eda tested the floor with her heel. Serad shut the folio.

“Gallery,” he said.

She climbed six steps. From there she could see the whole western field and the eastern release aisle she had opened. She could also see the service gate behind the drum alcove. Its hinges had been removed for dawn’s processional banners.

Serad began the count.

The company turned. His first landing came true. So did his second. He carried the three engaged holds in his shoulder and still found enough balance to receive the western anchor. Admiration rose in Eda before anger could stop it. Skill remained skill even when put to a cruel purpose.

On beat eight, the hidden pull beneath center tightened.

Serad’s knee bent. He corrected. The company continued.

Eda counted the intervals. The extra anchor answered every sixteenth beat, too deep to hear through ordinary soles. Folio Seven’s hidden center sequence had not described it. Serad had added a restraint after she opened the stitched pages. A center trying to use the public release would remain attached to the foundation.

No center without an exit.

Tavra’s maxim had irritated Eda because it arrived disguised as craft law. Now Eda understood the error beneath her anger. An exit given by Tavra could become another mother’s order. An exit cut by Eda belonged to her choice.

She descended the gallery.

Serad saw her movement and snapped, “Stay back.”

The command cadence entered her before thought. Her trained body halted.

From beneath the eastern passage came two quick taps.

Ossa struck again, not louder, only wrong against Serad’s count. Eda’s conditioned muscles reached for both rhythms and found they could obey neither without choice. The instant of confusion opened a space no command owned.

Eda stepped into it.

She crossed the edge of the western salt and took the empty seventeenth position. The apprentices faltered when they saw her. She gave them a training signal with her left hand: continue the figure, follow local center.

“What are you doing?” Serad demanded.

“Correcting the cradle.”

“You are not scored for this field.”

She set her right sole across the inward line. Pain bit the stress line in her hip. “Neither is the foundation anchor.”

His face changed. He knew she had felt it.

Eda turned on the cracked edge of her sole. The copied leather stayed warm against her ribs. The western company completed beat twelve. She had four beats before the hidden pull tightened again.

At thirteen, she drove her heel into the cradle’s inner rim.

At fourteen, she took Serad’s share of the western counter-motion for one breath. His body rose as the pressure left it.

At fifteen, she kicked the anchor stone sideways.

The cradle rang.

At sixteen, the eastern crack she had prepared opened across the floor.

Counter-motion poured into the unused aisle. Salt jumped in a black sheet. Every apprentice staggered, but none fell. Eda released on her left heel and shouted, “Out by pairs. Do not run.”

Serad lunged for the anchor. The western stone had not broken, and the measure had not failed. It had merely ceased to point inward. He could restore it after the company cleared.

That distinction kept Eda from becoming the killer his notices would name.

She reached the drum alcove. A gray-haired cleaner held the service gate open. Ossa did not stand there. Eda had expected her, and the disappointment nearly cost a step.

The cleaner shoved a folded practice coat at her. “Your drummer says the north lane is too obvious.”

“Which drummer?”

“A bad one, by the sound of her.”

Eda put on the coat and pulled its hood over her hair. Behind her, Serad called her name once. No rank. No command. The sound carried the same fear he had shown when an apprentice fell from a practice ridge years ago.

She chose the gate anyway.

The service road climbed behind the College kitchens, narrowed between archive walls, then descended toward the kiln quarter. Eda walked until the Court bells began. At the first crossing, a vegetable cart blocked two College riders. At the second, three washerwomen argued over a wheel placed squarely in the lane. Each delay looked ordinary. Together they made a route.

At the dye court, she found six eastern apprentices waiting beside vats of blue wool. The youngest still wore rehearsal shoes dusted with salt. Eda reached for the alley knife hidden in her sleeve, then saw that none of them carried binding cord.

“You ordered us out by pairs,” the youngest said. “We went out.”

“You should return before Serad counts the company.”

“He counted us all morning.” A broad dancer named Pellis pulled up one trouser leg. A dark mineral bruise crossed his ankle. “He did not count this.”

Eda knew each of their placements. Pellis held the weak side of every western turn. Laro lost breath after long figures but concealed it by volunteering for salt work. The youngest, Anet, landed late whenever a drum sounded beyond her sight. Eda had corrected those faults until the dancers could hide them from anyone who did not know where to look.

“Leaving now may cost your licenses,” she said. “Your rooms belong to the College. Your meal chits stop if your names enter the discipline book. The road companies may refuse unlicensed feet.”

“We know,” Anet said.

“No. You know the words. Tonight you may have nowhere to sleep.”

Pellis looked toward the ringing Court. “Will you?”

Eda could have lied and turned their courage into cover. Instead she said, “I have a route for this hour. Nothing past it.”

Two apprentices stepped back. One began crying without noise, wiped her face, and headed toward the College. Eda did not call her coward. Three more followed after a whispered argument. Anet and Pellis remained.

“You may leave us too,” Eda said.

“That sounds different now,” Pellis replied.

It did. Serad used consequence as a wall around every offered choice. Tavra sometimes used love the same way. Eda had no clean country to invite these dancers into, only a kiln yard and a measure still being invented.

“Go to the southern bathhouse,” she said. “Ask for the woman who repairs furnace shoes. Tell her the western cradle points inward. Do not mention me. If she has no safe bed, you decide again.”

Anet nodded. Pellis did not. “And you?”

“I decide again at the next crossing.”

They separated before a rider entered the dye court. Eda ducked behind hanging wool. Blue water dripped onto her practice coat, marking it with stripes no Fifth Measure center would wear. When the rider passed, she followed the dripping color toward the moving wall.

Ossa waited beneath a stair where the moving wall had left a wedge of shadow. Her drum hung from one strap. The outer face remained open to the air, and the inner skin carried old cuts that had once meant forced pace. Her bandaged left forearm rested against the rim.

“You kept me waiting,” Ossa said.

“You chose the wrong gate.”

“I chose three. You chose one of them.”

Eda stopped an arm’s length away. “Did Tavra send you?”

“No.”

The answer came without decoration.

“Did she know you were coming?”

“She knew I meant to listen near the Court. She did not know what I would hear.” Ossa tilted her head toward Eda’s coat. “You left center?”

“I left Serad’s center. I may enter another.”

“Your mother will dislike that distinction.”

“My mother dislikes distinctions she cannot cut into leather.”

Ossa’s mouth shifted. “She has improved by one distinction since yesterday.”

“What distinction?”

“Asked and told.”

Eda wanted proof before hope. “Take me to her.”

They moved with the wall’s afternoon adjustment. Ruhan waited at the wall shrine, his stiff right wrist tied close under his coat. He opened a rear panel and showed Eda a crawlspace behind the civic stones.

“The capital believes a shrine stays sacred by staying put,” he said. “This one has traveled farther than most couriers.”

“Where does it go?”

“Today? Kiln quarter. Tomorrow is an ambitious question.”

The passage smelled of old wax and stone dust. Eda crawled with her right leg extended where she could, though the hip still caught. Ossa followed, dragging the drum carefully. Ruhan closed the panel behind them.

They emerged in a kiln yard after sunset.

A circle of scuffed earth lay between stacked bricks. Its salt line had been swept away, but the ground inside had softened into a shallow, uneven roll. People had practiced there recently. Not College dancers. The footprints varied too much in size and skill, and most showed one step followed by withdrawal.

Tavra sat beside the kiln with her fractured ankle splinted forward. Her left knee looked swollen beneath the trouser cloth. She had a cutting board across her lap and a child’s plain work shoe braced against it. A small field beyond the yard moved in a slow ripple, no longer held flat.

For a moment neither of them spoke.

Eda had rehearsed this encounter in locked rooms. In some versions she threw Folio Seven at Tavra. In others Tavra apologized with such precision that the twelve lost years rearranged themselves. The actual woman looked exhausted, gray at the mouth, and fiercely alert to Eda’s weight.

“You’re limping,” Tavra said.

Eda laughed once. “I crossed half the capital by a shrine that thinks it is a corridor, and you begin with my gait.”

Tavra set the shoe down. “You’re here.”

“I left.”

“Did Ossa pull you out?”

“No.”

“Serad release you?”

“No.”

Tavra absorbed both answers. “Then you chose.”

“That should not surprise you as much as it does.”

“It does not surprise me.”

“It frightens you.”

“Yes.”

The honesty checked Eda’s next accusation. Tavra had once hidden fear under departure. Now she sat without a road and named it.

Eda took the copied page from inside her coat. “Folio Seven. Center projections, province loss, all seven anchor columns. The line beside Nareth blurred.”

Tavra reached for it, then stopped before touching. “May I?”

Eda placed it in her palm.

Tavra read. Ossa crouched nearby, favoring her left arm. Ruhan watched the yard entrance. The kiln workers who had practiced the one-step circle gave them distance without pretending not to listen.

At the column for the capital foundation, Tavra’s thumb went still. “He tied the center release to this?”

“He added a second anchor under the Court after I opened the folio. I felt it during rehearsal.”

“Can you cut free?”

“I opened one aisle today.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It is mine.”

Tavra looked up. The old reflex crossed her face before she spoke: measuring Eda’s hip, the route, the cost, arranging all of them into a plan without asking. “I can take your place. The center sequence began from my Nareth work. My body already carries the corresponding joins.”

“No.”

“If I wear the copied cut and enter before dawn, Serad may accept the substitution long enough for you to reach the foundation anchor.”

“No.”

“The counter-motion will recognize an experienced center.”

“It will break you before the second folio.”

“That may still leave enough time.”

Eda crossed the yard and knocked the cutting board from Tavra’s lap. The child’s shoe fell into the dust. Tavra flinched, not from anger but because twisting to catch it sent pain through her ankle.

“You do not get to turn dying into an apology,” Eda said.

“I am trying to keep you alive.”

“You keep saying that as though it answers who decides how.”

“The projections say your soles will take the first burn. Your hip is already stressed. The center column ends in a blank because they do not know whether any body can survive it.”

“And your answer is to put your body in the blank.”

“I know how to release an anchor.”

“So do I.”

“You know the center.”

“Yes.” Eda’s voice rose. “I know it better than you do now. I know Serad’s revisions, the apprentices, the Court floor, the foundation pull. You know Nareth. You know cutting. You know how to listen when ground changes. Why must your skill erase mine before you can call it protection?”

Tavra’s hands closed around the copied page. Eda saw how carefully she kept from tearing it.

“Because I left you there,” Tavra said.

The yard seemed to contract around the words.

“I left you with him. Every time I imagine you at center, I see the morning I walked out and told myself the College would keep you fed. If I stand aside now, it feels like doing it again.”

“You cannot repair abandonment by taking away the door I choose.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

Tavra bent to retrieve the small shoe. Her ankle prevented her from reaching. Eda picked it up first and set it on the board.

“I know enough to hear the question,” Tavra said. “Not enough to stop wanting the wrong answer.”

That sounded closer to truth than an apology polished for forgiveness.

Eda sat on an overturned brick opposite her. “I will go back to the Still Court.”

Tavra’s face emptied.

“I will enter center because companies are already attached to three holds. If I vanish, Serad will put an injured apprentice there or carry it himself until he falls. I will not save myself by passing the load to someone who has less choice.”

“Then let me enter beside you.”

“No. You cannot stand.”

“I can cut from the ground.”

“Good. Cut the capital anchor.” Eda pointed to the copied column. “Not my role. Yours.”

Ossa leaned over the leather. “The count reaches the foundation every sixteenth beat.”

“I can break Serad’s command cadence there,” Eda said. “Not forever. Long enough to open the exit.”

Ruhan came closer. “The wall shrine reaches the administrative slope before dawn if the wall keeps its usual turn. I can bring cutters through. I cannot promise the wall will cooperate with an imperial schedule.”

He unfolded a strip from his incomplete atlas. Instead of a line, it showed three possible approaches and two broad blank spaces. One route crossed a bakery court; another depended on a retaining stair that might move before dawn. The last stopped at a question mark beside the foundation sheds.

“Why leave this empty?” Eda asked.

“Because I have not walked it today.” Ruhan tapped the blank with his stiff knuckle. “A route I pretend to know becomes an order for somebody whose feet meet the mistake.”

Eda studied the unfilled paper. Serad’s folios had no blanks. Tavra’s copied page preserved columns precise enough to conceal whom they spent. This uncertain strip offered less comfort and more honesty.

“Do not promise the wall,” Tavra said. “Give us the route while it exists.”

Eda looked at her mother.

Tavra drew a slow breath. “You enter center with an exit you cut. I reach the foundation anchor and release it when you ask. Ossa breaks the conditioned count. Ruhan opens a route for those who choose to come. No substitution.”

“No hidden cost.”

Tavra placed the copied page flat between them. “No hidden cost.”

“And if I tell you to wait, you wait.”

Pain and fear moved across Tavra’s face. Neither disappeared. “I wait.”

The words did not heal the years between them. They made a plan possible inside those years.

Eda took the child’s shoe from the board. A single curved score crossed its sole, simple enough for an untrained foot to follow. The heel mark opened outward.

“No center without an exit,” she said.

This time the maxim did not belong to Tavra. Eda set the shoe beside the copied page, and together they began cutting two different routes toward the same dawn.

Chapter 21 — The City That Would Not Hold

The grain wardens had chained Veyr’s storehouse doors before breakfast.

Ruhan arrived while two bakers argued through the bars with a clerk holding the imperial order. The road beyond the square ran straight east between planted stones, pale and empty under a hard morning sky. Three grain carts waited on the far ridge. Their drivers had been forbidden to cross until Veyr renewed its civic hold.

A city could starve within sight of flour. Ruhan had seen mountains do less cruel things by accident.

He set his incomplete atlas on a rain barrel and studied the square. The civic anchor stood in a waist-high shrine between the storehouse and the eastern road. Step-salt boxed it inside a field broad enough for forty trained dancers. Imperial crews had left the lines ready, along with an order that gave Veyr until noon.

“We hold, they open the stores,” said Council Speaker Jorren. “We refuse, the grain goes south.”

A woman in a miller’s apron pointed at the locked doors. “That grain belongs to us already.”

“The seals belong to the road office.”

“Then feed your children wax.”

The crowd answered with rough approval. Jorren flinched. He wore no College badge, only a city cord and the sleepless look of someone asked to choose which neighbors would hate him by dusk.

Tavra sat on a cart at the edge of the square, her right ankle splinted and her cutting board across her knees. Ossa stood beside her damaged drum. Every movement of her left arm came carefully. Two Hadrun cutters laid plain shoes on a blanket, none paired for a full measure.

Ruhan watched people notice the insufficiency. A trained company had soles for every foot and a center prepared to carry the ground’s answer. Tavra had brought single shoes, scraps of felt, three jars of salt, and a practice that had moved one small field outside a kiln.

Jorren saw it too. “You promised an alternative.”

“I promised a trial,” Tavra said.

“Trials happen after the stores open.”

“Then hold the road.”

The square quieted.

Tavra did not soften her voice. “Use the imperial score. Put your road company at center. Renew the hold. You will have grain today and more stone in their joints tomorrow. The soil beyond the eastern marker will keep hardening. I won’t call that choice ignorance after Hadrun read the ledgers.”

A road-company veteran pushed through the crowd. His ankles bowed outward above polished boots. “And your trial?”

“The road may move.”

“How far?”

“We do not know.”

“Will the storehouse remain reachable?”

“Its final position remains unknown.”

“Then what do you know?” the veteran asked.

Tavra turned one unfinished shoe so its shallow groove caught the light. “We know this cut asks one foot for less than a common stair. We know a participant can withdraw before weight crosses the hip. We know an open salt boundary releases torque better than an imperial box. We know the anchor may answer unevenly, so trained cutters must stand at every gap. We know nobody in the field should be promised safety we have not earned.”

“You brought uncertainty to a hungry city.”

“Serad brought certainty with the injuries written on hidden pages.”

The veteran touched one bowed ankle. “My company knows those injuries without pages.”

“Then help us recognize the first bad turn.”

He studied her for a long moment. “I will not center.”

“I am not asking you to.”

“I will watch the north edge. If your salt begins closing behind anyone, I break it.”

Tavra offered him the shoe. “Agreed.”

Murmurs rose. Ruhan heard fear change direction. Tavra had learned to disclose cost, but her bluntness could leave people staring at an empty place where confidence ought to stand.

He opened his atlas to the page he had drawn while entering Veyr. “The storehouse has three approaches. East road, dye alley, river stair. If the anchor flexes by less than a house length, two approaches remain. If it moves farther, we negotiate a crossing after the trial.”

“Negotiate with whom?” Jorren asked.

“The people standing on each side of it.”

“That is not a road office.”

“No. A road office has chained your breakfast.”

The bakers laughed first. Jorren did not, but his shoulders lowered.

A courier came running from the western lane, one plain shoe tied to his chest. He had changed horses twice and lost one sleeve to briars. Tavra cut the cord while he drank from a public ladle.

Inside the shoe, a shallow curve crossed the sole from outer heel to first toe. The cutter had left the leather thick around it, safe against unintended turns. Kel’s name appeared in tiny letters beside a repair mark.

Tavra pressed her thumb into the groove. Something in her face eased. “He widened the release.”

“Is that good?” Jorren asked.

“It means a frightened foot can stop early without catching its weight.”

The veteran picked up the shoe. “A child cut this?”

“An apprentice,” Tavra said. “No ground-dance training.”

Jorren looked from the shoe to the waiting carts. “Tell them what happens if it fails.”

Tavra nodded to Ossa.

Ossa struck the bare rim once. Its thin note reached the whole square because everyone had stopped talking.

“This is not command cadence,” she said. “I will give a starting interval, then each section keeps its own count. You may leave before the step. You may leave after placing your foot but before giving weight. If a leader tells you that departure breaks the measure, that leader is lying. A trained cutter will manage the salt. Tavra will receive only the anchor’s immediate turn from a seated brace. No permanent center carries the field.”

“What can it do to us?” called the miller.

“Bruise a sole. Turn an ankle. Drop you if your neighbors rush. A bad release can put torque into the road and throw loose stone.” Ossa rested her weak forearm on the rim. “The College score would place greater known harm into fewer trained bodies. This trial distributes smaller risks among volunteers and returns the rest to the Deep Step.”

Someone shouted, “And if the grain leaves?”

“Then we have not solved grain,” Ruhan said. “We have only refused to purchase it with hidden bodies. The carts will still need a route.”

People began choosing.

Some went directly to the salt field. Others backed away. A cooper demanded to read the Nareth projections before deciding. Tavra handed him the copied sheets carried by the sole courts, not Eda’s original leather page. Three road-company dancers volunteered, then removed their licensed shoes and took plain wrappings so nobody would mistake their skill for a requirement.

Ruhan walked the boundary with Jorren. His stiff right wrist ached when he drove chalk pegs, so a baker’s daughter pushed them for him. They marked gaps every eight paces. No part of the circle could close people in.

“The imperial field has one entrance,” Jorren said.

“An entrance becomes a trap when everybody must use it to leave.”

“You have an answer for every line.”

“I come from a country that distrusts lines.”

Training began in the square outside the salt.

Tavra demonstrated from the cart. She could not put weight on her right ankle, so she used two wooden models strapped at the knee. One showed Kel’s curve. The other showed the release, a simple withdrawal that returned weight to the unmarked foot.

“Do not dance,” she told them. “Take one ordinary step. Feel where your balance wants to go. If the score asks farther than your body agrees, stop.”

A fish seller raised his hand. “How do I know what my body agrees?”

“You have been standing in markets longer than some licensed centers have been alive. You know when a board tips under a barrel. Use that knowledge.”

The first practice line stepped on bare ground. Ossa gave no beat. People moved at their own pace, laughing when neighbors leaned into each other. Ruhan watched the laughter carefully. It could hide embarrassment and make a refusal harder. When a bent old woman missed the curve, he stopped the line rather than turn her mistake into entertainment.

“You did nothing wrong,” he said. “The cut did not fit your foot.”

Tavra looked at the sole, then shaved the outer groove wider. “Try it without weight.”

The woman did. Her heel found the turn.

A second group practiced with Ossa’s starting interval. She tapped three uneven notes and fell silent. Each cluster spoke its own count afterward. The square filled with overlapping voices. No cadence traveled far enough to command everyone.

Ruhan took six volunteers on a walk beyond the field. He made them trace the storehouse approaches without looking at the imperial road markers. A potter found a narrow passage behind the bathhouse. Two sisters who delivered charcoal knew a sloped yard broad enough for handcarts but not wagons. Jorren dismissed both routes as informal until Ruhan asked who used them every day.

“People without road seals,” Jorren said.

“Then they already know how a city survives when its official road refuses them.”

They added both approaches to a chalk board beside the salt. Residents corrected distances and named gates that locked after dusk. Each correction changed the trial’s meaning. They were not merely preparing to move stone. They were deciding how to keep bread reachable if stone moved beyond the plan.

At the practice line, a young father removed his wrapping after three attempts. His son begged him to stay because neighbors were watching.

“I lean too far on the cut,” the father said.

Tavra inspected his stance. “I can deepen the heel.”

“No. I do not want my foot in it.”

She returned the wrapping without argument. “Then stand outside the west gap and keep children clear.”

The man accepted that work. His son scowled until Ossa asked him to carry water to the cutters. Ruhan watched shame dissolve into other useful tasks. A field that honored refusal needed places for refusers to remain part of the city. Otherwise departure became exile by another name.

Before the live trial, Jorren read the known risks aloud from the storehouse steps. He also read the right to stop. The wording sounded stiff and legal, but people interrupted until every phrase made sense. One woman asked whether leaving after the anchor moved would endanger those who remained.

“It may change their balance,” Tavra said. “That is why each section has a cutter watching. Your body does not become their property because they prefer the count unchanged.”

By late morning, twenty-six people stood inside the field. More than twice that number watched from outside. The road-company veteran took the place nearest the anchor but wore no center sole. Tavra’s cart sat just beyond the northern gap, with a broad scored board under her left foot. Two cutters held the board so she could receive a brief change without standing.

Ruhan walked to the eastern road. The grain carts remained on the ridge. Their drivers watched.

Jorren joined him. “If the road turns away, they cannot reach us.”

“Ask whether they will move.”

“They have orders.”

“So do you.”

The speaker rubbed both hands over his face. Then he lifted the city horn and called across the distance. “If our road shifts, will you bring the carts by another approach?”

The first driver cupped her hands. “Who pays the extra day?”

Veyr’s miller shouted from the field, “I will.”

“You do not own the mills,” Jorren said.

“I own one. The other owners can decide whether to look cheap in front of breakfast.”

Three mill owners began arguing. They reached terms before Ossa finished checking her rim ties.

Ruhan returned to the salt. He took no scored shoe. His work belonged outside, where he could watch approaches and call a halt if the city cut itself off.

Ossa raised her right hand.

“Beginning interval only,” she said.

The rim sounded three times.

Voices answered from four sections, none together. People bent their knees. Bare heels and plain soles touched Kel’s curved marks.

At first, nothing happened.

A child outside the field complained that everyone looked foolish. Then the civic anchor clicked against its shrine.

The eastern road lifted by a finger’s width.

Tavra’s left leg jerked. She gripped the cart rail but did not pull the motion inward. “Let it pass,” she called.

The veteran nearest the shrine began a trained correction. His body knew how to seize the change and hold it. He stopped himself before the second foot landed.

Around him, one-step participants gave weight at different moments. Each small turn opened and released. The road shifted north.

Paving stones rose in a slow diagonal. A stack of empty baskets toppled. Jorren shouted for the southern section to hold.

Ossa struck her rim once, a warning rather than command. “No holding. Choose your release.”

Four people stepped out. Their departure opened a gap in the local count. The fish seller looked ready to flee, then followed his own scored curve and withdrew cleanly.

The anchor rolled within its shrine.

Ruhan ran east. His right wrist knocked against his chest, but his feet found the moving seams. The grain road no longer pointed at the storehouse doors. It bent toward the dye alley, where a brick wall blocked the final approach.

“Clear the blue yard,” he shouted. “We need a cart width.”

Dyers hauled racks aside. A landlord protested until the first paving stone entered his courtyard. Ruhan knelt and felt the direction through his palm. The road wanted the lower grade by the river, not the storehouse square.

He could have called that wrong. An imperial map would have.

“Open the rear gate,” he told the landlord.

“That gate enters my private court.”

“For how long?”

The man stared at him.

“Name a term,” Ruhan said. “Name a price. Refuse if you choose, and we find another route.”

Behind them, the field voices broke into frightened fragments. Tavra called releases one by one. No single count controlled the whole circle. The road continued turning.

The landlord looked at the grain carts, the chained storehouse, and the paving seam approaching his wall. “Until sunset. No storage. City repairs the hinge.”

Jorren, breathless from the square, said, “Agreed.”

They unbarred the rear gate.

The road settled through the dye yard and stopped short of the river stair. It no longer ran straight, but it connected the eastern ridge to the storehouse by a turn through private ground. The distance grew by a quarter mile.

At the field, the last participant released.

Tavra sagged against the cart. Gray dust marked the cloth over her left knee. Ossa crouched beside her but did not touch until Tavra nodded. The civic anchor rested at a new angle. Soil inside the salt had cracked in soft ridges rather than flattening under a permanent hold.

Jorren looked along the altered road. “Did we succeed?”

A storehouse warden arrived with the imperial seal keys. “The order requires a renewed hold.”

“No hold,” Jorren said.

“Then the stores remain closed.”

The miller held up a hammer. “The hinges are city iron.”

Ruhan stepped between them before refusal became a broken door and a prison list. “The grain carts have a negotiated crossing. Let the drivers sell from the dye yard. The storehouse dispute can wait until people have eaten.”

The first cart entered under the landlord’s gate. Its wheels followed the new road without slipping. People cheered, but not everyone joined. Several stared at displaced market stalls and the cracked field. The Open Measure had not preserved the city unchanged. It had kept change from being hidden in one body.

By afternoon, residents had marked the dye-yard crossing for a single day. A committee of millers, tenants, dyers, and carters set another meeting before sunset. The landlord demanded his hinge repair in writing. Jorren wrote it.

Couriers began arriving before the second cart unloaded. Hadrun’s northern valley offered cutters. Kesta offered pass-runners but no promise that its orchards would remain near the capital route. Dorel sent word through Kel’s courier that bench workers had begun copying the simple curve.

One southern delegate asked Tavra to certify every copied sole. She refused. The sole courts would teach the cut, publish failures, and destroy any wrapping that trapped a heel, but no signature would turn the practice into her possession. Another delegate demanded a fixed number from each district. Jorren pointed to the people who had left Veyr’s field and said a quota would rebuild command before anyone reached Meren.

Ruhan recorded each offer in his atlas as a condition, not a border.

At dusk, representatives counted commitments around Tavra’s cart. Seven provinces would send people and shoe-cutters toward Meren. Some offered hundreds. Others promised only whoever chose after hearing the risks. No one pledged a road permanently.

Jorren watched residents erase the imperial salt box and preserve the open gaps. “What do we tell the capital?”

Ruhan closed the atlas with his good hand. Beyond the square, the new road bent through blue-stained ground while the old straight course ended at a wall.

“Tell them Veyr still has a road,” he said. “Then give them the terms for using it.”

Part IV

Chapter 22 — The Last Rehearsal

Ossa tied Eda’s wrists with a knot loose enough to fail under a hard turn.

They stood in the alley below the College laundry wall, hidden from the dawn watch by sheets stiff with frost. Ossa wore a scavenged cadence coat over her damaged drum. The imperial gray fit badly across her shoulders, and the empty outer face of the instrument made any close inspection dangerous.

“You should strike me,” Eda said.

“No.”

“A recaptured fugitive does not arrive clean.”

“You crawled through a shrine, slept in a kiln, and crossed Veyr dye yards. Clean has withdrawn from the discussion.”

Eda touched the bruise already darkening her jaw. She had made it herself against a door frame. “They will ask why I am walking beside you instead of behind.”

Ossa pulled the cord once. “Because you bite.”

“I do.”

“You are enjoying this too much.”

“I am returning to the man I escaped yesterday. Let me enjoy the useful parts.”

Beyond the sheets, the administrative hill rang first bell. The Still Court answered with seven bronze notes. Eda felt three engaged anchors through her right hip. The hidden foundation pull came later, a pressure under the final note.

Ossa heard her breath change. “There?”

“Every sixteenth beat. Serad may have connected the western cradle to it, but I have not seen the physical join.”

“Tavra says a join can be cut.”

“Tavra says many things from a comfortable distance.”

“Her ankle would dispute comfortable.”

Eda looked toward the moving wall. Somewhere inside its morning course, Ruhan guided cutters and volunteers through the wall shrine. Tavra rode on a mill cart because she could no longer put weight on her right foot. Eda had asked them to wait for her signal. She had also asked Tavra not to substitute judgment for fear.

The second request frightened her more than the first.

Ossa marched her through the laundry gate.

The watch captain recognized Eda and reached for his whistle. Ossa slapped a folded order into his hand before he could blow it. The seal came from an archive clerk who had resigned after reading Hadrun’s ledgers. It named Ossa as a temporary recovery officer and contained enough correct language to survive a hurried glance.

“Found her near the west wall,” Ossa said.

The captain stared at the open drum frame. “What happened to your skin?”

“She tried to eat it.”

Eda lunged with convincing fury. Ossa caught the cord and dragged her sideways. The captain stepped back.

“Master Chorearch wants her at center,” he said.

“Chancellor first.”

“He said center.”

Ossa leaned close. “Then you tell Chancellor Miren that a valuable prisoner crossed her civil gate without examination because a watch captain preferred College instructions. Give me your name for the report.”

The captain sent two guards with them to Miren’s offices.

Eda kept her head lowered through the administrative passages. Clerks carried document boxes toward the upper archive. Road officials argued over notices from Veyr, where a city had refused its hold yet received grain. Nobody looked stable enough to notice that Eda’s wrist knot had shifted.

Miren occupied a long chamber overlooking the Court. She stood at the window in a red civil coat, one hand gripping the sill. Three sealed route maps lay open behind her.

“Leave us,” she told the guards.

Ossa remained.

“You too, officer.”

“Prisoner bites,” Ossa said.

Miren turned. She had met Ossa during the Hadrun forced march inquiry, back when the empire needed one cadence officer’s desertion to carry the blame for an entire command. Recognition traveled between them.

“You,” Miren said.

Ossa cut Eda’s cord.

Miren reached for the bell on her desk. Eda set a folded copy of the ecological projections over it.

“Read Nareth first.”

“I have read the fugitive broadsides.”

“Not Tavra’s account. Yours.” Eda opened the sheet to the province columns. “These figures came from Folio Seven. Compare the notation to Serad’s route maps if you think I copied them falsely.”

Miren did not touch the paper. “You fled the College during an emergency.”

“I left a rehearsal after releasing every apprentice in my field.”

“You destabilized an anchor.”

“By a fingernail. He restored it within an hour.”

“You expect that distinction to protect you?”

“No. I expect it to keep us talking about evidence instead of treason.”

Miren looked at Ossa. “And what do you expect?”

“For once, a civil ruler to read the cost before signing the bodies.”

The chancellor’s expression hardened. Ossa had put command into the sentence even without a drum. Eda stepped between them.

“Three provinces,” she said. “Springs fixed in stone. Root paths unable to renew. Soil turning sterile beneath permanent holds. Serad calls the loss finite because the grain roads survive at first. Ask what they carry after the fields die.”

Miren picked up the projections.

She read standing. Her finger followed columns for water and roots before it reached the center-body estimates. She returned to the first page, slower now.

“I saw earlier projections,” she said.

“Which ones?”

“Route disruption. Company casualties. A severe range, not this.”

“Did the earlier pages name soil?”

“No.”

“Did they name duration?”

“Fifty years.”

“The center pages say permanent.”

Miren crossed to a locked map chest and took out a folio bound in blue. Her civil copy ended before the suppressed columns began. Someone had replaced the missing section with a summary stating that normal seasonal release would resume after consolidation.

“Serad signed this,” she said.

“So did you.”

“On what I was given.”

Ossa made a contemptuous sound.

Eda held up one hand. “If limited knowledge excuses a signature, then what you do after full knowledge defines it.”

Miren read the Nareth figures again. “Can the Measure be stopped safely?”

“Three anchors already hold. Dropping every company without release would injure dancers and leave torque in the provinces. Serad knows that. He began early so cancellation would become dangerous.”

“And your alternative?”

“Unproven at continental scale.”

“Then you ask me to choose between certain roads and an experiment.”

“I ask you to stop calling dead soil certain roads.”

Below them, workers rolled the western anchor into rehearsal position. The sound climbed through the window frame.

Miren folded her civil copy closed. “I will issue withdrawal of civil authority from the Dominion convocation. No road warden, tax office, granary, or city guard will enforce attendance or supply the Court.”

“Serad commands the College companies,” Eda said.

“The College charter permits emergency measures without my seal when active ground failure threatens the capital.”

“He has manufactured that condition through the early holds.”

“I cannot dissolve the charter before dawn.”

“Can you arrest him?” Ossa asked.

“With which guard? Half trained under College cadence, and the other half has families on held roads.” Miren took up her bell, then stopped. “I can withdraw what remains mine. I can open civil salt stores and refuse him carts. I can publish these pages.”

“That will not defeat him,” Eda said.

“No.” Miren met her eyes. “It will tell the capital that civil obedience has ended. I spent twenty years making such a sentence impossible to say. Do not mistake lateness for ease.”

Eda did not forgive her. She believed her.

Miren rang for her chief clerk. By the time guards reached the door, the withdrawal order carried her signature and three witnesses. She gave one copy to Ossa.

“Take it to every public board,” she said.

Ossa glanced at Eda.

“Go,” Eda said. “I need you outside the Court before final count.”

“You need a captor.”

“Miren can provide one.”

The chancellor called in four civil guards and ordered them to escort Eda to Serad. Nobody used binding cord.

Before they left, Miren opened the balcony doors and read her withdrawal order into the Court. Her voice lacked Ossa’s reach, so clerks repeated each sentence from lower galleries. Civil salt stores would no longer provision the Dominion Measure. Granary offices would not punish cities for refusing attendance. Tax riders would not seize local anchors. City guards would protect departures from public fields instead of forcing bodies into them.

The words moved down the hill in layers. Some workers cheered. Others stood bewildered beside carts they had spent weeks loading. A civil salt clerk unlocked a wagon, handed one jar to a College surveyor who claimed an active hold required it, then hesitated with the second jar in both hands.

“Does withdrawal mean we let dancers fall?” he called.

Miren gripped the balcony rail. “No. Existing holds receive release supplies. No new field receives civil support.”

Serad’s deputy shouted from below that every jar already belonged to an active continental field. The clerk looked between authorities and began crying.

Eda descended to the wagon. “Mark each jar at issue. Put half beside the release passages, under civilian custody. Give none to an unopened anchor.”

“By whose order?” the deputy demanded.

“By the difference between release and expansion.”

The clerk accepted that because it named the work in front of him. He painted a black heel on the release jars. Miren added the distinction to her order before any official could convert confusion into obedience.

Withdrawal did not empty the Court. It broke a single command into arguments at every cart, gate, and storeroom. Eda saw danger in that disorder. She also saw people asking what their hands would support before they lifted anything.

Serad waited on the central disc with all seven folios open around him.

He looked first at Eda’s wrists, then at the guards. “Where did you find her?”

Miren answered from the gallery. “She returned with evidence.”

“You saw what she carried?”

“I saw what you withheld.”

The companies had begun gathering in the passages. Serad would not argue where they could hear. He closed Folio Seven and said, “Chancellor, this Court remains under College emergency authority.”

“Civil authority is withdrawn.”

“Then remove your officers.”

“I have.”

Miren descended no farther. Serad watched her leave. The hurt on his face looked different from what Eda had seen after her escape. This fracture touched the order he had spent decades building, not a protégé he believed he might retrieve.

When the civil doors shut, he faced Eda.

“Did your mother send you back?”

“No.”

“Ossa?”

“No.”

“Then why are you here?”

“Because three active anchors have bodies attached. If you center the full Measure without me, apprentices die first.”

His breath left slowly. “You still understand necessity.”

“I understand custody. Those dancers cannot leave safely until we release what you began.”

“You will center?”

“I will rehearse.”

Serad accepted the smaller promise because dawn approached and he needed her skill. He handed her the practice shoes kept beside Folio Seven.

Anet and Pellis stood among the eastern company. Eda had sent them to a bathhouse after her escape; now they wore borrowed work clothes under their rehearsal belts. Serad either had not learned they left or had chosen to restore useful bodies without public discipline.

Pellis met Eda’s eyes. “Are we prisoners?”

Serad answered before she could. “You are licensed dancers under emergency call.”

“That was not the question,” Anet said.

A day earlier, she would have swallowed those words. Eda felt pride and fear together.

“No guard will stop you if you leave before we enter salt,” Eda said. “After an anchor engages, release by your section cutter. Do not run across another field.”

Serad’s voice remained level. “Departure now transfers your place to an apprentice with less rehearsal.”

There lay his strongest restraint, not a locked gate but the suffering of someone behind them.

Eda faced the gathered company. “He began holds early and arranged the burden so leaving would injure another person. That fact matters. It does not give him ownership of your feet. Anyone who stays should know the foundation anchor may prevent the center from using the public release.”

The dancers looked toward Serad. He did not deny it.

Three left. Pellis stayed because his younger sister held an outer position in the southern company. Anet removed her licensed shoes, cut the imperial heel mark out with a salt knife, and put them back on.

“What does that accomplish?” Serad asked.

“It reminds me that a mark can be removed.”

Eda had no promise that such reminders would survive counter-motion. She only knew command entered most easily when nobody named the door.

She checked the soles Serad had given her. The public release line remained where she remembered it. Under the arch, a new crosscut ran toward the heel, disguised as a reinforcement groove. She pressed it with her thumbnail. The groove carried the hidden foundation pull.

“Who cut these?”

“I corrected them.”

“You are not a score-cutter.”

“I know the Court better than any cutter alive.”

“That confidence sounds familiar.”

His gaze sharpened. “Put them on.”

The final rehearsal used only four anchors, but Serad placed a complete company around each. Eda entered center. Her right hip took the first pressure before she finished the opening turn.

Serad counted from the lectern. His voice moved through trained bodies with the weight of years. Eda let the first twelve beats pass cleanly. On thirteen she tested the hidden crosscut by raising her left heel.

The foundation answered.

Pressure closed around both ankles, not enough to injure yet. The public exit on her sole pulled outward while the new groove drew inward. A center following the visible release would split the load across her pelvis.

Eda changed weight before Serad saw.

At sixteen, she let her right toe drag half a mark. The western company compensated. Counter-motion skimmed the central disc and revealed a darker stone seam beneath the salt. It ran from Eda’s position to the lectern.

Not merely a cut in her shoe. A second anchor lay under Serad’s folios.

She followed the next figure toward it. The apprentices interpreted her drift as part of rehearsal and widened their ring. Through the thin leather she felt a buried stone no larger than a fist, joined to the capital foundation by a scored copper cradle.

Serad had turned his place of authorship into the lock.

“Center,” he warned.

“My exit catches.”

“It will settle under load.”

“It tightens under load.”

“Continue.”

Eda did. An open accusation would make him remove her and start with another dancer. She needed the hidden anchor exposed when Tavra arrived.

At the final turn, Eda drove her heel beside the dark seam. A hairline crack reached the lectern. Salt drained into it, leaving one corner of the buried cradle visible.

She released before the crosscut closed.

Pain traveled from her right hip to the base of her spine. She remained upright until the companies cleared, then took off both shoes and set them before Serad.

“I will not wear those at dawn.”

“They keep the center from abandoning an active continental hold.”

“They keep the center from choosing release.”

“If you leave at the wrong beat, provinces tear against one another.”

“Then tell me the right beat.”

“Complete the Measure.”

Eda looked at the crack under his lectern. “That is not an exit.”

A bell rang from the western wall.

Not a Court bell. Three low strikes came through the administrative hill, followed by a pause and two more. Ruhan’s signal.

Serad heard only stone settling. Eda had spent enough time with Ossa to hear the route inside the rhythm.

“The wall moved early,” an anchor surveyor called from the gallery.

Serad turned toward him. “Stabilize it.”

“No civil salt carts remain.”

Miren’s withdrawal had begun.

Eda carried her practice shoes toward the north passage. Serad blocked her.

“You remain in the center rooms until dawn.”

“I need my own soles.”

“I will send a cutter.”

“No one else fits my hip.”

He weighed the need for control against the need for skill. “Two guards.”

Eda walked between them to the center rooms. At the first stair, the floor shifted sideways. Both guards caught the rail. Eda did not. She followed the motion down, turned through the robing passage, and kicked the laundry door open.

The western service lane had narrowed to a crack between walls. At its end, the wall shrine stood open.

Ruhan crouched inside the displaced panel, his stiff wrist bound to his chest. Behind him waited Hadrun cutters, Veyr volunteers, and two apprentices from the dye court. They passed salt jars hand to hand through the moving cavity. Ossa’s withdrawal notice had been pasted across the shrine face.

Tavra came last on a low grain cart. Her splinted ankle projected over the edge. She held Eda’s copied page in one hand and Kel’s sample sole in the other.

“You found it?” she asked.

“Under Serad’s lectern. The second anchor runs into the foundation through a scored cradle.”

“Can you expose it again?”

“At final count.”

Tavra’s mouth tightened. “Then I wait.”

Eda heard the effort inside the promise.

Above them, the Court bell began striking an alarm. A herald’s voice followed from the administrative gallery, carrying across the capital: by order of the Master Chorearch, the Dominion convocation would commence at dawn, one day before the revised public hour.

The cutters froze. People looked toward the paling eastern sky.

Ruhan measured the shrine passage. “The wall gives us less than an hour before it turns.”

Eda stepped aside and opened the laundry door wider. One by one, the coalition entered the College through a sacred wall that had never agreed to stay where empire put it.

Chapter 23 — Seven Thousand Bare Feet

The first cutting hall ran out of leather before midnight.

Ossa heard the shortage travel through the old bathhouse as a change in knife rhythm. For two hours, cutters had worked in rough waves: scrape, turn, draw the shallow curve, pass the sole. Then one bench fell silent. Another followed. At the far end, a Hadrun cutter held up the last piece of goat hide, too narrow for an adult heel.

People waiting in the bathing galleries began talking all at once.

“We have felt,” Kel called from somewhere near the door.

Ossa turned so fast the drum frame struck her hip.

Kel Orra pushed through the crowd carrying a pack taller than his back. He looked older than fifteen until he grinned at Tavra across the hall. Behind him came Dorel bench workers with rolls of furnace felt, bark leather, old market awnings, and every serviceable strap they had collected on the road.

Tavra sat at the central bench with her splinted right ankle extended under it. The copied Folio page remained folded inside her coat. When she saw Kel, she gripped the bench edge instead of reaching for him.

“You left Dorel,” she said.

“You noticed.”

“I told you to stay.”

“You stopped being my master when the College named you traitor.” Kel dropped the pack. “Then you became my master again in three provincial notices, but none agreed on the date. I chose the useful interpretation.”

Ossa expected Tavra to cut his decision apart. Instead she asked, “Did you read the risk sheet?”

“Twice. Dorel’s cutters read it aloud at every bench.”

“Do you understand the capital field may turn a simple step against a foot?”

“Yes.”

“Are you here to cut or step?”

“Cut.” Kel’s grin disappeared. “I have no ground-dance training. That fact has not improved during the journey.”

Tavra nodded once. “Take the north bench.”

It did not resemble an embrace. Ossa heard what it cost both of them.

The bathhouse served as only one of eleven cutting halls. Civil granaries, dye sheds, two temples, and a College pension office had opened their floors after Miren’s withdrawal. Messengers ran patterns between them. No hall kept a complete score. Each received Kel’s one-step curve, Tavra’s release notch, and a list of known failures.

Leather remained scarce.

Kel cut the last narrow hide into heel patches and sewed them onto cloth. A Dorel awning became strips wide enough to protect toes. People removed belts, boot tops, apron ties, and cart covers. When even those ran low, cutters mixed charcoal and oil into thick salt paste and marked the curve directly onto bare-foot wrappings.

“Those will blur with sweat,” a licensed score-cutter warned.

“So redraw them before the field,” Kel said.

“They will not hold a deep cut.”

“They should not. Nobody outside the center companies takes a deep cut.”

The cutter looked toward Tavra for correction. Tavra kept shaving a felt heel. “He is right.”

Ossa moved between benches and listened for haste. A fast knife could close the release groove. A proud cutter could add a flourish that made one ordinary step into training. Twice she stopped work and burned whole batches whose heel marks trapped weight.

People protested the waste.

“Bad soles cost more than leather,” she said.

A cutter from the eastern pension house challenged her beside the furnace. “You burned forty heel patches. Forty bodies may stand unmarked because you prefer purity.”

“I prefer a foot that can withdraw.”

“You were a cadence officer, not a cutter.”

“No. I learned to inspect what commands did after they left my hands.”

She brought Tavra over rather than win by rank. Tavra tested the rejected groove with a wooden foot. The model crossed the curve, reached the heel, and caught. Under strain, a frightened participant would turn inward toward the anchor instead of leaving.

“Burn them,” Tavra said.

The cutter folded her arms. “Then name what we use instead.”

Kel searched the supply heaps and found braided curtain cord. He laid two lengths across a felt strip, leaving a raised channel rather than cutting one. The first model foot slid. He resewed the outer cord at a shallower angle. On the third try, the heel released.

“It will rub skin,” the pension cutter said.

“Line it with bath cloth,” Tavra replied. “Mark each pair for inspection after practice. We do not pretend a rushed substitute lasts beyond one use.”

The cutter watched Kel make the next wrapping, then sat beside him and improved the knot. Forty failures did not become forty identical repairs. Felt thickness varied. Ankles varied. One person’s missing toe required the curve to begin farther back. A woman with a stone-stiffened arch could not use wrapping at all, so she joined the disclosure readers.

Ossa listened as the hall changed tempo again. Scrape and cut gave way to stitch, test, reject, recut. Slower work produced fewer soles. It also produced no hidden trap in the heel.

Her left forearm trembled when she lifted the damaged batches to the furnace. She shifted them to her right hand. The inner drum skin at her side showed old command cuts in the firelight. She had once inspected cadence equipment with equal care because a clean command kept soldiers moving. Precision had never guaranteed innocence. It only made intention arrive accurately.

Near the eastern door, Ruhan arranged route groups by neighborhood rather than province. His stiff wrist prevented him from tying chalk cords, so children did it under his direction. Each group learned three paths away from the Court. Nobody could promise which would remain open after the ground moved.

“Do not call them retreat routes,” Ossa told him.

“What should I call a path people use to go away?”
