“Exits.”

Ruhan considered. “Better word.”

Before dawn’s first gray, the cutting stopped for disclosure.

Ossa climbed onto the drained bath. She disliked height without a drum command to justify it. Faces filled the tiled chamber, stood on stairs, and crowded the doors. Messengers waited to carry her words to the other halls.

She struck the bare rim once.

“This sound asks for attention. It does not order silence.”

Conversation faded unevenly.

Ossa held up a wrapped foot marked with Kel’s curve. “One step. Outer heel touches first. Weight crosses only as far as you choose. Toe follows the curve. Then you withdraw through the open heel. No second step. No turn. No person here becomes a ground-dancer because the capital wants a larger body.”

A messenger repeated the instructions. Voices carried them out through the doors.

“The known risks are bruising, falls, turned ankles, cuts from moving stone, and counter-motion if your section closes badly. The unknown risk comes from scale. This practice has worked in a kiln field and at Veyr’s civic anchor. It has not been used against seven permanent holds.”

Someone called, “Will it save the center companies?”

“It may give them room to release.”

“Will it stop the Dominion Measure?”

“We do not promise that.”

“Then why step?”

Ossa looked at the command cuts inside her drum. “Because Serad’s score gathers control into one count. The Open Measure can return part of that count to ordinary bodies who hear the cost and choose. If too few choose, we change the attempt. We do not fill the field by hiding what may happen.”

A woman in a tax-office coat asked, “What happens to my district if I leave after the sections form?”

“Your section recounts. It may shrink or combine with another. You owe no replacement.”

“What if my husband stays?”

“His choice does not purchase yours.”

The woman left the bathhouse. Six people followed. Nobody jeered.

Others came forward to ask about the fossilized provinces, the center injuries, and the active anchors. Sole-court readers displayed copies of Nareth’s root evidence. A College pension clerk named the bends in her own legs and described how permanent counter-motion deepened years after retirement. Ossa recited Pel’s death and her part in the Hadrun march without turning confession into qualification.

When the questions ended, participants registered by section. The total changed with every messenger. Some names had been counted twice at different halls. Whole family groups withdrew after seeing the risk sheet. Late arrivals from Veyr replaced none of them automatically; each listened before giving a name.

No quota passed from province to province.

Ossa left the bathhouse for the avenue below the Still Court. Dawn had begun whitening the high walls. The administrative hill rose above residential roofs, its official stairs clogged with abandoned civil carts. College guards remained at the Court gates, but Miren’s city guard kept side streets open for departure.

Ossa divided the avenue into listening sections.

In the army, she had built tempo from center outward. Every drummer repeated her beat. Soldiers far beyond her sight moved because the same cadence reached them through other hands.

Here she did the opposite.

She taught the northern section a four-breath interval, then handed its count to a miller from Veyr. She gave the river section no drum at all; its people chose a spoken pair of words in three local dialects. Kestan pass-runners refused a fixed interval and agreed to begin when the shadow of the eastern wall crossed a chalk peg. Dorel cutters used the market stone’s nine-day work song, slowed until it contained only one step.

A former cadence sergeant objected. “They will not land together.”

“They should not.”

“The anchor response will scatter.”

“That is part of the release.”

“You cannot coordinate a continental field with neighborhood songs.”

Ossa removed a section of cord from her drum rim and gave it to him. “Then coordinate the six groups that ask you. Not the seventh. Not the street behind them. Your beat ends where consent ends.”

He tied the cord around his wrist.

At every boundary, she placed a person whose task involved listening for refusal. They carried no drums. A raised palm, dropped scarf, or extinguished lamp would stop local count. One section chose two listeners because its alley curved out of sight.

Ossa tested the arrangement without warning. She sent a boy to drop a red scarf in the river section while its people practiced. The nearest speaker saw it and stopped. The next cluster continued for two breaths before noticing. Farther uphill, nobody noticed at all.

“In the army, I would strike a halt,” Ossa told them. “One sound would reach every trained body. We are not rebuilding that power, so the signal must travel by people who accept the task.”

They formed a relay of watchers between sections. Each watcher faced sideways, one eye on the local step and one on the neighboring exit. A deaf stonemason proposed raised lantern shutters for people who could not hear spoken counts. Two laundry workers tied white cloth to poles so a refusal remained visible above shoulders.

The second test stopped the river section cleanly. The northern millers kept moving because their own practice carried no red-scarf signal.

A civil clerk objected. “If one section halts and another continues, the field becomes uneven.”

“It will already be uneven,” Ossa said. “Bodies do not agree on a perfect beat. The cutters manage local torque. Eda and the trained companies manage the center. Our task here is to keep refusal from disappearing under scale.”

They rehearsed a stumble next. One volunteer sat down in the path and covered her head. People flowed around her too quickly, eager to preserve tempo. Ossa made them repeat until neighbors protected the fallen body first and let their count break.

“Broken rhythm is not failure,” she said. “Stepping on someone to preserve it is.”

By the final practice, every section had its own stop sign and a named relief watcher. Those names remained local. Ossa did not collect them into a command list.

The arrangement looked untidy from the Court stairs. That pleased Ossa less than she expected. Part of her still wanted to hear one clean response travel through a crowd. She knew how to make it happen. Her body contained the old cadences even when she slept.

Kel found her rubbing the inner skin with her thumb.

“Does that command people?” he asked.

“It used to.”

“Why keep it?”

“For evidence. For memory. For the work it may do when cut.”

“Cut into what?”

“I have not reached that beat.”

He looked over the avenue. Thousands practiced the same simple curve with different timings. Bare-foot wrappings flashed pale against charcoal marks. “Tavra says not knowing can be honest or lazy.”

“She says many encouraging things.”

“She said it while making me recut an entire pair.”

“That sounds more like her.”

A registrar ran up from the southern hall. “We lost four sections.”

“Lost?” Ossa asked.

“They refused.”

“Then say they withdrew.”

“We fall below the disclosed muster.”

The number mattered because each ordinary step could carry only a tiny adjustment. Too few steps would leave the center company holding most of the continental counter-motion. Too many bodies admitted without training would turn the Court approaches into a crush.

“How many heard the full risk statement and still wait?” Ossa asked.

The registrar gave a figure short of the field need.

“Recount duplicates. Do not recruit.”

“We can ask the pension houses.”

“They have received the same notice as everyone else.”

“What if they missed it?”

“Send the written disclosure. No appeal from a uniform, no mention of duty.”

The registrar ran back.

Ossa climbed the avenue. She checked every local tempo without striking her drum. The miller rushed his fourth breath when nervous. A temple group had chosen words too long to shout under strain, so they shortened them. The Kestan shadow peg stood where an awning would cover it after sunrise; Ruhan moved it with their permission.

Near the upper gate, people began removing wrappings.

A rumor had spread that Serad meant to start before sections entered the outer salt. Some believed waiting near the Court made them trapped participants. Ossa ordered the side exits widened. She did not tell them the rumor lacked proof. Serad had already advanced one schedule.

“Anyone may go now,” she called. “The section will not close behind you.”

A broad lane opened through the crowd. Many left. Parents carried children away. Two College veterans removed their marked shoes and sat on a doorstep to help treat blisters rather than step.

Then new groups arrived from the lower city. Miren’s public evidence had reached neighborhoods after midnight. These people came without provincial cords, and some distrusted every person wearing one. Ossa made them hear the disclosure from civilian readers, not coalition officers.

Kel’s Dorel cutters arrived last from the western halls, carrying bundles of bare-foot wrappings over their shoulders. Tavra rode among them on the low cart. Her face had gone pale from the journey uphill.

“You should be inside,” Ossa said.

“So should you.”

“I can stand.”

“An enviable habit.” Tavra looked at the changing registers. “Where is the count?”

“Still moving.”

“Do not freeze it early.”

“I know.”

Tavra held Ossa’s gaze. They both heard the command hidden inside urgency.

“I am reminding myself,” Tavra said.

Kel unloaded wrappings onto the final tables. He did not step. He inspected heel marks, rejected blurred paste, and taught local cutters how to redraw the curve. His work filled gaps left by departures without making him a participant for symbolic effect.

The eastern sky brightened.

Registrars converged at the center of the avenue. They compared hall sheets, removed duplicate names, crossed out everyone who had withdrawn, and added only those who had heard the complete disclosure. Each section keeper confirmed that its waiting bodies matched the page.

The first count came short. A group from the river stair had not arrived.

Ossa sent no drummer after them.

The second count exceeded the field by nineteen because a dye hall had copied one line twice. Those names were checked in person.

At the third count, a woman raised her hand and withdrew. Two brothers followed. A Kestan runner arrived with three people who had completed instruction at the lower wall, but one chose blister work instead.

The tally moved until the people and pages agreed.

Seven thousand participants had heard the cost, practiced one step, and chosen to wait outside the Still Court. Many who had stood in the halls earlier had refused. Others had taken their places only after making a separate choice. The number described the present moment, not a debt owed at the next beat.

Ossa gave the final register to seven civilian keepers. She kept no complete copy.

Above them, the Court gates remained shut. Serad’s command drums sounded inside, disciplined and perfectly aligned.

The old cadence tightened Ossa’s shoulders before she recognized it. Her left hand tried to answer, though the healing forearm could not hold a full stroke. She let the reflex happen without obeying it. The two surviving straps crossed her ribs, bearing the drum like a piece of service she had not completed. At the Court, those straps could still transmit command into her stance. She touched the knife at her belt and left it sheathed for now.

The avenue did not answer as one body. A miller breathed his local interval. Kestan runners watched a moving shadow. Dorel voices hummed a market song. Between those tempos, exits stayed open.

Ossa rested her right hand on the bare rim and waited without raising it.

Chapter 24 — The Measure Begins

Serad began while the western public gate still stood closed.

Eda heard the first command stroke from the robing passage. The floor seized under her right foot. Apprentices waiting beside her pitched toward the Court, pulled by trained reflex before the herald finished announcing the opening.

“That is not dawn count,” Anet said.

“It is now,” Eda replied.

She shoved open the passage door.

Four companies already moved inside the Still Court. The three early anchors held their provinces in a pressure Eda had carried for days. A fourth rolled into engagement as its dancers turned. Beyond the galleries, crowds filled the eastern and southern approaches, but the northern sections remained outside the salt. The western gate trapped hundreds behind iron leaves.

Serad stood at his lectern. Folio Seven lay open beside the other six. He had removed Eda’s altered practice shoes and placed a fresh pair at the central mark.

“Center,” he called.

Ossa’s distributed sections had not reached their places. Tavra’s cart remained somewhere beneath the west gallery. Eda could refuse entry. She had cut that right into her own soles during the night.

If she used it now, the four active companies would keep turning until Serad chose another center. Anet stood behind Eda. Pellis’s younger sister held the southern anchor with a mineral bruise already rising above her shoe. Serad would spend them before surrendering the count, and the unfinished holds would carry torque back into provinces where people had no warning.

Eda crossed the salt.

She did not do it because he called.

At the central mark, she kicked his shoes aside and put on the pair Tavra had cut. The right sole fitted her stressed hip. The left heel contained an open release that no hidden groove crossed.

“You changed the score,” Serad said.

“I changed my shoes.”

“Same consequence.”

“No.” Eda tied the final lace. “One belongs to your Court. One belongs to my feet.”

The fifth command stroke landed.

She entered center by choice.

Before taking weight, she gave the companies three signals they had practiced without Serad. Palm down meant reduce force. Two fingers turned outward meant prepare local release. A fist against her ribs meant the foundation had closed and nobody should follow her exit blindly. Anet repeated the signals to the eastern ring. Pellis carried them south.

Serad watched the gestures. “Those are not in the folios.”

“They keep dancers from guessing what happens inside center.”

“The count tells them what happens.”

“The count tells them what you want their feet to do.”

A western cutter asked whether the new signals overruled his page. Serad said no. Eda said they carried bodily information the page could not know in advance. The cutter looked at the anchor already pulling against its cradle and chose to watch both.

That small divided loyalty mattered. A center usually received strain in silence while outer companies trusted the choreography. Eda made her pain public before it arrived. She pointed to her right hip and showed the existing stress. She touched both soles to indicate heat risk. She named the second anchor under the lectern while every nearby dancer could still leave the salt.

Two did. Serad ordered reserves forward. One reserve refused the open place. The other entered after Eda described the pressure already active in that field.

No clean rebellion formed. Bodies stayed for siblings, pensions, fear, skill, and belief in Serad. Others left for equally mixed reasons. Eda had wanted a simple line between command and choice. The Court gave her people standing with one foot on each.

Counter-motion rose through both soles. The first three anchors felt rigid, their stored pressure cold and deep. The fourth carried fresh movement from a Hadrun valley fighting the hold. Eda bent her knees and let that motion pass across her rather than locking it into the hip.

“Complete first folio,” Serad ordered.

Companies turned in rings. Salt snapped under their heels. Every field pointed toward Eda, but she felt gaps where public sections had not arrived. Serad’s early start made those absences dangerous. The master count reached for bodies that did not stand there and fed their share back into center.

Heat gathered under Eda’s toes.

At the east gallery, Miren’s withdrawal notices hung over empty civil seats. College surveyors had filled missing salt carts from private stores, but their lines ran thin. Outside, local voices tried to find their separate intervals against command drums.

Serad raised two fingers.

The fifth anchor engaged.

Its stone struck the cradle with a sound like a dropped mill wheel. Eda’s right hip folded. She caught herself on the outer edge of her left foot. The correction dragged the fifth province half a pace toward stillness and pushed its answer into her arch.

The sole smoked.

She smelled leather before pain arrived. Then both feet burned from heel to toe. Tavra’s scores opened release channels, but counter-motion entered faster than the cuts could pass it outward.

“Slow the fifth company,” Eda shouted.

Serad’s count continued.

The cadence drums around the Court answered each other. Their pressure did not move stone by itself. It moved trained bodies. Every dancer struck harder, and the combined impacts compressed the air inside the amphitheater.

Eda’s left ear rang.

A command beat landed close to the center. Sound became a flat blow against that side of her head. She saw Anet call something from the eastern ring, but the words reached her only through her right ear. On the left, the drum reduced to a dull pulse without edge.

She touched no hand to it. A center who changed posture would redirect load.

“Sixth folio,” Serad called.

Pellis broke formation.

He crossed one pace toward his sister, then remembered Eda’s warning and stopped before entering another field. “Southern center is failing.”

“There is no southern center,” Serad said. “Eda carries all anchors.”

“My sister is bleeding through her shoe.”

“Maintain your line.”

Pellis looked at Eda.

“Release her section by pairs,” Eda said.

Serad struck the lectern with his palm. “Maintain.”

The command drum repeated him. Pellis’s body turned toward his assigned mark even as his face refused. He drove his own heel into the salt, spoiling the line under it. The broken mark gave his sister room to step out. Three dancers followed.

Their load reached Eda.

Her burned soles stuck to the leather. She screamed once, then converted the falling breath into a count the remaining dancers could use.

“Local release. East on four. South when clear. Do not cross fields.”

Some obeyed her. Others followed Serad. The Court developed two counts, each carried by trained bodies. Neither fully controlled the ground.

The sixth anchor dropped into its cradle.

Its answer entered differently from the others. Instead of weight, Eda felt absence: a spring route cut off so completely that the center pressure found no returning water. The empty channel pulled moisture from the Court salt. Black lines whitened around her feet.

A surveyor at the sixth field read the change as successful stillness. “Anchor settled,” he called.

“No spring answer,” Eda shouted.

Serad glanced at the field diagram. “Within projection.”

“Say it aloud. The spring has stopped.”

His jaw tightened. “The sixth watershed will require managed wells after consolidation.”

Dancers near him heard. One missed her turn. Another completed it with tears on his face. Serad had not lied when pressed, yet the truth arrived only after the ground had already paid it.

At the western approach, a public section tried to enter around an abandoned salt cart. Its local count collided with the outer company’s trained turn. Eda saw three people fall. Their section listener raised a white cloth. The nearby public voices stopped, but Serad’s company continued across the blocked approach.

“Western outer ring, shorten by one pace,” Eda called.

The company leader waited for Serad.

“Maintain field geometry,” he said.

“A fallen body lies on your next mark.”

The leader looked down, saw a woman covering her head beneath the raised cloth, and broke the circle. His dancers passed the interruption from foot to foot. Their field warped but did not collapse. Two civilians pulled the woman clear.

The leader returned to his line on a changed count. Eda felt the error arrive as a brief twist in her left knee, survivable because he had spent precision to protect a body.

“Record that correction,” she told the nearest apprentice.

Serad said, “There will be no second performance.”

“Then record what this one costs.”

A seam opened along the eastern gallery. Masonry rose and settled. Outside, people shouted as the public approaches shifted. The northern gate finally opened, but sections could not enter in their planned order.

Eda felt Ossa before she saw her.

A broken rim beat came from the west passage, deliberately late against every College drum. Then local spoken counts rose around it. Ossa entered beneath the gallery with her incomplete instrument at her hip. The two surviving straps crossed her chest. Each imperial stroke pulled at her shoulders through conditioned stance.

She fought the cadence step by step.

Eda gave her a sixteen-beat signal with two fingers.

Ossa nodded.

The seventh anchor began moving toward engagement.

Serad turned Folio Seven to the center sequence. “Full count.”

College drummers lifted their sticks.

Ossa drew her knife.

She cut the lower strap first. The drum dropped sideways and wrenched her weak left forearm. She caught the rim against her thigh. The upper strap remained tight around her shoulder, carrying the command into her spine.

A drummer beside her shouted, “You will lose the instrument.”

“I am trying to lose what it does.”

She sawed through the upper strap.

The damaged drum fell onto the salt. Its inner command skin struck the ground and split along one erased order. Ossa did not pick it up. She kicked the bare rim toward a Veyr miller, who struck his own local interval and passed it down the section.

Without the command straps, Ossa’s shoulders stopped answering Serad’s beat. She raised empty hands and began speaking counts instead.

The seventh anchor engaged.

Every held road in Eda’s perception drew tight. She could not see the provinces, but their differences entered her body: spring pressure under the left heel, root drag along the right calf, mountain weight across the hip, city paving against the spine. Serad’s score tried to make all of them arrive as one fixed answer.

Her soles blistered inside the shoes. Skin separated with each shift. She smelled blood and hot felt.

Through her right ear, she heard public voices multiplying. Her left ear carried only thick impacts, as if water filled the skull.

“Now,” Serad said.

The center sequence closed.

Eda took the visible release. Her heel moved outward exactly as Tavra had cut it.

The second anchor answered beneath the lectern.

Pressure snapped back through the foundation cradle and locked both of Eda’s feet to the central disc. The hidden anchor did not need a groove in her shoes now. With all seven provincial anchors engaged, Serad had bound her exit through the capital’s own stone.

Eda’s knees buckled. She caught herself on both hands. Salt entered her palms.

The trapped load showed her the capital foundation more clearly than rehearsal had. Residential streets branched away from the administrative hill in shallow moving shelves. Generations of small releases had let those districts adjust around wells, markets, and homes. The Still Court sat on a deeper shelf pinned by its buried anchor. Serad’s new join dragged the living branches toward that central immobility.

He had called the capital one foundation. Under Eda’s body, it felt like many grounds forced through one throat.

She signaled fist to ribs. Anet saw and warned the eastern company not to copy Eda’s attempted exit. Pellis passed the warning south. On the left side of the Court, Eda heard none of their shouted replies. She saw mouths move and signals travel, but sound reached her as a low crush.

Panic rose when she turned her head and the world vanished on that side. She forced herself to face right, using the ear that still separated voice from drum. A center did not need perfect hearing to choose. She needed other people to stop assuming she heard them.

“My left ear is damaged,” she called. “Visual signals only from west.”

A western cutter raised both hands to confirm. The message moved by gesture around the ring. Nobody promised the hearing would return. Nobody told her to endure silently for the figure.

Serad left the lectern and came one pace toward her. “Do not fight the foundation. It carries the center safely through completion.”

“It carries me nowhere.”

“It prevents a release that would tear the road web.”

“You began before the Open Measure sections were placed.”

“Because Miren deprived the Court of supply and your coalition entered by force. Delay became failure.”

“You made every refusal an emergency, then called emergency consent.”

His face held no triumph. Sweat ran from his hairline. The active anchors loaded his injured shoulder even outside center. “I called it survival.”

The mountain north of Meren entered Eda’s hip.

Its mass arrived as a downward turn, slow but immense. The Dominion score ordered the range to hold above the city. The Deep Step beneath it continued moving. Opposed directions met in Eda’s pelvis and spread fire through the stress line.

She looked at Folio Seven.

The page lay open under Serad’s hand. Its center command matched the pressure around her feet: close heel, receive foundation, hold until continental stillness. She could not reach the folio without abandoning balance, but Tavra had copied the sequence into memory with her at the kiln. Eda knew where it entered her scored sole.

She pulled the salt knife from her belt.

Serad saw. “If you cut while loaded, the unfinished command may return through your legs.”

“Then tell the drummers to stop.”

“No.”

The answer hurt because he gave it quietly.

Eda drove the knife into her left sole.

Hot leather resisted. She cut across the command line copied from Folio Seven, not the open release. The blade entered the blister beneath her arch. Pain erased the Court for one beat.

When sight returned, blood darkened the salt.

The severed score loosened the command around her left foot. The foundation still held through the right. Eda shifted weight onto the cut, accepting the wound because it belonged to a route she had chosen.

“East sections,” she shouted. Her voice sounded crooked with half her hearing gone. “Do not take the public step. Open your exits first.”

Local callers relayed the instruction. Some sections heard. Others did not. The crowd remained partly outside the salt, pressed by late-arriving groups and moving approaches.

Serad resumed the master count himself.

Ossa climbed onto a broken cradle with no drum attached to her body. She spoke a different count and pointed to the local keepers. They carried it away in fragments, not as an order to step but as room in which each section could decide.

Eda cut the right sole.

The knife found Tavra’s release groove, deepened it, then turned toward the foundation pressure. She stopped before severing the last leather. If she left at that beat, the center load would transfer into Anet and the nearest company. Choice required timing, not merely a door.

“Tavra!” she called.

Her left ear gave back no answer. Through the right came distant wheels and a score-cutter’s voice beneath the western gallery.

“I’m at the cradle,” Tavra shouted.

Eda turned her face to bring the good ear toward the floor. “Do not release it yet. The southern company still carries the seventh return.”

“How long?”

Eda counted bodies rather than beats. Pellis’s sister had cleared. Anet guided two apprentices through their heel release. The western leader pulled his company away from the fallen-body gap. Every safe departure changed what remained attached to Eda.

“Wait for my open hand,” she called.

A tool stopped striking below. Tavra had heard and obeyed, though the mountain pressure increased with every breath. That pause carried more trust than any rescue Tavra could have forced upon her.

Serad heard it too. “You are giving timing to a woman whose last unsanctioned release buried Nareth.”

“I am giving my timing to my mother.”

“She cannot carry this anchor.”

“She knows.”

Eda raised two fingers toward Anet, ordering no body, only warning that the next change approached. “That is why she asked.”

She could not see Tavra. She felt a cutting tool rest against the scored copper under Serad’s lectern.

The foundation anchor shuddered.

Dominion faltered. Three companies missed Serad’s next beat. A provincial road tore free enough to send a ripple through the Court floor. Galleries leaned outward, then returned.

Beyond the northern wall, stone cracked with a depth no drum could imitate.

Everyone looked up.

The mountain above Meren had not held. Its lower shelf began bending toward the administrative hill, carrying forest, old stairs, and a white line of winter rock down toward the city.

Serad gripped the lectern. Eda remained trapped at one foot, both soles burned, her left ear reduced to pressure.

Below them, Tavra struck the foundation cradle again.

Chapter 25 — The Mountain Takes a Knee

The grain cart lost a wheel beneath the western gallery.

Tavra fell against the sideboard. Her splinted right ankle struck stone, and pain tore through the joint before two cutters caught her shoulders. Above them, the descending mountain filled the open side of the Still Court. Its lower shelf moved through morning cloud, slow enough to watch and too fast for any city below it.

“Leave the cart,” she said.

Kel crouched at the broken axle. “We can brace it.”

“No time.”

“You cannot crawl across that floor.”

Tavra looked through the gallery arches. Eda knelt at center with one foot fixed to the foundation pull. Blood darkened the salt beneath both shoes. Serad stood beside the lectern, trying to restore the master count while companies broke into local releases.

“I can cut from the ground,” Tavra said.

Kel and a Hadrun cutter crossed their arms behind her back and lifted. Her left knee carried no useful bend. The right ankle hung inside its splint. Every jolt sent a bright line up the calf. They moved her between salt jars and fallen masonry until the foundation seam reached the gallery wall.

The crack Eda had opened during rehearsal ran under the lectern. A corner of scored copper showed beneath black salt. Tavra could not reach it from the passage.

“Board,” she said.

Kel brought the cutting board from her cart. They laid Tavra on it and slid her under the gallery rail. Her shoulders scraped stone. The Court floor shifted again, trying to roll them toward center.

Ruhan appeared at the lower passage and caught the board with his good hand. His stiff right wrist stayed bound against his chest. “Your route has become discourteous.”

“It learned from me.”

He tied a cord through the board handle and looped it around a pillar. “This holds until the pillar moves.”

“Comforting.”

Above them, the mountain shelf lowered another span.

Tavra dragged herself along the foundation seam. Kel followed with her knives, a salt wedge, and Eda’s copied Folio page. The seam widened under the lectern into a shallow cavity. Inside lay the second anchor, a fist-sized piece of dark capital stone clamped in copper. Seven scored channels joined it to the provincial fields. An eighth line cut downward into the deep shelf beneath the administrative hill.

Serad had made the foundation anchor perform two tasks. It prevented center release, and it held the Deep Step under the Court. Cut the wrong channel and the provincial torque would return through Eda. Break the downward line without receiving its counter-motion and the administrative hill might meet the descending mountain at full movement.

Kel handed Tavra the copied page.

She matched Eda’s tiny columns to the cuts. One figure had blurred beside Nareth, but the foundation sequence remained clear. Serad had reversed Tavra’s old release from the failed holding. At Nareth, she had opened a lateral path too late and displaced a hillside district. Here he had closed every lateral path in advance, forcing all release into the body at center.

The join carried his fear with impressive craftsmanship.

Tavra put two fingers on the copper. Pressure entered her lithified knee and stopped there. She changed angle. The mountain’s descending weight came through the eighth line, while Eda’s trapped foot pulled through the seventh provincial channel.

Two loads, one knot.

Tavra tested the knot with a pinch of step-salt. She drew a small open curve on the cavity floor and touched its end to the seventh channel. The salt blackened and blew toward Eda, confirming the center pull. When she connected the curve to the eighth line, the salt pressed flat under her thumb. The mountain load did not seek release sideways. It sought a body or the city shelf.

Kel held the copied page close to the copper. “Could the public sections take this part?”

“Not while the foundation remains closed. Their one-step marks return motion to the Deep Step. This channel blocks that return under the Court.”

“Could we break the anchor stone?”

“The incomplete measure would leave every provincial line carrying torque. Roads could kick. Springs could split new courses under houses. Eda receives the first return because Serad tied her to it.”

“What if we cut each province separately?”

“Possible with seven trained teams and time to read local response.” Tavra glanced at the descending shelf. “We have neither.”

She hated the narrowing. Serad had built it deliberately, but craft rather than fate created the trap. That meant no miracle waited outside the rules. The answer had to use the body positions, salt, scored copper, and anchor pressure already present.

Tavra widened the receiving curve on the board. A lying center would claim the cut could pass harmlessly through old stone in her knees. An honest one had to name where the load would continue when those joints refused it.

Ossa reached the gallery without her drum. Cut straps hung from her coat. Her left arm lay tight against her ribs.

“Can you break it?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“What happens?”

Tavra did not answer immediately. She traced the possible release with the point of her knife. A shallow cut could free Eda but send the foundation load into the city shelf. A deep cut could carry that load into a prepared center. No standing dancer could enter the cavity. Tavra already touched it at pelvis height from the ground.

“It needs a center here,” she said.

Ossa looked at her legs. “No.”

“Do not make Eda’s argument badly on her behalf.”

“What happens to you?”

“The counter-motion enters through the board and my hips. The old stone in my knees may keep it from reaching the chest.”

“May?”

“I have not centered a foundation while lying down.”

Kel’s face had gone pale. “Will you walk?”

Tavra could have lied for speed. “Probably not.”

“Will you live?”

“I think so.”

Ossa swore.

“The other cut?” Kel asked.

“Frees Eda and lets the mountain continue into the administrative hill. It may break the deeper shelf. Residential branches could follow.”

“How long can you hold the counter-motion?”

“Long enough to pause the descent. Not long enough to finish the Open Measure.”

The distinction mattered. Tavra could not purchase victory with her body. She could only create time for other people to act.

Serad’s voice crossed the Court. “Restore center geometry.”

Eda looked toward the gallery but turned her right ear the wrong way. She could not hear Tavra from this angle.

Tavra asked Ossa to relay by hand. Open palm toward center. Point down to the cradle. Two fingers for two loads. Ossa climbed where Eda could see and repeated the signals.

Eda answered with a fist to her ribs: foundation closed.

Tavra held up the copied page. Then she pointed at herself and the eighth channel.

Eda understood. Her face changed.

“No,” she shouted.

Tavra waited. Her first impulse pressed against her teeth: tell Eda the mountain left no choice, cut before argument could become refusal, accept the injury because she had earned it. That impulse had put Eda inside plans for most of her life.

She made herself speak each cost.

“If I take the foundation counter-motion, your foot releases when you cut the last leather. The load enters my pelvis and spine. I expect permanent injury. The mountain pauses. The Deep Step stays trapped until the public sections release it.”

Eda listened with her right ear turned toward the floor.

“If I do not,” Tavra continued, “you remain attached or I cut you free into an uncontrolled foundation release. The shelf may strike the administrative hill and pull residential ground. Those are the choices I can see. There may be another.”

Serad had heard. “There is. Restore the center sequence. Complete the hold.”

Eda looked at Folio Seven beneath his hand.

Tavra did not answer him. “Eda, do you want me to take the anchor?”

The mountain groaned over the Court. Stone dust fell like dry rain. Kel crouched beside Tavra with tears running openly. Ossa kept both hands raised so local counts would not mistake her movement for command.

Eda bent over her trapped right foot. “Not yet.”

Tavra lowered the knife.

The copper vibrated under her hand. Every instinct demanded that she call delay foolish. She could feel the eighth channel deepening toward rupture. A roof at the northern edge of the administrative hill folded under falling stones. People ran from it while the residential shelf tilted away.

Kel leaned close. “How long can we wait?”

“Until the channel splits without the knife, or Eda gives the time.”

“Which comes first?”

“I do not know.”

He looked frightened by the answer and stayed beside her. Ossa relayed Eda’s release signals across the gallery. Tavra counted the companies leaving center, not to overrule her daughter but to understand what Eda protected with each breath.

At the kiln, Tavra had promised to wait. The promise had sounded difficult in conversation. Here it required holding a blade still while a mountain moved. She understood at last that consent did not become optional when urgency made obedience more convenient.

Serad stepped from the lectern. The floor tilted. He struck his shoulder against the stone edge and fell to one knee. Folio Seven slid across the salt toward Eda.

She caught it.

With her knife, she cut the stitched center pages free from the binding. Serad reached for them but his bruised shoulder failed under weight. Eda tore the hidden sequence once across, then again through the foundation command. She pushed the pieces into the wet blood beneath her left shoe and ground them into the scored sole.

The other folios remained open on the lectern and floor.

“Now the page cannot tell another center where to close,” she said.

The foundation pull tightened around her right foot.

Tavra kept the blade away from copper. “Your timing.”

Eda signaled Anet. The eastern company released by pairs. Pellis carried the signal south. Ossa turned toward the public approaches and held up the white cloth that meant wait. The mountain continued descending.

“Not yet,” Eda called again.

Tavra’s old instinct screamed at every lost breath. She watched the eighth line instead. Its cut deepened as the descending shelf pressed harder. She placed the cutting board across her hips, braced its ends under two copper lips, and scored a receiving curve into the wood. Kel packed step-salt into the groove.

“This will split,” he said.

“Yes.”

He added a second board beneath it.

One by one, attached companies left the central pull. Eda counted them with her right hand. Her left foot had come free, but the right remained bound. When the final southern pair crossed its release, she opened her hand toward Tavra.

Eda chooses, Tavra thought, not as a slogan but as the fact governing her knife.

Tavra drove the blade through the seventh channel and into the eighth.

Copper snapped.

Eda tore her right foot free.

The foundation counter-motion entered Tavra through the boards.

Her pelvis lifted from stone. For an instant she felt every old injury separately: the locked left knee, the fractured right ankle, the pale lines in both joints. Then the mountain’s weight joined them. Pressure drove through both hips and climbed the spine in hard increments.

The first board split.

Tavra tried to release through her heels, forgetting she could not reach the ground with either foot. The counter-motion had nowhere else to go. It crossed the pelvis, compressed the lower spine, and turned sensation below her waist into white absence.

She did not feel the second board break.

Kel caught her head before it struck stone. Ossa shouted her name. Tavra could hear them, but her body ended at the ribs in a way no pain had prepared her to understand.

Above, Eda rolled out of center on both burned feet and collapsed beside the torn folio pages. Serad remained alive against the lectern, one shoulder hanging low.

The mountain slowed.

Its lower shelf stopped short of the administrative roofs. Forest trees leaned over the northern gallery. Broken stairs descended through open air and ended above the Court. The great mass had not returned to its old position. It had bent, paused, and placed its weight like a knee beside Meren.

People in the residential districts ran through streets that still lay on their own shallow shelves.

Tavra tried to move her left leg. Nothing answered. She tried the right. The same absence remained.

“Do not tell me it will return,” she said before Kel could speak.

He swallowed and nodded.

Under the Still Court, the deeper shelf still strained against the trapped Deep Step. Serad’s master count had broken, but the seven holds had not released. Outside, Ossa’s public sections waited for a choice that could not come from Tavra alone.

Eda crawled toward the edge of center and reached one burned hand down through the cracked floor.

Tavra could not lift her body to meet it. She raised her own hand instead.

Chapter 26 — A Country of Dancing Stone

The second board had broken across Tavra’s hips, but its scored half remained under her hand.

She gripped the splintered edge and pulled. Nothing below her ribs helped. Kel braced one foot against the cavity wall and dragged the wood free. The salt groove Tavra had cut to receive the mountain load remained visible, blackened at the center and open at both ends.

Above them, Serad found his feet.

His bruised shoulder hung low, yet his voice crossed the Still Court with the old precision. “Outer companies, return to marks. Drummers, master interval. Surveyors, close the foundation seam.”

One College drum answered. Then another.

Ossa stood on the western cradle with no instrument attached to her body. She called to the public sections, but the Court broke her words into echoes. The seven provincial anchors remained engaged. Every unfinished line fed torque through the ground, searching for the center sequence Eda had destroyed.

Eda still lay within the central field. She had freed both feet from the foundation pull, not from the measure itself. Her burned soles bled through split leather. She turned her right ear toward each speaker because the left gave her only pressure.

The mountain shelf shifted overhead.

Its pause had bought minutes, not safety. Counter-motion still held in Tavra’s pelvis and lower spine. She could no longer receive more. If the eighth channel rejoined under Serad’s count, the load would return through Eda or break the administrative shelf down into the residential branches.

“Salt,” Tavra said.

Kel emptied the last jar beside her. “Where?”

“Everywhere the seam can breathe.”

“That is not a cutting instruction.”

“It is the beginning of one.”

Tavra laid the broken board along the foundation crack. The receiving curve crossed copper, stone, and old Court mortar. It had been designed for one body. She turned it outward. From the cavity, she could score an open line under the west gallery and into the public approach.

Her first cut reached only an arm’s length.

Kel crawled ahead and copied the curve into the salt. A Hadrun cutter took it from him, preserving the open heel shape without deepening it into a command. The line passed beyond Tavra’s sight.

“Do not close the ring,” she called.

The cutter answered, “No ring.”

Serad heard. “You will release seven provincial holds into an unprepared crowd.”

“The crowd prepared while you started early.”

“They prepared one step. They cannot receive a foundation.”

“They will not. We are opening the foundation to the Deep Step.”

“The Court shelf will move.”

“Yes.”

“It carries hospitals, granaries, homes.”

“Not all of it.” Tavra pressed her palm to the eighth channel. The movement she had felt before remained legible despite the injury. Residential ground branched from the deep shelf in seven shallow joins. Generations of market releases and wall adjustments had kept those branches flexible. The administrative hill alone had been pinned to the Court’s foundation.

Serad had called the whole capital one body because one authority governed it. The ground carried a different arrangement.

“Ruhan,” Tavra called.

He slid under the gallery rail with his atlas strapped to his back. Dust coated his hair. “I was beginning to suspect you had invented a route without me.”

“Read the city joins.”

“My wrist reads poorly.”

“Use your knees.”

He knelt along the seam and leaned into the changing floor. Tavra described the seven shallow branches. Ruhan marked them on a blank atlas page, then sent pass-runners to the gallery exits.

“Residential shelves still follow market lanes,” he said when reports returned. “North branch flexes at the dye quarter. South branch follows the river stair. The administrative road crosses all of them at held joints.”

“Can people leave the hill?”

“Three routes now. Perhaps two when the mountain moves again.”

“Open them. No promise they stay.”

Ruhan sent one runner toward the laundry lane, another to the river stair, and took the archive passage himself. He returned carrying a jail key ring and followed by six clerks who had discovered prisoners still locked beneath the tax court. Miren’s withdrawal had emptied guard posts before anyone opened the cells.

“Custody records say nine,” one clerk said.

“Then find nine bodies,” Tavra replied.

The route runners reached the lower cells while cutters broke the held joint beside the stair. Seven prisoners climbed out. An eighth had been moved to a College room. The ninth name belonged to a woman released months earlier whose discharge had never entered the civil book.

“Do not leave the empty number unmarked,” Tavra said. “A false prisoner can hide a real one later.”

The clerk crossed the ninth line once, dated the correction, and kept the old entry readable. Ruhan directed the group down the laundry lane. Nobody required them to carry archive boxes as the price of rescue.

A pension dormitory stood closer to the Court. Several retired centers could not descend the moving stairs. Apprentices used salt boards as sleds, lowering each person along the open curve Tavra had started. The work slowed evacuation, but no one called their bodies an acceptable remainder.

Ruhan left with the pass-runners. He did not wait for Serad’s permission to redraw the capital.

Tavra cut the board again. Her hand shook. She had always used the hips to steady a long incision. Without them, the knife wandered. Kel put his fingers beside hers, not over them.

“Tell me the pressure,” he said.

“Light through the first curve. Deeper at the salt break. Stop before copper.”

Together they extended the line.

At center, Eda pushed herself upright on one knee. She could not stand on the burned soles. Anet and Pellis moved toward her, but she waved them to the outer companies where releases remained incomplete.

Serad gathered the loose folios.

The torn remains of Folio Seven clung to Eda’s bloody left shoe. Its hidden center pages could no longer be read, but the cover and public sequence remained attached to the other volumes by red binding cords.

Serad reached for them.

Eda planted her salt knife through the cords.

The binding snapped. Folio Seven separated from the Master Score. She tore its cover away from the surviving public pages and threw those pages toward the civil gallery, where Miren’s clerks gathered them as evidence. The empty binding went into the open foundation crack.

“You preserve your accusations and destroy the craft that could finish safely,” Serad said.

“I preserve what people must judge,” Eda replied. “I destroy the part that claims one author must close every exit.”

He stepped toward the crack. Eda held the knife between them.

Serad stopped. His shoulder prevented a fight, but his gaze went past her to the seventh cradle. “The mountain will resume. Your mother cannot receive it twice.”

“She will not.”

“Then someone centers.”

Eda looked around the Court. Released apprentices tended dancers beside broken salt fields. Public sections waited outside, each with its own count. Ossa stood empty-handed. Tavra lay beneath the gallery, unable to move either leg.

“No one body centers this,” Eda said.

Serad laughed without humor. “A sentence does not alter counter-motion.”

“No. Feet do.”

She turned her back on him and crawled toward the foundation seam.

Tavra saw the pain in every movement. “Stay at center.”

“I left your part to you. Leave mine to me.”

“You cannot stand.”

“Neither can you.”

Eda reached the dark line. She used both hands to cut a branching release through the central salt, joining Tavra’s ground-level curve to the seven provincial fields. The cut did not invent a new score. It combined the Nareth release, Kel’s open heel, and the gaps each local section had practiced.

Every branch ended before the public approach. No command line crossed into a participant’s sole.

Ossa descended from the cradle. “They cannot hear Eda from the outer streets.”

“You have a drum,” Serad said.

Ossa looked at the split inner skin on the salt. “I had a command.”

“You know how to carry a count across distance.”

“I know how to give one away.”

She retrieved the bare rim from the Veyr miller, struck one call for attention, then passed it back. The miller sounded his local interval. A Kestan runner raised a shadow cloth. Dorel cutters began their slow market song. Ossa moved between them speaking the same disclosure each time.

“The Court line is open. The mountain load remains. One step may return movement to the Deep Step. You may still leave. Your section stops for a refusal or a fallen body. Do not take a second step.”

Her voice reached only the nearest groups. Section keepers repeated it in their own words. Some asked questions again. Nobody shortened the answers because the shelf moved overhead.

Serad climbed the lectern with one usable arm. “If the crowd steps out of sequence, provincial roads shear.”

Tavra pressed her fingers into the seven channels. “They already carry local torque. Your sequence forces it into one answer. The open lines let each field return what it can.”

“And what it cannot?”

“Stays for trained cutters to release.”

“Years of unstable roads.”

“Years of work.”

“Famine does not wait for craft debate.”

“Then grain routes receive terms first. Not permanent chains.”

The argument contained no proof. The proof waited in bare feet outside.

Ruhan returned from the lower hill. “Administrative residents are leaving by the laundry lane and river stair. Still Court staff remain at east gate. The northern route is closing under the mountain shelf.”

“How many homes stay on the deep shelf?” Tavra asked.

“Twenty-three along the judges’ court. People are clearing them now. Hospitals lie on the south residential branch. Granaries split, two civil below and one College store above.”

“Keep the south join open.”

“We need cutters at three held crossings.”

Kel sent the Hadrun workers. Ruhan drew temporary paths in chalk, dating each for the next hour only.

Miren appeared on the civil gallery with her clerks. “The administrative archive?”

“Moves with the hill unless you carry it out,” Ruhan said.

“Records of tax, custody, and land seizure fill twelve rooms.”

“Choose what people need for judgment. The hill will not wait for filing order.”

Miren sent clerks for prisoner rolls and property seizures first. Route maps stayed. She had spent a career treating them as the capital’s memory. Now she chose records of whom the maps had harmed.

The mountain shelf dropped another handspan.

The foundation crack widened. Tavra felt its movement only through her ribs and palms. Her legs lay at an angle that should have hurt. Their silence frightened her more than pain.

Kel saw her looking. “Your right foot is turned under.”

“Straighten it so it does not break farther.”

He used both hands. Tavra watched the splint rotate. Nothing reached her.

“After,” he said, “we find a board with wheels.”

“After, you finish this cut.”

“I can do both.”

The answer carried no promise of cure. She let it stand.

Ossa’s spoken disclosure reached the far sections. White cloths rose and fell as local listeners checked exits. One group near the river stair withdrew because their approach had narrowed. Ruhan redirected them away from the field. Other trained people who had completed disclosure waited outside the salt, but Ossa did not call replacements by duty. The registrars recounted live participants.

The final number returned unchanged. Those who had withdrawn from one section joined no other. People shifted positions only within the waiting muster, opening wider gaps while keeping the disclosed count.

Seven thousand remained ready to take one step.

Tavra looked at Eda. “Can the provincial channels open together without closing into center?”

Eda turned her right ear toward the question. “Not together. Close enough that no one field takes the others’ return.”

“Ossa?”

“I can start windows,” Ossa said. “Local keepers choose inside them.”

Serad shook his head. “A window broad enough for choice permits destructive phase.”

“No College terms,” Tavra said.

He corrected himself with visible effort. “The roads may pull against one another.”

“They may.”

“You accept that?”

“I disclose it.”

Serad looked toward the city below. People still crossed from the administrative hill. He could see Oris in every moving roof, Tavra knew. A winter town under a cliff. Six hundred bodies. A stair that remained while his family did not.

“You think motion becomes moral because many choose it,” he said.

“No. It becomes answerable to more than you.”

“If the north road tears and grain fails, who carries blame?”

“Everyone who chose the terms carries part. Cutters carry their work. Councils carry allocation. I carry what I taught. You carry what you hid.”

“That is no responsibility. It disperses until nobody can be judged.”

“Then keep records. Hold inquiry. Do not hold land.”

The Court floor lurched. Serad caught the lectern with his good hand.

The old master interval sounded from two College drums. He had signaled them while arguing. Trained companies stiffened. Eda’s shoulders turned toward center by reflex. Ossa’s empty chest answered the cadence even without straps.

Serad raised his hand for the full count.

Tavra could end him.

The foundation crack ran under the lectern stair. One deep cut through the supporting salt would drop that stair into the moving cavity. His shoulder could not catch him. The falling stone would break the master count before it began.

She set her knife against the line.

Serad saw. Neither of them looked away.

At Nareth, Tavra had obeyed him and buried a district. In Dorel, she had hidden from every consequence except the stone in her knees. Killing him now could feel like judgment while removing his testimony, his records, and his chance to answer before people he had used.

She turned the knife sideways and cut around the stair.

The salt released on both sides. The stair rose intact on a small island of Court stone. Serad lost his stance and sat hard on the upper step, but the ground beneath him held.

“You do not get a clean death in my score,” Tavra said.

He looked down at the preserved stair. Understanding reached him with horror and anger. “You would leave me to watch roads fail.”

“I leave you alive to hear what they call failure.”

The College drummers waited for his hand.

Eda pulled the remaining command cords out of the salt and cut them into short lengths. Anet took one piece, Pellis another, and each company leader carried a fragment away from center. No drum received the full written interval.

Ossa climbed the west cradle again.

“Attention only,” she called.

The bare rim sounded once in Veyr hands.

Across the public field, local keepers raised their signs. The north millers began a breath count. River sections spoke paired words. Kestan runners watched the moving shadow. Dorel voices held their market song at the note before weight.

Ossa did not complete any of them.

“First window open,” she said.

The north sections chose.

Bare heels touched scored wrappings. Not one mass impact, but thousands of small contacts moved down the avenue. Some landed early. Others waited for a neighbor to regain balance. A white cloth rose near the eastern wall, and that local count stopped until a participant stepped out of the path.

The first public pressure reached Tavra’s salt line.

It came softly enough to mistake for settling dust. Then the foundation channel warmed under her palm. Movement that Serad had forced toward center found the open branch and passed beneath the west gallery.

“Second window,” Ossa called.

River voices chose their words. Feet crossed one simple curve and withdrew. The south residential join shifted. Ruhan shouted that the hospital road remained connected.

Eda opened the sixth provincial branch with her knife. Spring pressure rushed into wet salt. Somewhere beyond the capital, a stopped watershed moved. Water did not appear by magic in the Court. Only the empty drag under Eda’s sole eased.

The third window opened.

Kestan sections followed shadow rather than voice. Their steps landed across a long interval. The anchor orchards’ provincial channel released without fixing where those orchards should go next.

Serad called, “Stop. The western road is pulling north.”

Tavra felt it. One channel scraped another, building torque at the Court’s southern edge.

“Hold third branch where it is,” she told Eda. “Ruhan, route check.”

Ruhan’s messengers ran. A grain road outside the city had shifted toward old pasture, but no buildings stood on the seam. Its carts would need a new crossing.

“Disclose the change,” Tavra said.

Ossa passed the report to section keepers. People heard that a road had already moved and could still leave before their own step. The public count paused without collapsing.

A woman in the Dorel section removed her wrapping. Her neighbors opened an exit. She walked out of the salt. A trained cutter who had already registered and practiced stood waiting, not summoned by rank. The registrar confirmed her disclosure. She entered the open place by her own request.

The live count returned to seven thousand.

“Fourth window,” Ossa said.

Dorel’s market song crossed the held note. Their simple steps carried the rhythm of rebuilding stalls around a stone that moved every nine days. The foundation line widened.

Tavra’s board shifted under her shoulders. Kel and the Hadrun cutter held it. She could feel the Deep Step now, not as a voice or intention, only the vast slow movement the Court had suppressed. It pressed upward along every open salt branch.

Serad felt it too. “The foundation is leaving its seat.”

“Yes,” Eda said.

“You cannot control where it goes.”

“No.”

Fear crossed her face. She did not hide it from the companies.

The fifth window opened.

More sections took their one step. Open heel, chosen weight, withdrawal. Thousands of tiny adjustments distributed what no center could survive. Some participants fell and received hands from neighbors. Local counts broke and resumed. Nobody took a second step to correct the first.

The administrative hill moved west.

At first the change measured less than a paving seam. Then the Still Court galleries leaned away from the civil market below. Held joints along the residential branches cracked in sequence.

“Hospitals?” Tavra called.

“South branch flexing,” Ruhan answered. “Still joined.”

“Dye quarter?”

“North branch opened at the market lane. Residential shelf stays with lower city.”

“Judges’ homes?”

“Cleared. They move with us.”

Us. Ruhan stood on the administrative shelf now. Everyone inside the Still Court did.

The sixth window reached the outer avenues.

Ossa’s voice had gone hoarse. She gave the opening to local keepers and stopped speaking. The count traveled without her. Every section chose its own moment inside the broad interval.

The ground answered in overlapping turns.

The mountain lifted slightly from its kneeling pause. Not upward to its former place, only away from the administrative roofs as the deep shelf slid out from under its line of descent. Forest soil spilled across the northern gallery. An old stair touched Court stone and broke free again.

Eda opened the seventh provincial channel.

Counter-motion left her burned soles in a rush. She collapsed onto her hands. Anet caught her shoulder without pulling her back to center.

The last public window remained.

Serad sat on the isolated stair. He could still signal the College drums. Instead he watched the widening gap between administrative hill and residential city. Miren’s clerks carried prisoner rolls over the final temporary bridge. Civil residents ran behind them.

“Bridge clear,” Ruhan called.

Miren crossed last. She left the imperial route maps in the archive.

Ossa looked toward every section keeper she could see. None looked back to her for a command. Signs rose locally.

The final participants stepped.

Seven thousand people had each taken one disclosed step. No person took the next one for them.

The Deep Step returned beneath the Still Court.

The foundation anchor rolled out of its copper cradle. The remaining master cords slipped into the crack. Tavra’s open salt line broke into separate curves as the shelf moved, preventing any single cut from becoming a permanent new score.

The administrative hill traveled west on its own deep shelf. Court, College offices, route archive, tax chambers, and the upper granary moved with it. Residential districts remained on connected branches around markets, wells, hospitals, and homes. The gap widened to a stone channel crossed by no held road.

The mountain settled beside the departing shelf. Its lower forest stood at a new angle. No building lay beneath it.

Provincial pressure released from the seven cradles. Some roads turned. Some hills lowered. Springs began seeking paths no Court map named. The ground did not become safe. It became moving country again.

The Still Court stopped a mile west of Meren’s residential edge.

No scored road connected it to the city.

Silence followed, uneven and human. People cried, argued, called names, and asked which routes remained. College dancers sat where their fields had broken. Public participants removed wrappings and examined bruised feet. Cutters marked every injury they could find.

The first lists stayed separate. Court apprentices recorded company wounds by field. Civil readers took names from public sections without converting them into military rolls. Sole courts collected failed wrappings beside successful ones. A physician marked Eda’s hearing loss without guessing whether it would improve. Another examined Tavra and wrote absent movement below the pelvis, lower-spine counter-motion, immediate accommodation required.

“No heroic language,” Tavra told the clerk.

The clerk scratched out sacrifice and wrote injury.

Near the seventh cradle, people found the split command skin face down in blood and salt. Ossa asked witnesses to lift it together so no one could claim she had altered the cuts after the fact. The erased orders remained visible along the tear.

Kel leaned over Tavra. “Can you breathe?”

“Yes.”

“Can you feel this?” He touched her left thigh.

“No.”

He moved lower. “This?”

“No.”

Eda crawled to them. Blood had dried at both soles. She positioned herself on Tavra’s right so her good ear faced the conversation.

“You waited,” she said.

“Yes.”

“You asked.”

“Yes.”

Neither word repaired what preceded it. Tavra would not use the climax to demand forgiveness.

“Your feet?” she asked.

“Burned. Left hearing damaged. Hip still mine, though it objects.”

“My legs are not answering.”

“I know.”

Serad descended his preserved stair. His shoulder had swollen under the coat. Two former apprentices moved between him and the remaining folios.

“I will not run,” he said.

“There is nowhere scored to run,” Ruhan replied.

Miren posted civil witnesses beside the folios. The surviving pages, the split command skin, Eda’s bloody shoes, Tavra’s copied page, and the broken foundation cradle remained where everyone could see them. No one burned the archive.

Ossa collected the cut command straps and placed them with the evidence. She kept the bare rim only after the Veyr miller handed it back.

Outside the separated hill, citizens began calling temporary crossings across the new gap. Ruhan refused to draw one until both sides named a term.

Tavra lay on her broken cutting board while the stone beneath the Court moved in a slow westward drift. She could not feel that motion through her legs. Her ribs felt it. Her palms felt it. Around her, people measured injuries and routes instead of victory.

Eda sat beside her, turned one listening ear toward the open ground, and began helping Kel record the feet that had paid.

Chapter 27 — The Roads After Empire

The first medicine cart reached the new gap and found no bridge.

Ruhan stood on Meren’s residential side with his incomplete atlas open under one elbow. Across the stone channel, the separated administrative hill drifted west by a few yards each day. The Still Court rose on its edge, galleries broken but occupied. Former College dancers, civil clerks, prisoners who had chosen to remain for testimony, and several hundred residents waited there with one upper granary and no hospital.

The cart driver pointed at the gap. “My order says Court infirmary.”

“Your order names a road that moved.”

“The medicines spoil by tomorrow.”

“Then we need terms today.”

A College quartermaster waited opposite with a white cloth. Ruhan called the cart inventory across the channel. Fever bark. Burn salve. Clean wrapping. Two boxes of mineral poultice for lithified joints. The quartermaster offered archive paper and upper-granary flour in exchange.

A month earlier, a road office would have named the crossing, toll, schedule, and armed escort. Now six groups claimed standing: medicine workers, lower-city millers, hill residents, former College staff, injured public participants, and the neighborhood whose courtyard would hold the temporary approach.

They argued for three hours.

The courtyard tenants refused overnight traffic. The driver refused to unload without shelter. Hill residents wanted flour excluded from exchange because they considered the upper grain public property. Lower millers would not accept archive paper until civil witnesses confirmed it carried no custody records.

Ruhan wrote every refusal beside the proposed line.

His right wrist no longer needed a splint, but it remained too stiff for long writing. A Dorel clerk took dictation when the hand failed. Ruhan did not let her clean the page. Crossed terms showed what people had rejected.

At noon, both sides agreed to a rope-guided stone walkway for six hours. Two local cutters prepared step-salt around loose slabs. No center held them. Workers shifted stones by hand when possible and used one shallow ground-dance turn where the gap narrowed. A retired College center received that small counter-motion with full disclosure and released before pain deepened.

The medicine cart stayed behind. Packages crossed on backs.

When the final burn salve reached the hill, both sides erased the salt approach. The deep shelf continued west. No official pretended the six-hour crossing had become a road by habit.

The crossing closed before two hill children returned from visiting relatives below. Their mother demanded an extension. Courtyard tenants refused because night traffic would pass beside sleeping rooms. The argument nearly reopened the salt by force until a Kestan runner found a lower shelf moving parallel to the hill for part of the night.

It could carry people, not carts. The mother crossed with a lantern and met the children at its eastern end. Ruhan walked the shelf first, marked two crumbling edges, and erased his chalk after all three returned. By morning that route had moved beneath a retaining wall.

The incident entered the emergency record. So did the mother’s anger, the tenants’ refusal, and the fact that temporary terms separated a family for four hours. Revocable roads did not eliminate harm. They made the decisions and repairs visible enough to contest.

On the hill, medicines went first to burned center-dancers and public participants with infected cuts. Serad received a shoulder wrap after people in immediate danger, not as punishment and not as privilege. Tavra’s mineral poultice remained below because it could not repair spinal counter-motion. Her care team requested pressure cushions, clean cloth, and a board narrow enough for residential doors instead.

Ruhan dated the crossing in the atlas and drew the line faintly.

Other emergencies allowed less argument. A Hadrun grain road turned into pasture and stranded three valley towns. Kesta’s anchor orchards moved north, opening one pass and closing two. Dorel’s market circuit shortened by half a day. Springs returned unevenly in the sixth watershed, flooding an abandoned imperial tollhouse while leaving two wells dry.

Route teams traveled without master maps. They carried dated pages, local anchor stones, simple release soles, and lists of who needed food first. Each crossing required someone to say what movement might cost.

Mistakes remained costly.

At a southern ridge, cutters opened a medicine path and blocked shepherds from winter grazing. The first agreement had excluded them because nobody at the table knew the flock had shifted. Shepherds dismantled the salt markers. The town called it sabotage. Ruhan called another meeting and made both words enter the record.

At the river stair, a temporary grain turn collapsed two market awnings. No one died, but three sellers lost a week’s goods. The route accord had named bodily injury and ignored stall loss. Compensation took longer than rebuilding the approach.

People missed the old roads most when hungry.

They also remembered who had paid to keep those roads still.

The public inquiry began on the twelfth day after the separation.

No hall could hold everyone, and the Still Court lacked a fixed crossing. Testimony took place on both sides of the gap. Witnesses spoke from Meren’s lower gallery. Serad and College leaders sat on their preserved stair across the channel. Civil scribes made duplicate records and exchanged them at each day’s temporary walkway.

Ruhan attended because his atlas contained the moving wall route, provincial refusals, and the evacuation terms. He preferred passes to courts. A pass admitted when weather overruled intention. Courts often treated procedure as proof that intention had prevailed.

Miren opened by naming her own signatures.

“I authorized supply, compulsory attendance, and seizure of civic anchors,” she said. “The folio presented to me concealed permanent ecological projections. I withdrew civil authority after learning that fact. Withdrawal did not erase the earlier orders or the harm they enabled.”

Serad listened with his bruised shoulder bound to his chest.

When his turn came, he did not claim possession or madness. He described Oris. He described grain routes that had prevented famine, walls held long enough for children to leave moving houses, and provinces whose officials threatened war whenever a pass changed jurisdiction.

“I believed a permanent arrangement would prevent deaths that local negotiation repeatedly failed to prevent,” he said. “I withheld projections from civil review because I believed fear of finite loss would block continental safety.”

A Nareth survivor stood on the lower side. “You called our district finite?”

“Yes.” Serad’s voice broke, then steadied. “That word permitted me to continue.”

“Do you still believe the Measure should have finished?”

He looked beyond the inquiry to the roads shifting around Meren. “I believe people will die because it did not.”

Murmurs rose.

The presiding witnesses waited rather than striking for silence.

Serad continued. “I also know the center pages concealed deaths and land loss from people whose consent I considered an obstacle. Both statements remain true.”

His coherence angered some listeners more than a plea would have. It also left his choices available for judgment. No pardon entered the proceeding. No execution shortened it. He and the College command circle remained under witnessed custody on the separated hill while charges were drafted for coerced service, concealed casualty projections, unlawful anchor seizure, and deaths from the early holds.

The inquiry questioned the cutters who altered Eda’s practice shoes. One had followed Serad’s direct sketch. Another had recognized that the crosscut trapped release and said nothing because emergency corrections fell outside ordinary review. Both insisted the foundation line protected provinces from unfinished torque.

Anet asked them why nobody told the person wearing the shoes.

The senior cutter answered, “A center who anticipates restraint may resist it at the wrong beat.”

“So you needed her ignorance to keep her safe?”

“We needed completion.”

The distinction entered the record.

Road-company pension officials testified next. They had tied food and housing to continued center service long after injured dancers requested release. The officials produced budgets showing that without imperial route tolls the pension system would have failed. Retired centers answered that a system funded by further injuries had already failed them.

Miren’s former deputies tried to divide civil and College guilt at the charter boundary. Witnesses traced salt seizures signed by civil offices and command orders issued by the College. Some acts carried both seals. The public inquiry kept both names rather than allowing one institution to become the other’s excuse.

Ossa testified beside the split command skin.

She named every conditioned cadence cut she recognized. Her left forearm tired before noon, so another witness held the skin while she pointed. She did not let the inquiry use her desertion as proof that Hadrun belonged to one guilty officer.

“The order traveled through my hands,” she said. “The institution trained those hands, rewarded accuracy, and wrote Pel and the civilians as route loss. Judge my action. Also judge the structure that made one drummer convenient to blame.”

Eda presented the surviving Folio Seven pages.

She wore soft open sandals over bandaged soles. She turned her right ear toward questions and asked speakers on her left to repeat themselves. The first clerk described that as temporary impairment. Eda stopped testimony until he removed temporary.

“You do not know that,” she said.

He wrote partial left-ear hearing loss, duration unknown.

She explained the hidden foundation anchor, Serad’s altered shoes, her chosen entry, and the destroyed center sequence. Serad challenged one technical detail about the sixth channel. Eda checked the page, corrected herself publicly, and continued.

Tavra testified from a low wheeled board built by Kel and two cartwrights.

The board had a back brace, leather hand loops, and wide wheels for rough ground. Two people helped transfer her from bed each morning. She could sit upright for an hour before the pelvis injury forced rest. Her legs carried no reliable sensation and no useful movement.

Ruhan watched the Court turn toward her as though injury made every answer sacred. Tavra noticed too.

“Do not use my body to settle the argument,” she said. “I took a disclosed load after Eda chose the timing. That does not make every decision before it correct.”

She named Nareth without asking acquittal. She described leaving Eda, hiding the score fragment, and repeatedly planning to substitute herself for her daughter. Then she explained why a stable road could save lives and still become a chain.

Serad asked her one question. “If the winter grain road fails, will you take responsibility?”

“For the cuts I teach and the costs I conceal, yes. I will not claim responsibility as a reason to own every foot.”

The inquiry continued past the first month. Its purpose involved public judgment, not swift absolution. Provincial witnesses would take seasons to arrive. Evidence stayed under mixed custody: sole courts, civil clerks, injured dancers, and route communities each held witnessed copies. The originals remained in a dry chamber on the separated hill, reachable only by temporary crossing.

After Tavra’s testimony, Ruhan helped Kel push the wheeled board to a rented room near the lower market. Eda followed with one hand on the wall when her burned feet tired. Inside, she and Kel transferred Tavra to a padded bench. Tavra directed every grip because sudden twisting drove pain above the place where sensation ended.

“You can leave,” Tavra told Eda after Kel went for water.

“I know.”

“I mean the room, not the city.”

“I know that too.” Eda adjusted her chair so Tavra sat on her right. “You keep offering exits after I have chosen to stay.”

“I am practicing.”

“It can become another way of asking me to reassure you.”

Tavra looked toward the shuttered market. “Yes.”

Eda unwrapped one foot. New skin had begun forming around the burns, tender and uneven. The knife wound in her left arch remained open at one edge. “I did not forgive you at the Court.”

“I did not think you had.”

“I loved you there.”

Tavra’s hands tightened on the board loops.

“Those are not the same thing,” Eda said.

“No.”

They sat without converting the distinction into comfort. When Kel returned, Eda helped rewrap her own sole and asked Tavra whether the pressure cushion needed turning. Tavra said yes. Eda asked where to place her hands before moving it.

Between inquiry days, Ruhan traveled.

His incomplete atlas became thicker and less complete. Every province added pages. Kesta contributed orchard routes with no final borders. Hadrun drew seasonal releases and marked where imperial pension houses stood. Veyr recorded its dye-yard crossing with the landlord’s one-day terms attached. Dorel sent a market circuit that changed before the ink dried.

Several delegates proposed a central route council.

Ruhan listened until they began assigning permanent seats.

“Who removes a council that controls every grain crossing?” he asked.

“Member provinces,” said a northern delegate.

“How?”

“By vote.”

“What happens to a walking country with no fixed provincial count?”

The answer took too long.

They abandoned permanent authority and drafted route accords instead.
