Each accord named a purpose, start, end, affected communities, known bodily and material risks, compensation, exit routes, and a date for reconsideration. Any party could call a halt, though emergency food agreements required a short wind-down to prevent carts being stranded midpass.

The first drafts failed.

Millers wanted long terms so grain prices could settle. Cutters wanted short terms because ground response changed. City councils tried to sign for outlying farms. Kestan runners refused signatures tied to borders. Retired centers demanded injury support before anyone authorized a held crossing.

Ruhan kept the failures in the atlas.

By the fourth week, three valleys agreed on a grain approach lasting nine days. The term matched Dorel’s market circuit, short enough for the road to be read again and long enough for carts to complete a round trip. Hadrun cutters would inspect daily. Two local centers volunteered for shallow corrections with a six-beat release. No body could serve center on consecutive days. Pension support came from a toll disclosed at both ends.

The road itself had shifted into an old quarry.

On opening morning, Ruhan stood beside the first cart. The quarry town wanted the line moved ten feet to spare a memorial wall. The valley millers objected that the turn would tip loaded axles. Cutters tested both approaches. A third line around the wall added half a mile but kept the grade.

They amended the accord before laying salt.

Every signatory then read the changed terms aloud. A quarry tenant found that compensation still named only cart damage. She added injury to walls, wells, and work yards. A retired center added a clause requiring public record of pain that appeared after the crossing term expired. The millers objected to indefinite claims, so the parties set three later examination dates rather than pretending counter-motion ended with the road.

No imperial seal closed the page. Each affected group kept a copy, and the salt could not be laid until their versions matched. Ruhan marked one blank line for any community they had failed to identify.

Tavra and Eda arrived in a covered wagon.

Kel rolled Tavra’s board down a ramp. The transfer jolted her spine, and she closed her eyes until the pain passed. Eda moved slowly beside her, sandals wrapped against quarry grit.

“You chose a road opening for your first outing,” Eda said.

“I was promised an argument.”

“You have standards.”

“Falling, but present.”

Eda positioned herself on Tavra’s left, then noticed and moved to the right so conversation reached her good ear. The adjustment happened without comment.

Ossa arrived carrying the bare rim by hand, no straps. She had been asked to open the crossing with a ceremonial beat and had refused. A quarry child struck a work rhythm instead. Ossa joined only after the cart crews began their own song.

The road-company veterans inspected the step-salt. Their old bends remained. No cure followed imperial collapse. The new pension toll would not straighten them. It would pay for braces, food, and work that did not require further centering.

Ruhan opened the atlas to a clean page.

At the top he wrote: Quarry Grain Crossing. Term: nine days.

Below it he listed the memorial wall, axle grade, daily inspection, release rests, toll, and right to halt. He left the route line unfinished until the first cart reached the other side.

The cutters performed one shallow turn. Stone in the quarry floor shifted around the wall. A road wide enough for a grain cart settled along the longer grade.

Nobody cheered yet.

The driver took the reins. At the salt boundary, she asked who guaranteed the crossing.

“No one,” Ruhan said.

She frowned. “Who answers if it fails?”

He pointed to the accord board where names, duties, and compensation stood in public view. “Those people answer for the work they named. The ground may still change.”

The driver considered the road, the memorial wall, and the waiting towns. Then she guided the cart forward.

Wheels crossed the new stone without tipping. At the far end, a Hadrun cutter marked the hour and inspected the first shift under the axle.

Only then did people breathe.

Ruhan completed the faint line in his incomplete atlas. He stopped it at both ends and wrote the date when it must be questioned again, along with the names of those charged with calling that question in public.

Chapter 28 — Where the Ground May Go

One year later, Eda returned to Dorel on a road scheduled to disappear at sunset.

The cart crossed the eastern quarry while morning light reached the scored stones. A board beside the approach listed the term, weather reading, wheel limit, affected wells, and names of three people authorized to halt traffic. Someone had added a fourth name in charcoal after a shepherd challenged the original accord.

At the Dorel end, the road bent around a field that had not existed the previous spring.

Eda stepped down carefully. Scars crossed both soles, thicker under the left arch. Long journeys made them ache. Her left ear still heard voices as if a wall stood between speaker and listener. She turned her right side toward the market and let the familiar disorder reach her.

The leaning stall was gone.

A public square occupied its old place. Market shelters stood on wide runners so sellers could move them when the nine-day circuit shifted. Every stall displayed two prices: goods carried on currently agreed roads and goods carried by paths whose cost remained unsettled. Arguments began before breakfast.

The largest wall bore anniversary terms rather than a monument. Dorel would practice one open route at noon. Participation remained voluntary. Ground readers had inspected the field that morning. The proposed crossing might shorten the western water walk. It might also move two dye tables and alter drainage behind the kiln. The accord lasted one market circuit unless renewed.

Below the terms, affected people had written objections.

The dyers wanted raised tables before the practice. Kiln workers wanted runoff kept away from cooling pits. A family with a handcart wanted the slope reduced. Two children objected because the proposed line crossed their game square.

The market itself carried changes beyond movable shelters. A former toll booth served as an injury kitchen where road-company pensioners ate without proving current service. The sole court occupied three stalls and posted every failed cut beside the successful patterns. Sellers who used a temporary route contributed to repair funds at its ends instead of paying a distant office.

Not every change had improved trade. Salt cost more because several old supply roads now wandered through wet country. Western fruit spoiled while three towns argued over a hill crossing. One cloth merchant had closed his stall after a nine-day route ended and no replacement received consent. His empty shelter remained on the market plan so the loss did not vanish beneath stories of freedom.

People disagreed about whether to renew the anniversary route before practice began. Water carriers wanted the shorter walk. Dyers feared the line would drain their yard. Kiln workers asked for compensation if runoff spoiled a firing. The children offered half their game square for one day, then withdrew the offer when adults treated it as permanent.

A sole-court reader added every change to the board. Nobody reduced the dispute to opponents of motion and defenders of safety. The route would have to answer the uses actually present.

Eda read every line.

“You can smile at the children’s objection,” Kel said behind her. “They drew a replacement square on Tavra’s teaching sheet.”

Eda turned her good ear. “Did she approve?”

“She asked what happened to the old square if the road did not move.”

“And?”

“They are negotiating shared chalk.”

Kel had more gray in his beard and a wider cart. A ramp folded from its side. He opened it while Tavra maneuvered a low wheeled board to the top.

Tavra descended backward using leather loops fixed beside each wheel. Kel kept one hand near the frame without touching. The board carried a padded seat, a narrow score table, knives, salt bags, and replacement cushions. Tavra’s legs rested in shaped supports. Her right foot had healed crooked inside a soft brace; neither leg provided motion.

Halfway down, one wheel caught the ramp lip.

“Stop,” Tavra said.

Kel stopped.

She shifted her weight with both arms, lifted the wheel using the side loop, and rolled over the lip. Sweat stood on her face by the time she reached ground.

Eda waited until Tavra looked up.

“Do you want a push?”

“Across the mud, yes. On stone, no.”

Eda took the rear handle and moved the board through the wet patch. At the square’s edge, she let go.

Tavra inspected her. “Your left sandal wears faster.”

“My left foot disagrees with roads.”

“Roads have survived worse criticism.”

They did not embrace. Tavra disliked being leaned over on the board, and Eda had learned not to make reunion perform intimacy for onlookers. She touched Tavra’s forearm. Tavra turned the touch into a brief handclasp.

The anniversary workshop began beside the well.

Thirty people had registered. Tavra refused the raised platform built for her and taught at ground level where everyone could see the relation between board, hand, and salt. Dorel carpenters laid a hard strip under her wheels so she could move along the practice line.

She placed a copied open-heel pattern on the score table.

“Who owns this?” she asked.

Several students looked at Eda.

“The sole court keeps copies,” one said.

“That answers custody.” Tavra waited. “Who owns the cut?”

“Aren Dast created the first version,” said a retired road dancer.

“He documented a gap used in older greetings. I altered it at Nareth. Kel found a safer shallow depth. Eda joined it to public fields. People in seven provinces changed its timing. Which one receives ownership?”

Nobody volunteered an answer.

A city clerk raised his hand. “Without a named authority, dangerous versions will spread.”

“They already have,” Tavra said. “Naming me owner would not recall them. It would let me license one version and pretend the others disappeared.”

“What prevents misuse?”

“Records. Peer reading. Public disclosure. Injury support. Refusal. Inquiry when those fail.”

“That does not prevent everything.”

“No.”

The clerk seemed offended by the admission.

A woman from the western water crews asked who could call herself a cutter. Tavra turned the question back to the group. Answers included apprenticeship, sole-court review, surviving a road company, completing a certain number of routes, and acceptance by affected communities.

“Each test excludes someone,” Tavra said. “Some exclusions protect bodies. Others protect employment or reputation. Name which you are doing.”

The retired dancer proposed that no person certify alone. A dyer objected that three-person panels would favor cities with enough trained readers. Kel suggested traveling review tables. The children asked whether a bad cutter could stop being one. That question produced the longest silence.

They drafted no universal rank. For the afternoon practice, they agreed on narrower duties. One person would cut, two would read, affected workers could halt, and an injury recorder would remain independent of the route crew. Any uncertainty about the ground stayed written beside the score.

Tavra demonstrated how she now cut from the board. Without hips to stabilize the blade, she used a leather wrist loop and asked another reader to hold the sole against her table. The method allowed precision but tired her shoulders quickly. She named that limit before beginning.

“I teach differently because my body changed,” she said. “Do not turn adaptation into proof that injury gave me wisdom. Other people built this board, tested these loops, and corrected the first angle I cut from it.”

Kel held up two fingers. “Three angles.”

“Your record is hostile.”

“Publicly so.”

Laughter loosened the group without erasing the lesson.

Tavra gave each group a practice sole with one deliberate error. One closed the heel. Another carried a crosscut that would trap release. A third hid its receiving load beneath decorative marks. Students had to find the error, explain the likely body cost, and state what they could not know without reading actual ground.

Eda worked with the children from the game square.

They found the closed heel because chalk dust gathered in it. The younger child wanted to carve a bird beside the gap. Eda asked whether the bird changed pressure. They tested it on damp clay. One wing deepened the outer edge.

“Then the bird cannot go there,” the child said.

“It could be shallower.”

“Would people think it means something?”

“Probably.”

They removed the bird.

At another table, the retired dancer refused to cut because her hands shook. Tavra asked her to read while a younger carpenter held the knife. The dancer called depth and direction. The carpenter stopped before each turn and repeated the instruction. Neither person became the invisible helper of the other.

By midday, Tavra’s lower-spine pain forced her flat.

Kel unfolded the board back and adjusted a cushion under her hips. Teaching continued from that position. Nobody called the change defeat. Nobody called it courage. A student held each sole where Tavra could inspect it without twisting.

Eda watched the old instincts cross her mother’s face. Tavra still wanted to take the knife when a beginner cut badly. She now named the impulse.

“I want to correct this with my hand,” she told the group. “If I do, you learn my movement and not the error. Start again. Tell me why this line closes.”

The student found the answer.

At the end, the city clerk tried once more. “Will you authorize the public route score?”

“No.”

“Will you certify the cutters?”

“No.”

“Then what have you done here?”

“Taught what I know. Disclosed where I failed. Read their work.”

The clerk looked toward Eda. “Will you establish a traveling school, at least? Communities need a standard.”

Eda had received that proposal in six provinces. Each offered rooms, staff, and authority to issue sole marks. Every offer began with safety and ended with one office deciding whose practice counted.

“I travel when communities invite me,” she said. “I leave copies and local readers. I do not keep a gate.”

“How do we know whom to trust?”

“Ask who records refusal. Ask who pays for injury. Ask what they hide when the cut succeeds.”

The answer dissatisfied him. Eda preferred that to obedience.

Her year of travel had produced no single method. In Kesta, orchard workers used shadow intervals and refused written borders. Along the sixth watershed, well keepers demanded that every score name downstream villages before opening springs. Hadrun pensioners taught apprentices to recognize pain before technique failed. Veyr weavers tied temporary route terms to market days because landlords had once treated access as a private favor.

Eda carried practices between them only with permission. Sometimes a community rejected an imported cut. Once, a spring council erased her entire lesson after local readers found that the receiving curve would drain into burial ground. She stayed to document the error and left without a credential to defend.

Communities also changed what she taught elsewhere. A Kestan refusal sign became a river-section cloth. A Dorel injury form gained space for lost stall goods after Meren’s collapsed awnings. Tavra’s board loops appeared in three pension houses, built to different widths because bodies and doors differed.

None of that required a school above the communities. It required travel, copying, argument, and the willingness to leave a useful blank when knowledge did not belong to the traveler.

After the workshop, she and Tavra ate beside the kiln. Tavra chose a place where her board could turn without help. Eda sat on her right.

For several minutes they discussed the western water walk. The dye tables could move uphill, but runoff might reach the kiln pits. Tavra proposed a shorter practice line. Eda thought it would place too much load near the children’s replacement square.

“You still protect the open space first,” Tavra said.

“You still think every open space wants a score.”

“Not every one.”

“Most.”

Tavra considered. “Too many.”

The admission carried no plea.

Eda broke bread. “I leave for Kesta next week.”

“How long?”

“Two months. Orchard routes, then the sixth watershed if their sole court still wants an outside reader.”

“Will you come back?”

“Yes.”

“Because you want to?”

“Because I want to.”

Tavra looked down at her hands. “I still hear your question in the kiln. Whether you had to disappear for me to tell the truth.”

“You answered it.”

“Not enough.”

“There is no enough that changes the years.”

“I know.”

Eda’s left ear caught the market as soft impact without words. Through the right she heard knives being packed away and children arguing over chalk. “You left me. You came back after I made a life that did not include you. You tried to make your body pay instead of asking what I wanted.”

“Yes.”

“I love you.”

Tavra closed her eyes.

“I am not saying it to free you,” Eda continued. “I am saying it because making you guess has become another kind of work I do not want.”

“I love you too.” Tavra opened her eyes. “I am sorry.”

“I heard you.”

Eda did not say the apology had repaired the abandonment. Tavra did not ask.

A pressure spasm tightened across Tavra’s lower back. She gripped the board loops and waited through it. Eda stayed where Tavra could see her.

“Cushion?” she asked.

“After it passes.”

When Tavra’s hands loosened, Eda asked again. Tavra said yes and directed the adjustment.

Ossa began drumming in the market square.

She used a shallow hand drum with no command skin and no body straps. Her left forearm still weakened after long phrases, so three market musicians shared the rhythm. The dance had no center. Couples, children, retired road workers, and visiting cutters joined when they wanted and left without breaking anything.

Ossa saw Eda at the kiln and changed nothing in the beat.

That pleased Eda.

Ruhan arrived late with the atlas under his good arm. His right wrist had stiffened in cold rain, and a young route clerk carried the heavier folios. He opened the newest volume on the public board.

Maps filled some pages. Others held lists of temporary terms, failed crossings, injuries, and names of communities discovered after agreements were signed. Between them, deliberate blanks remained.

A Dorel merchant tapped one. “You know the western shelf exists.”

“Yes.”

“Why not draw it?”

“Three groups dispute its use. One asked me not to publish the spring approach until they can protect the wells.”

“So travelers go without knowledge.”

“They receive the risk and the local contacts. They do not receive a line that may become a claim.”

The merchant muttered about inconvenient principles.

Ruhan handed him a dated pass around the shelf.

At noon, the route-practice assembly formed. Dyers had raised their tables. Kiln workers dug a runoff trench. The handcart family tested the revised slope. Children marked a new game square with shared chalk. Every affected group read the amended terms aloud.

No one asked Eda to stand at center.

She took a place beside the western water carriers. Tavra waited at the score edge on her wheeled board, serving as a reader only. Kel stood near the open heel. Ossa stopped drumming social dance and set the instrument down before approaching the route field.

Ruhan marked the practice route in his atlas with an ending date.

Local keepers gave attention calls. Participants entered in small sections. The movement used shallow turns and one-step greetings already taught. When a dye table shifted half an inch, its owner raised a red cloth. That section stopped. Cutters adjusted the runoff gap, disclosed the change, and asked again.

The owner lowered the cloth.

Practice resumed.

Stone beneath the western edge loosened. The proposed line settled around the kiln rather than through it. Near the children’s square, the ground turned north. Their replacement chalk stayed untouched.

The route reached the water walk.

Before opening it, the assembly tested more than feet and carts. Kel pushed Tavra’s wheeled board up the grade while she kept both hands away from the loops. One turn proved too narrow; the outer wheel slipped toward runoff stone. Workers widened that curve by hand. A woman with a walking brace tested the next section and found a ridge that caught her toe. Children marked it until cutters lowered the lip.

Tavra refused to let her board become the only measure of access. Water yokes, handcarts, pension braces, and market runners each found different faults. Every correction entered the day’s record with the person who had named it.

Nobody named it permanent. The accord board gave it nine days, daily inspection, a load limit, and the right of any affected group to halt use. Sellers rolled two market shelters toward the new edge. Water carriers tried the grade with empty yokes, then with full buckets.

Eda took the first public crossing only after the people who needed it had tested the surface.

Her scarred soles recognized shallow movement. The ground shifted under one step, then held long enough for the next. She did not ask it for permission or hear a message in stone. Cutters, dyers, children, water carriers, and the changing shelf had shaped a usable crossing together.

At the far end, she turned back.

Tavra waited on the low board beside the kiln. Ossa had resumed the market dance. Ruhan left the new line open at its western edge, where the atlas page met a deliberate blank.

A water carrier passed Eda with two full buckets. Behind him came a handcart, three children, and a dyer moving one table before the afternoon rain.

Eda stepped aside and let them use the road.
